i would like to add my support to the words of mr deva and mr tajani. i have sent you a letter to that effect, asking you to make a virtue of the need and to try to work more efficiently in the face of the disaster. we ended the plenary session here in strasbourg in 2004 and we are now starting a plenary session without any interim meetings. i think that this is not efficient, that it is a missed opportunity and that we should look at the efficiency of this house and of the plenary sessions. we could have easily compressed a piece. i have not yet received your reply, but i assume that, given the agenda, the answer is no, perhaps because you fear that a precedent will be set. i think, however, that you should take every opportunity to meet efficiently where necessary, and not meet to meet because it happens to be in the treaty.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, i should also like to begin by thanking mr florenz for his chairmanship of the delegation, mr dimas and the dutch presidency for the pleasant cooperation. fellas, i'm starting at the north pole. the warming is happening faster than expected . glaciers are melting, permafrost is thawing, the tree line is shifting... the people of the arctic regions are now being forced to adapt their lifestyles to warming. the societies in the arctic regions are experiencing on a small scale what the world as a whole will see. we are faced with the dual task of adapting to climate change while at the same time limiting climate change. in the face of this enormous challenge, the outcome of the climate conference in buenos aires is particularly disappointing. it is of course good that the kyoto process was just being kept on track by a seminar. it is good that the european union has won its way and that this seminar is also a chance to look to the future, but it is all very thin. we must also acknowledge that the eu has become increasingly isolated. of course, a number of american states are willing to work on reducing greenhouse gas emissions and there is indeed interest in our system of tradable emission allowances. and indeed, scientists who deny man's influence on climate are hard to find today. we have to count our blessings, but the essence of buenos aires is that the urgency felt in europe is not shared in other parts of the world. why is that? is it a lack of responsibility on the part of the american government? is it a lack of understanding on the part of the rest of the world or is it a lack of diplomatic skills on our part? do we lack the power of persuasion? we must regret that the eu did not have the necessary appearance. binding targets beyond 2012 are worth pursuing, but unfortunately they have become a european workhorse. the eu will have to prepare very well for the seminar in the spring. we need practical proposals, and that requires leadership and persuasion, but above all courage. it is important to come forward with good, productive and substantiated proposals quickly. the eu will also have to be open to the approach in other countries. i see at least three ways out that we all need to explore. first, to do more to adapt and help adapt, especially for developing countries. the contributions of african and central american delegations in buenos aires all began with mentions of unpleasant climate change. adaptation is not easy, but it certainly should not be limited to aiding in major disasters. the adaptation of lifestyles is a major issue and requires credible programmes. secondly, the transfer of technology. we must abandon our ideological reservations about the clean development mechanism and instead invest fully in the transfer of technology. thirdly, we will have to focus much less on a national approach, the targets per country. we must put much more emphasis on sectoral objectives. in the discussions we had as a parliamentary delegation, the sectoral approach emerged as a viable way forward. we will have to negotiate with the steel, cement and paper industries. competition in these sectors is global and global objectives based on best practice are therefore obvious. the wto can play a role in this.
president. - mr president, this parliament has had many debates in recent years on the need for climate policy. the kyoto protocol will actually come into force next month, and it is therefore good to look back at what we have decided in recent years. i notice that there is less interest in climate policy. it is becoming increasingly difficult to get a majority for necessary measures. the reason for this, i believe, is that we are not seeing much of climate change and that the negative effects will be felt most strongly in remote countries. it is also thought that reducing greenhouse gas emissions will cost economic growth. i would like to draw the attention of members to a study by iiasa, the international institute for applied systems analysis in laxenburg, austria. the scientists involved have shown that climate policy can be much cheaper if air quality policy is linked to climate policy. the net cost is reduced enormously, but we have to look at the regulation of other gases besides co2. so an effective policy does not have to be expensive. i would also like to take this opportunity, mr president, to point out to the commission the problems with the emissions trading system. the directive has been interpreted very differently in the member states, which threatens to create major problems. in some countries, for example, the chemical industry has been excluded from the scope of the directive. this was a reason for other member states to exclude the chemical industry from the application there too, in order to avoid distortions of competition. in addition, fines imposed on companies for exceeding their emission quotas are deductible in certain member states. again, there is no level playing field in the internal market. i would like to hear from commissioner dimas whether he is aware of this and whether an amendment to the directive can be proposed which would block these possibilities. in addition, in a number of member states there is state aid to industry. in my own member state, the netherlands, a national government has spent 600 million euros to buy emission allowances abroad, which has reduced the reduction for industry. i would also like to hear the commissioner's views on this. mr president, when the directive was being discussed, two amendments of mine were adopted in this house which should have prevented these problems. i must now say that the problems have arisen. our climate policy must aim at improving the quality of our environment, we have agreed to this and we must stick to it. we do not own the earth, but only temporarily use it.
president. - the climate conference in buenos aires did not produce the result we wanted. we had expected a major breakthrough with ratification and we wanted to set out, if possible, exactly what was to happen after 2012. when i look at the media coverage of the past few weeks, i sometimes feel that we are talking ourselves into the ground if we concentrate on these two issues. we all know, of course, that the united states has not ratified it. we also note that the rapid growth in india and china must also have an impact on the involvement of these countries in climate policy. yet, we do not often count our blessings. i agree with the commissioner who has just said that it is good that there are results. there are now 128 member states that have signed the climate convention. in the united states and canada too - mr president, the european parliament is debating today - at least according to the rapporteurs - a treaty which is a constitution in nature and substance. the report takes as its starting point the apparent contradiction between the council and the member states on the one hand and parliament and the citizen on the other. the rapporteur is ignoring the fact that the european institutions owe their existence to the voluntary transfer of powers by the member states. moreover, citizens continue to identify primarily with their own state. the european identity imposed from above, with its own symbolism, will not change that. the treaty gives the european union the characteristics of a state. the foreign minister is a deplorable example of this. a common figure must cover the lack of a common policy. moreover, the institutional anchoring of this person is unfortunate. the rapporteur should acknowledge that the dual responsibility towards the council and the commission creates confusion and gives rise to potential conflicts of loyalties. i therefore cannot describe this treaty as anything other than a blind and ill-conceived leap forward. the constitutional treaty is subject to criticism, mr president, and although the text is certainly an improvement on the present situation, the question remains whether the new text is good enough. i think that in some respects this is a missed opportunity. let us look at the election of the president of the european commission, for example. i think that this should be done by the citizens of europe, or at least by the european parliament. yes, it's in the new constitution, but there's only going to be one candidate, not several candidates, so there's no choice. the same applies to codecision: the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european model, a broad majority of peoples and a political majority, is applied across the board. it is in the constitution, but in practice it will not be enough. so it can all be a lot more efficient and democratic. the debate on ratification, and especially in countries where a referendum will take place, will not, however, be about the subtleties and differences between the old and the new situation. that's a debate for gourmets like us. the treaty is about more than these innovations, it is about a whole text. we are voting on a text which reflects what we have built europe in half a century. half a century of peace, security, free movement of people, goods, services and capital. when i think of the opportunities and challenges that dutch people, all europeans, can now seize in this great union, i have no doubt. i'm voting for that. in my country, there will be a referendum in a few months. i am now opposed to institutional chaos in europe, large member states can exert a disproportionate influence and smaller member states benefit from law and order. i choose good and democratic governance over chaos. mr president, the dutch liberals will be campaigning actively for the new eu constitutional treaty in the forthcoming referendum.
the dutch delegation to the european parliament is debating the constitution, not whether we are for or against europe. we say yes, critically, because we are concerned with the advantages of the new constitution compared with the nice treaty. the europe of nice is unclear about the scope of europe. the new treaty, however, guarantees the influence of the regional, the decentralized and puts the national parliament as a watchdog, without any superstate, but effective and transnational cooperation against crime and food security. the new constitution also replaces more than 30 types of acts with terms that are easily recognisable to citizens, such as law and framework law. the constitution thus provides more clarity about the kind of europe we want and the decisionmaking we use to achieve it. the constitution also makes us more effective by extending the range of areas where decisions are taken by qualified majority, so that no single country can block the whole thing. there will be more democratic accountability, as much decision-making is taken out of the back rooms and council decision-making meetings become public. finally, the constitution gives us more rights for citizens: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, including the social dialogue, the incorporation of the charter of fundamental rights and, of course, the citizens' initiative. those who do not support this important step in the process of european integration will be responsible for maintaining the current nice treaty, which is more bureaucratic, slower, less clear and in many respects undemocratic. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the social europe is still too weakly anchored, so a critical yes. in short, mr president, the dutch pse delegation is saying a critical 'yes' to the constitution. we will be happy to express this attitude, supported by johan cruyff, catalan dutchman or dutch catalan in spanish action, in the run-up to the referendum in the spring. we hope that this will help to get support in the netherlands for this new project, the new constitution.
mr president, this constitutional treaty is worth defending. as a christian democrat, i am glad that the principle of subsidiarity has been elaborated, deepened and consolidated at this time. the european union has now also recognised and consolidated the role of local and regional authorities - the regional dimension. a better balance between what is happening centrally and decentrally, in brussels, in the national states and in regions and municipalities, is necessary. as a regional manager, i have also noticed for many years that information from the european union has been left at the council level for too long, that regions and cities have been involved in the debate and decision-making too late. i have also been able to participate in the hearings of the convention on behalf of the european organizations, particularly on subsidiarity. the treaty strengthens the position of regions and cities, which are close to citizens and where important tasks are carried out in many areas. this is closing the democratic gap that mr prodi also mentioned in his report on good governance. it is an important step that the european parliament now supports this and remains critical of the interpretation of this convention at the decentralized level in the coming years. i also propose that we as parliament ask both the council and the committee of the regions from time to time questions such as: the european parliament has been a key player in the process of european integration. do national parliaments also take into account the views of regions and cities in the subsidiarity test? i know that the committee of the regions is also carrying out a similar test. in short, this treaty is worth defending. that is why we will be working for it in the national states, especially when referenda are on the agenda.

in writing. while the european parliament has not yet taken a decision on the draft constitution, a party is being organised around us to celebrate its adoption, costing 375,000 euros. this is a further proof that two prejudices about europe, namely that it is undemocratic and wasteful, are entirely justified. my party is against this constitution for a number of reasons. we condemn the neo-liberal and socio-economic element which under the guise of 'free trade' undermines public services and deprives national and local authorities of the right to determine how they want to organise their public services. we strongly oppose the militarization of europe which is reflected in the constitution, for example in the passage which forces member states to increase their defence budgets. and we reject this constitution because it confirms the undemocratic structure of the union, rather than significantly improving it. perhaps some of my colleagues are prepared to sell their souls to neoliberalism in exchange for a handful of additional parliamentary powers, but i will not do that. the european parliament has been a great help to the people of europe in the past, and it is a great help to the people of europe today.
in writing. today the supporters of the constitution celebrate their feast. the european parliament cannot change the text of that document. the national parliaments or the voters who take part in national referendums cannot do so either. everyone can only agree or reject, and accompany that choice with feasting or mourning. this makes it impossible to abolish the obligation to increase armaments and solidarity with nato. nor can the free and fair competition which is so glorified in that text be subordinated to the environment, public services or the protection of labour. nor can it be decided that the collection of one million signatures of citizens required by article 46 should be rewarded by a referendum. the new social rights are lacking. the traditional authoritarian model of eu governance, with a strong role for the council and the presidency, without good opportunities to correct it by means of parliamentary democracy, is also not in question. i don't want this constitution. the festivities today are not in my name. in the netherlands, i will be participating in the campaign of my party, the socialist party, in the coming months to get a majority of voters to reject this flawed text.

president. - the great scale of the disaster and the devastation caused by the tsunami, and its emotional and material impact, will be felt by the victims for years to come. i congratulate the commission and the council on their rapid and adequate response immediately after the disaster and on the commitment they have shown. now, a few weeks later, we have to prepare for one of the largest reconstruction programs in history. my main concern is that we avoid the mistakes of the past. in any case, aid to asia must not be at the expense of aid to other areas of poverty such as africa. the world is rightly focused on asia, but tomorrow, some 20,000 people will die again from poverty-related diseases, such as hiv, aids, malaria and tuberculosis. tomorrow many more people will die or be displaced, for example in darfur, sudan and elsewhere. these people must not be the victims of the good and generous actions for asia. another point, mr president, is how we as europe can ensure that aid also reaches the victims, and thus not, for example, the criminal or criminal organizations or the pockets of the corrupt officials. good coordination is an absolute prerequisite, and the un has an important role to play in this. i am glad that agreements have been reached in jakarta on this, and they must be respected. mr president, we must do everything possible to prevent criminal organizations from taking advantage of the chaos and attacking us through robbery, child trafficking or whatever. international cooperation is of great importance in this respect. it is also necessary - as has been said before - to establish an early warning system for marine earthquakes. in short, i hope that the disaster will be a unique example not only of financial solidarity but also of the implementation of aid programmes. we must give not only with the heart, as someone said, but also with the mind.

president. - the next item is the report (doc. these precious qualities are more than necessary for transatlantic relations for the european institutions at the start of bush's second term. the only country which can still provide some order in our confused and unpredictable world is the united states, and therefore certainly not the united nations. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it is also washington which guarantees the survival of nato. it is precisely because of nato that europe today does not have to fear a conventional military threat. the european union is also far from being able to compete with the united states in the fight against terrorism and the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i sincerely hope that all the european institutions, including this house, will become increasingly aware of this. in the meantime, we look forward to a fruitful visit to brussels by president bush. i wish the council and the commission much strength and commitment to this end.
the debt burden is a major problem and is now receiving additional attention as a possible aid in the reconstruction after the tsunami. it is good that the debt burden is high on the agenda. debt relief is one of the millennium development goals. the interest burden has often been a huge burden on the budgets of developing countries for many years and in some cases is a real obstacle to further development. we need to look at ways of doing something about it, but the subject is complex and does not lend itself to impulsive approaches. i can imagine a moratorium on interest and repayment being imposed on tsunami-hit countries, but in general debt relief should not depend on occasional dramatic events. mr president, there is a difference between debt cancellation and debt relief. our group does not support the general cancellation of all debts which some in this house are advocating. we say yes to debt relief, but only where necessary, only for the poorest countries and always on certain conditions. we have tabled the following in a resolution as the ppe-de group, and the commissioner has already referred to it: debt relief is not a panacea for poverty. we must bear in mind that any amount of debt that is waived will be based on the amounts available for development cooperation. debt relief alone does not provide a country with new sources of income, provide schools or hospitals, reduce poverty in the countries, nor does it in itself stimulate further development. the funds available for this will be considerably reduced after the amnesty. when it comes to the development of a country, there is also an important responsibility on the part of the countries themselves. debt relief only makes sense if it is accompanied by good governance, democracy, respect for human rights, investing in one's own people; in short, if the money really does reach the poorest. debt relief cannot therefore be an end in itself, but only makes sense if it is a real stimulus to development. we can be generous, but there must be conditions attached to it, as with all forms of development aid.
mr president, there is nothing wrong with debt relief, especially when it concerns countries in acute need, as is the case at present in south-east asia. but one cannot comb everything together. it has been said by several speakers. there are countries with good governance and there are countries with bad governance and we cannot say for all countries that we have forgiven their debts and that is the end of the matter. in practical terms, we cannot treat zimbabwe in the same way as, say, uganda. for each point, for each country, what is politically best must be considered, and debt relief is one of the elements that can contribute to the development of the country. as for the resolution, it is extremely vague. what are the practical consequences for the european budget, for example? i believe that the european investment bank is the only one that lends. the european budget guarantees these loans. what happens when we write off those loans? how much is that gonna cost? perhaps the commission could comment on this?
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, we in this parliament are pleased to welcome the outcome of the third round of the presidential elections in ukraine today. the unravelling of the crisis in ukraine is a great victory for democracy and freedom in a country that has been on the brink of an autocratic state for the past decade. it is also a boost for the european union and, in particular, for this parliament, which has played a major role in the positive outcome of events in ukraine. democracy has finally been established in ukraine. the people have been able to express their opinion freely on the course of the country and the result reflects the will of the people. what has happened in ukraine in recent months is a fundamental change. a kind of democratic uprising which - and we should thank all those involved for this - did not end in violence, as is often the case elsewhere. a new phase in the development of the country is beginning. the consolidation of democracy and the rule of law and the development of the economy are the first tasks for the new president's government. the european union must urgently implement the action plan put forward by the european commission in the framework of the neighbourhood policy - and i think we all agree on this - in order to strengthen as much as possible the ties with ukraine within this framework. the possibilities offered by this action plan must therefore also be fully exploited. however, the events in ukraine are not just a challenge to the country itself. the european union will have to reconsider its approach to ukraine. it is no longer enough to simply intensify the limited dialogue which has always been maintained with the regime of outgoing president kuchma - despite his policies. europe now has the task of giving a new content to its political relations with ukraine, now that a president has been elected who does not hide his european orientation and aspirations. those who demonstrated for democracy in kiev demonstrated for brussels rather than for moscow, and i think we should take this into account in our policy. i agree with the epp colleague that we really have to ask ourselves whether the european commission's policy, as just described by commissioner potočnik, is ambitious enough. in any case, the resolution which this parliament is discussing today goes far beyond what the commission or the council have been prepared to do so far. the question at present is also to provide real support to those in ukraine who really want to carry out reforms. they will have to make some difficult decisions and the support of the european union, clear signals from the european union, can certainly help. we have taken on a great responsibility for the smooth running of the elections. we are now also responsible for the further consolidation of democracy in that country. i would add that what has happened in ukraine is, in my view, a moment in time and a reason for the european union to review its policy towards all the countries in the region. it is also important to formulate answers and to anticipate the questions that will certainly come from ukraine but also from other countries in the region.
first of all, mr president, i am pleased to hear the bible mentioned in a positive way in this chamber. as a committed calvinist, i'd like to put that out there. on monday evening, january 10, the central election commission of ukraine officially declared viktor yushchenko the winner of the presidential election, and a stunning fact for both east and west became a reality. in one large former soviet republic, the opposition managed to defeat the post-communist nomenclatura by democratic and peaceful means. it is only natural that this should encourage the democratic forces in neighbouring belarus. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. at the same time, i hope that the council and the commission will give the best possible support to the reform-minded citizens of belarus. the same european backbone cannot be lacking in the yushchenko camp either. in two useful amendments, mr klich has put this very practically. the european union has a duty to help ukraine to become a member of the world trade organisation and to ensure that it is recognized as a market economy. in addition, european support has been equally provided to combat the widespread evil of corruption and to strengthen the justice system. in the meantime, the new ukrainian president and government are faced with two sensitive tasks. the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject. the first thing to do in foreign affairs is to establish the necessary good relations with moscow in a self-confident manner. and so ukraine gets a second chance at a new beginning 15 years after 1991. under president yanukovych, this would certainly not have been possible. i find the historical parallel that ukrainian expert professor gerhard simon drew in a recent lecture at eichstett university to be striking in this respect. the so-called orange revolution in ukraine is on the same political level as the fall of the berlin wall or the end of the soviet union. mr president, tibet has long been internationally recognized as part of the territory of china against the will of its indigenous people. it is an area that is being colonized by chinese and where many newcomers fill the function of civil servant, policeman or military. for many years, resistance to this humiliating situation has been suppressed by violent means. the death penalty and the death penalty with suspension are among the means used by the chinese state to suppress the desires of a large part of the tibetan population. we are rightly indignant in this parliament, despite all the political differences, at the death penalty and certainly at the use of the death penalty as a political instrument, and we hope to prevent the execution of a respected and influential buddhist lama in two weeks' time. china today is like south korea in recent years. in that model, economic growth comes before everything else, above people and the environment. the temptation to quickly swallow criticism of china is great when one is reasoning from a short-sighted european self-interest in the short term. after all, china is the country with the largest population and the fastest growing economy. for a century, this country has been stagnating and sidelined internationally by internal wars, foreign occupations and idealistic but ill-conceived experiments. it now looks as if it will play a very important role in the future. we are not going to accept that such a leading country applies the death penalty, mistreats minorities, neglects the environment, pays workers poorly, maintains unsafe living and working conditions and does not have a democracy. or do we want to promote from europe that our views on a democratic rule of law, diversity of political and religious views, freedom of association and democratic majority decisions are also reflected there? the european union must therefore be able to stand unambiguously on the side of the minorities and movements which are threatened and which must be able to play their part in the future. the commission and the council should not take as their main starting point in relations with china the fact that it is a large country with a rapidly growing economy, but that it is a country which does not release colonized peoples, puts economic growth above all else and also violates human rights. then we can get results.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party. - mr president, last month mrs maryam rajavi, leader of the main opposition movement in iran, visited the european parliament and addressed 150 members. it called attention to the injustice done to the iranian people by the fact that the mujahideen, who are fighting for a democratic state of law comparable to europe, are still on our list of terrorist organisations. the european list gives the regime of fundamentalist religious fanatics the right to execute political prisoners who can be linked to it. the regime has given a lot of negative attention to ms. rajavi's visit. those who want to continue unhindered the killing and torture of opponents have every reason to demonize and isolate the democratic opposition in exile. it is high time that the civil liberties and foreign affairs committees reconsider the list of terrorist organisations and thereby contribute to putting an end to the violation of human rights in iran. i regard the unabridged adoption of the proposed resolution, including paragraph 9, as a call for this. this is very important in order to achieve a democratic rule of law in iran.
president. - the fact that the cambodian government or any government is not taking adequate action against the trafficking and abuse of women and children is totally unacceptable. the rights of women and children must not be treated as secondary issues. it is the moral duty of the european union to use all possible means of pressure to change this situation. the european union has a duty to help the victims of modern slavery and to ensure that the victims are treated fairly. it is also very important to support ngos and individuals who are involved in the fight against violence against women and children. this is a task for both the eu and the member states. i would therefore like to thank those who have worked for this resolution.
president. - the european parliament is debating this afternoon the european commission's plans for 2005. i would like to begin by looking at the relationship between the commission and parliament. in the autumn of 2004, president barroso bowed to parliament's pressure. this creates the image of a powerful parliament against a powerless commission. i would like to ask president barroso if he can identify with this image of a commission without power. the european commission has the thankless task of leading the referendums on the european constitution in 2005. the commission has, however, manoeuvred itself into this precarious position. the commission has always rightly maintained that ratification is first and foremost a matter for the national state. this clear line has been abandoned, unfortunately. by actively campaigning and releasing financial resources, the commission has lost its independent position. with the help of their own tax money, the citizens are forced to accept a constitution they never asked for. the campaign for a european constitution should bring citizens closer to europe. but the question is whether the citizen will feel taken seriously if progress is already being made on this constitution. for example, behind the scenes, the design of the european external action service is already being worked on. however, national parliaments and citizens have not yet spoken, and that is despite commissioner wallström's statement that europe must learn to listen to the citizen, rather than continue with a policy of the past.
i have read with interest the 2005 work programme, which has the wonderful keywords prosperity, solidarity and security. there's a lot to talk about. as coordinator of the ppe-de group on development cooperation, i have looked at it with those eyes. it is rightly pointed out that the european union is not only responsible for the peace, prosperity and well-being of its own people, but also has a responsibility towards the rest of the world, especially the poorest countries. five years ago, the european union and other countries set out in the so-called millennium development goals how we intend to fulfil this responsibility. these include halving global poverty by 2015, ensuring universal access to at least basic education, improving the status of women, stepping up the fight against aids and promoting sustainable development. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). the european union is doing a lot, but there is also a lot more to be done, and certainly more to be done in terms of cohesion, cooperation and coordination. these millennium development goals will not be achieved if the international community, if we as the european union, do not significantly improve the quality of aid. resources can and should be used more efficiently. mr sachs of the united nations millennium project challenged the european union at our committee meeting last week on the role of the european union in the world. the work programme also deals with this. the european union is the biggest donor, and the challenge is to be the biggest player in terms of vision, cohesion and leadership. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i call on the commission to look at this again.
i am very pleased to hear, mr president, that the commission is going to work for the renewal of europe. i think that innovation really should be the key word in the coming years and i have heard similar voices from the presidency and i welcome that wholeheartedly. we need innovation, especially in the economy. many colleagues have pointed out the disadvantages and possible risks of reforms. i think we should talk a little bit more about the downsides and risks of the status quo. reforms require political courage. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i hope that this commission, unlike the previous commission, will be prepared to do the same and not put forward another set of watered-down consensus proposals without substance. change almost always hurts. change affects vested interests, and this may also explain in part the resistance that exists in some circles. but the longer we delay reform, the greater the pain. mr barroso, in dutch we say that gentle healers make stinking wounds. i don't know how to translate that, but i think the message is clear. there's a lot of talk about innovation, but in the meantime, we're still putting our money into old businesses. we are still pouring our money into an old agricultural system designed for the priorities of the 1950s, and into social security systems designed for the society of half a century ago. we are still maintaining, with a lot of state aid, industries that are deadly and polluting. we are still trying to solve the economic problems by increasing the budget deficit. i hope that this commission will take the lead - and i also call on the presidency and the member states - to invest in knowledge and skills, in education, education, education and innovation, in the environment and in the services market. i hope the commission will simply keep to its word. in free markets, open markets and in initiative and entrepreneurship, that's where we should put our money and not in old politics.
i thank the president of the commission for the brief, clear and powerful presentation of his work programme. i prefer to call it an action plan for the next five years. i would like to make a point of saying that many of the action points you have mentioned can only be really assessed in march and april, when we know what will come out of the renewed lisbon process. i am not talking about the lisbon process, but there is one thing that worries me a little. we are all talking about a strategy, the strategy that focuses on competition, growth, more jobs, but also sustainability. in a strategy, you can't take out one element. you were right to speak of social sustainability. i did not hear you say anything about environmental sustainability. but this too must be an integral part of the lisbon strategy, otherwise it will not work. i therefore expect that social and environmental sustainability will receive greater attention in the lisbon process. i have had time to go over what you have just said. what i really miss - but perhaps you will come back to that later - is the point of mobility in europe. this means that we must not only deal with the question of financial services but also with diplomas and ensure that a migrant worker or intermediary feels at home in europe. in this connection we must also create more jobs. you have spoken of partnership, and that means partnership not only with the citizens but also with the member states, the regions and the social partners.
on behalf of the green group, i would like to begin by expressing my appreciation for the energy, time and commitment that the high representative has put into keeping a fire burning over the past few years. a very small spark of hope, hope that despite all the setbacks, a solution could be found in the middle east, in the conflict between israel and the palestinians. because let's be honest, we've had a lot of debates here in the last few years, a lot of resolutions have been passed full of good intentions, but frankly i think there were a lot of people - and i was one of them - who thought: this is a hopeless case, we'll never find a solution. it seems that this situation of hopelessness has now come to an end or may soon come to an end. arafat has been succeeded by abbas, a president who is not only prepared to make good promises but also to take bold steps. the sharon government is made up of parties which are actually in agreement on one thing, namely withdrawal from the gaza strip. i know, when it comes to the middle east, too much optimism is never in the right place, because one bombing and the momentum could be gone. but i think that the evacuation of the gaza strip offers a unique opportunity, the best opportunity in years, to break out of that atmosphere of cynicism and scepticism and finally take another step forward. but this also means that the role of the european union must change. the european union must move from beautiful, general statements to very concrete, targeted assistance in the evacuation, in order to make it go well. what does that mean in the opinion of the european parliament and also in the opinion of my group? that evacuation can only take place in consultation, consultation between israel and the palestinians in the presence of an international observer mission. what exactly can and will the european union do? in november 2004, the high representative, in consultation with foreign ministers, reiterated the council decision of march 2004. the european union supports the withdrawal from the gaza strip if five conditions are met. it has to be part of the road map, it has to be a step towards a two-state solution. until then, i think, no problem, but watch out! israel must not use the evacuation of the gaza strip to establish new settlements in the west bank or to expand existing settlements. the transfer must be carried out in consultation with the palestinian authority and israel must cooperate in the reconstruction of the gaza strip. that, of course, is where the problems lie. hence my question to the high representative: does he think that these conditions are being met at the moment or does he think there is a chance that they will be met in the near future? if not, what will the european union do to ensure that these conditions are met and if they are not met, what will the european union do? are we taking our hands off it? i hope not, but if we're serious, we should try to stick to our own terms. okay, let's be optimistic, because that's what this case needs, and let's assume that the conditions are met. what exactly is the eu going to do? i would like to say that we must now move from beautiful declarations to concrete proposals. for example, are we going to cooperate in sending an observation mission? this is in all the plans, but will the eu be involved? what exactly are we going to do with the united states? - we're going to mediate? is there a request from israel, is there a request from the palestinians? are we going to finance the reconstruction? not in general, but very concretely - if you go to gaza, you know what is broken and what should be restored - are there plans for that? once again, mr president, madam high representative, madam commissioner, the evacuation of the gaza strip offers a golden opportunity to take a step forward. if we let that chance, if we let that possibility slip out of our hands, if it goes wrong in the gaza strip, then i'm afraid that the picking up of the pieces can take a very long time.
mr president, europe must keep hope alive for both peoples, and the only way to do that is to keep the hope of a lasting peace alive. for this we must try to put the two-state solution and a peaceful and total end to the occupation at the top of the agenda. withdrawal from gaza is a hopeful step, but it cannot be the final horizon. without the involvement of the united states of america, this cannot be achieved. mr abbas has set a huge agenda for himself: stop the violent intifada, reform the security forces, create a viable palestinian state and create a lasting peace with israel. we must fully support his agenda, again, to keep hope alive for both peoples. we can actively help him now to fight corruption, further promote democracy in palestinian institutions and support good governance – things the palestinians desperately want– because for this we do not need the united states. we should offer our complete assistance as soon as possible by asking mahmoud abbas how we, the european union, can help further.
president. - mr rübig's report is on the improvement of digital content. one aspect of this improvement is certainly the increase in accessibility. digital content should be made as accessible as possible to people with disabilities. consider, for example, the visual impairment, for which speech technology or a braille keyboard is used. this accessibility is not very good, because the technical features of websites do not correspond to the special equipment used by these people. for the sake of good website standards, there are the w3c guidelines. what it still has is a complete lack of implementation. the econtentplus programme should contribute to the international harmonisation of accessibility guidelines for people with disabilities. it is even financially attractive for companies to make it work. the construction of the accessible application costs more money, but the cost savings in maintenance and management make the financial result positive. this is a subject which deserves a place in the programme, all the more so since the objective of accessibility, more than the other objectives, requires international coordination. the focus on accessibility for people with disabilities is important to me in order to determine whether there is sufficient added value beyond national policy to be able to agree to the budget increase. mr president, attention to people with special needs must be given a hand by integrating accessibility into the programme as a whole. can i assume that the commission intends to include accessibility requirements in the selection criteria and performance indicators? please find out how much emphasis the commission is going to put on accessibility for people with disabilities in relation to the corresponding budget. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the programme is implemented in a way that is compatible with the european union's values and standards.
in writing. today we remember the atrocities of a great and powerful state founded on the principle of the inequality of men, which therefore considered it its right to starve and then slaughter groups of people deemed undesirable in its own country and in other countries. the important thing is that this is now widely abhorred in retrospect and that virtually everyone wants to prevent a repetition of this. even the parties represented in this parliament which are most closely linked to the nazi past do not vote against this resolution but abstain, partly on the grounds that my group is also one of the authors of the resolution. the text was questioned in my group because it seems to have deliberately concealed the important role played by communists in resisting the nazi barbarity and, without amendment, could give the impression that poland shares responsibility with nazi germany for the murder of millions of jews. as a descendant of victims who were killed in auschwitz, i look less at the precise text than at the breadth of the condemnation. the lesson of auschwitz must be that humanity can only survive on the basis of human dignity, equality and solidarity.

member states should cooperate effectively. that they can see beyond borders and also know how things are elsewhere. that they also agree on a number of basic standards, for example in criminal procedure law, and above all that they also build on trust in each other and also on the reasons why they can indeed trust each other. the proposal by mr costa is particularly important for the work on confidence, efficiency and good administration of justice. the report by mr di pietro is mainly concerned with improving cooperation on information. my group therefore fully supports both reports and i would also like to thank you very much for the great work you have put into this and the fine cooperation, but much more is needed than these small steps forward. there is a whole host of new proposals in the making, and they are all small steps forward, at least on paper. because in my experience, the member states are very often on the brakes. for them, the idea of mutual recognition seems to be a way of not having to change anything at national level. we all have our own shop and the others must respect the decisions of that shop. i can tell you that my group is not afraid to look beyond the frontiers. we are a little bit reluctant to face this huge pile of new rules because such a pile often affects the visibility, the resilience of the people who are constantly faced - i'm the reporter. mr president, i am sure i will do you a great favour if i say that i will not speak too long and will not bother you too long with my soft voice, because i am afraid that as a liberal i must say that there is not exactly a level playing field between me and the flu virus, but that i have simply lost the competitive edge. the eu treaty actually has two objectives which are relevant to this report. one is: on the one hand we have state aid rules which are supposed to ensure the proper functioning of the internal market, on the other hand the treaty also states that these rules must not impede the proper functioning of public services. the single market has brought us many benefits, including in the area of public services. we also note that we are all very attached to the so-called european social economic model with good public services, which has also always given us a great deal of competitiveness. the proposals of the european commission are very welcome and i am glad to hear that the words of mrs kroes actually only underline what we have also put in our report, namely that we are aiming at reducing state aid on the one hand and on the other hand also at eliminating unnecessary administrative burdens and that we are focusing on those matters which really disturb the internal market. that is bad for our economy and therefore bad for the foundation of public services. let me just summarize a few key points. firstly, i would like to emphasize once again that the focus must be on the citizen as a user of services and as a taxpayer. that seems a bit of an open door, but too often in reality it is actually about political interests, corporate interests, institutional interests and all kinds of other interests and that is why the interest of the individual citizen has to give way quite a bit. the second point is the definition of what are known as small services of general interest. the commission has proposed to exempt these small services from the state aid notification requirement and has more or less suggested that we apply the same threshold as for small and mediumsized enterprises, namely an annual turnover of up to eur 50 million. firstly, the committee on economic and monetary affairs has followed that suggestion on my proposal. i do think that in a few years' time we will have to start looking at what that means in practice, because we really have no idea how many companies we are dealing with, how much state aid in total, how many state aid applications. so i actually want to know exactly what the impact of that boundary is. another important point for me is what is called evidence-based policy making. for years we have been waging a classic trench warfare here in parliament between the left and the right on the liberalisation of public services. i think it is important that we get the facts on the table there at least once. we therefore call on the commission in the report to present a detailed report on the state of these public services in four years' time, when this system is due to be renewed. finally, a little more or less technical point. there is a paragraph in the report somewhere, which was inserted after we had adopted an amendment, and that paragraph seems to suggest that parliament is proposing that in all cases the contract for the provision of a particular service should be awarded by a tendering procedure. that's not the point, of course. i think there are many people, perhaps not all of them, but many who agree with me that this is a good procedure in itself and that it leads to good results in many cases, but i would like to clear up a misunderstanding, that it is not the case that parliament is going to decide here today that from today all services must be provided through such a procedure. unfortunately, no amendments have been tabled to correct this misunderstanding, but i would like to convey that message to the european commission here in the plenary.
mr president, you have indeed noted that we are here tonight with many ladies in our group and i know that the commissioner will do well to do so. i would like to try to compliment mrs vayssade on the sharp words which have been said on our part and to see how we can reach compromises on this matter. i see that there are a number of points where she herself has already indicated that she wishes to take account of our amendments. the first point is, i think, the inhouse provision of services, and we would like to stick to what we said in the herzog report last year. so if she could support amendments on that point, i think we could go a long way. the second point is public procurement, the transparent tendering which is in a number of mr purvis' amendments, which is also in an amendment by mrs wagenknecht, which states that tendering should only take place where legally required, that is where it is really required. perhaps that could be a bridge to clarify what you yourself indicate was not properly captured. and then we can, in the different texts, because there are always four different texts, we can make a consistent story. the last point is indeed the exclusion of hospitals and social housing. we also regard that as a fairly random list. we would also like to see that clearly examined and looked at what it means, but in fact we also want to indicate that much more needs to be done and much more needs to be looked at how you now set the criteria? we believe that this should not be done in four years' time, but that we should have a framework directive on services of general interest now, when the services directive is also under discussion, in order to establish clear criteria for a mix of private and public services. so i would like to continue to speak to the commissioner on that point.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party thank you, mr president, i think mr karas has rightly said that we are dealing with a kind of battle of will in this parliament and a debate on how to approach macroeconomic policy in the european union. i often get the strong impression that the epp and also parts of the liberal group are waging the previous war in this debate, and are still very much relying on old arguments, on the stability and growth pact and structural reforms, and are not tackling the challenges that are presently being faced in a clear way. and, moreover, i often get the impression that there is a great deal of national politics and national discussions going on in this european parliament instead of focusing on the european dimension. my group is more in favour of the approach taken by the european commission to reform the stability and growth pact and also the approach taken by the luxembourg presidency. i refer to the decisions of the ecofin council of 13 september last, which very clearly also set a course for reforming the stability and growth pact in the context of the wider debate and in relation to the lisbon strategy and the global economic policy guidelines. the reform of the stability pact should therefore address the preventive aspects, the differences in economic developments in the member states and the corrective aspects, and also promote an improvement in the excessive deficit procedure. but what is most important, and this is what we in our group want to emphasize, is that we are going to pursue a genuine macroeconomic policy in europe, and at the same time consider europe as an economic unit, as an economic entity. bush recently said that in the context of foreign policy. i think that this is precisely what should be at the heart of macroeconomic policy, that we should no longer compete with each other primarily as member states and seek to profile ourselves against each other and therefore also defend ourselves when it comes to the stability and growth pact, but that we should choose that european perspective and start to conduct economic policy from there. for this reason, we must also take a serious look at the lisbon strategy and its link with the reform of the stability and growth pact, and that is why my group will continue to emphasize in that debate that we will put the emphasis on investment and also look at how we will distinguish, within the standards set out in that stability and growth pact, between current expenditure and expenditure that is really intended to invest in the knowledge economy that we want in that lisbon strategy, and we do not want to do so in a way that member states themselves can decide how to account creatively, but by making clear european choices in it and setting out a european strategy to write off, for example, investments in it over a longer period, as is also the practice in the business world. we hope that some of these issues will be dealt with in these reports and that this debate will be continued in the march plenary session on the revision of the lisbon strategy and the preparations for the spring summit.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party president, first of all, my thanks and congratulations to frédérique ries. it has done important work to improve the implementation of the action programme on the environment and health. the action programme itself is disappointing. i admit, of course, that research is needed, but an action programme also requires that we take strong measures to tackle health problems, and those measures are lacking. our discussions in recent weeks have focused on dangerous substances and the conditions under which they can or should be withdrawn from the market. the plasticisers in toys are the most conspicuous, but other substances are also involved. our group could live with the text as adopted in the committee on the environment, but we want a stricter line. if a substance is dangerous, it should be withdrawn from the market immediately and not wait for safe alternatives. a second point of contention was smoking. tobacco smoke is a major threat to the health of the heavy smoker. we therefore call for an active policy to combat smoking in public places. we call on all member states to take inspiration from the irish and italian examples. the report on the environment and health is timely. air pollution is a particularly serious problem in europe. the european commission has calculated that air pollution causes more than 300,000 premature deaths in europe each year. in the netherlands, 13,000 people die prematurely. it does not take much imagination to assume that deaths occur more frequently among residents of highways and industrial areas than among residents of wooded hamlets. europe has taken measures over the last 20 years, of course, and these have also led to large emission reductions. the use of catalytic converters in cars is successful, but the efforts so far have not been sufficient, the figures are worrying. what do you want me to do? first of all, of course, better compliance with existing legislation, but more can and must be done. for example, where do the car's soot filters go? the question is whether the limits and limit values in the legislation are sufficient. the european environment agency notes in the signals 2004 report that there is increasing evidence that health effects are already occurring at dust and ozone concentrations below the values currently set to protect health. the protection of the environment, health and consumers requires a european approach. in the context of the lisbon process, ambitious environmental policy is a stimulus for technological development and improvement of european competitiveness. if we do not invest in environmental quality, we will not only be seriously undermining public health but also european industry. at the detroit auto show last month, a zero-emission passenger car was unveiled. we haven't seen that zero-emission car at the european car shows. so we're left with the question of who should pay for the soot filters? i prefer an ambitious europe with adequate measures to protect public health and let us start right now with making soot filters compulsory. that's very important.

in writing. switzerland is a european union in miniature, surrounded by eu territory but divided into 23 states, each of which has a great deal of autonomy and can therefore also have its own tax policy. this has so far prevented direct taxation agreements between the eu and switzerland as a whole. the individual swiss cantons are in this respect comparable to liechtenstein and other small tax havens where letterbox companies are located. the cantons of zug and schwyz are particularly keen to take up this role. in reply to my written questions, it was revealed that the then european commissioner, mr bolkestein, preferred to ignore this and to continue to do business with switzerland as a whole. as long as these further steps are not possible, it is good that agreements are made on vat, smuggling, corruption and money laundering. it is also important that banking secrecy can no longer be used as a reason for refusing investigative requests from other countries and that direct contacts with judicial authorities are made possible instead of having to go through the diplomatic representation. the rapporteur rightly points out that further steps are needed, but unfortunately he fails to mention the most urgent ones.
in writing. without funding and planning by states, regional governments and municipalities, we would not have adequate public transport, universal education and universal health care. for such vital activities, the idea of a government retreat and allowing free competition is disastrous. when that happens, there will no doubt be small privileged groups who feel that their freedom of choice has been increased and that their individual needs are now better met, but the vast majority and society as a whole will go backwards. this report defends the neo-liberal view and even seeks to establish an interpretation of the altmark judgment on the possibility of private award of concessions in public transport. the european commission and the european parliament have waited for years for that legal ruling because of the many conflicting interpretations on public service provision, costing and competition. there is now a legal solution which preserves the right of municipalities to provide public transport services themselves. the european commission intends to submit an amended proposal which follows my proposals adopted by this parliament on 14 november 2001. i reject neo-liberal interpretations which stand in the way of that.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, we have known for years that less and less land is needed for food production. that means we can have more extensive agriculture and in that way also create more nature. agriculture is a specific part of rural development in my view, and so is the natura 2000 programme. nature reserves play an essential role in rural development. these cannot be seen in isolation from other aspects of agriculture and other rural employment initiatives. europe has been promoting this for years through legislation, we know the birds and habitats directive, in the netherlands it is translated into the flora and fauna act, and for all these schemes we have created special payment systems. we know about the mountain farmers scheme, we know about the natura 2000 supplementary payments, etc. what do you want it to look like in the future ? i have already said that, in my view, all these programmes are part of rural development and i therefore think that we must also ensure that within the framework of the rural development fund there is a special fund for natura 2000. the big question is: how should the money be spent? we know the system of cofinancing, as the commissioner has already pointed out, and i believe that cofinancing can take special forms. in some countries, this co-financing is clearly a problem. it is probably a bigger problem for poorer countries than for richer ones. perhaps the commission could examine the possibility of having different cofinancing schemes under this programme? another point which the commission might perhaps examine - i have already said that agriculture is also a form of rural development by nature - is: are subsidies to agriculture generally more expensive than subsidies to nature development in europe?
mr president, once people have acquired the right to drive a motor vehicle, they should normally retain it as long as there are no physical or legal objections to it. i don't think there's any doubt in this house. what we do have doubts about is the way in which the citizens are confronted with it. more specifically, the standards for obtaining and maintaining that right in the form of a driving licence. in addition, driving licences also regularly function as identification material. that also makes verification aspects important. i believe we should be extremely careful in establishing someone's identity, especially when it is a stranger. the misuse of identity cards does not always seem to be conducive to a sustainable society. therefore, up-to-date identification is a must, both visually and technologically. i believe that driving skills can make a significant contribution to reducing the number of road deaths. since reduction begins with prevention, it is of the utmost importance that drivers are able to participate in traffic in a good way, both physically and mentally, in theory and in practice. it is not wrong to make demands on all drivers. those requirements should ensure the suitability of the holder. the report by mr grosch gives me a great deal of reassurance on these points, for which i am grateful.

- i'm the reporter. mr president, we are discussing the directive on criminal sanctions for combating illegal oil spills at sea and as your rapporteur i am very pleased with the agreement reached with the council at second reading. it took a lot of intensive consultation and the agreement we have reached with the council ensures a firm but also fair european approach to illegal oil spills and oil disasters. i would like to stress once again in this house the social importance of this directive. the bill is a direct result of the prestige oil spill. hundreds of kilometres of coastline were seriously damaged and the current package of measures really does not only cover those accidents but also aims to combat illegal discharges into european coastal waters. these illegal oil spills do not easily make the front pages of the newspapers but are a greatly underestimated and under-reported problem, since some 90,000 cases of illegal oil dumping take place in european waters every year. every year beaches become polluted again, and as many as 40% of the dead seabirds on the north sea coast are the result of oil pollution. the council has taken a long time to reach a common position. that common position was not enough for parliament. we do not want paper tigers but good european agreements on detection and sanctions. the council has met parliament's expectations in this respect, and that is good for the citizens of europe and the environment. i should like to explain the main points. firstly, the harsh penalties for preventing illegal discharges. in december the council adopted a framework decision on the sanctions in the third pillar. the council has thus paved the way for this agreement. this third pillar proposal is also firmly anchored in the directive by including a passage on criminal offences. the council also ensured that both legislative proposals were adopted in parallel. parliament has thus strengthened its institutional position in anticipation of the new constitution. secondly, the detection of illegal discharges. most countries have little or no ability to act against illegal discharges, but by lowering the threshold for combating them to serious negligence, we ensure that europe can really act against violations; however, we must not go into a disproportionate criminalization of crew and captain and there has been much discussion about this. parliament welcomes the protection afforded by international conventions in the case of illegal discharges, but this is more complicated in the case of accidents. the commission and the council do not want to have additional marpol protection in territorial waters, because then there would be little practical action. a majority of the transport committee agreed with that. i have to say that i am not satisfied with the way in which the commission has dealt with this matter. can you, commissioner barrot, remove the fear that some people still have that the crew will be criminalised in advance in the event of accidents, because that is not the intention? mr president, the third point. the european coast guard, this is a longstanding wish of this parliament, necessary for effective prevention and detection of oil pollution. this wish has not been recognised by the council so far, and this agreement has. i am therefore very pleased that the council has recognised the principle of a european coastguard. it has been agreed that the european commission will present a feasibility study before the end of 2006, and commissioner barrot, we welcome the results of your study and also your proposal for a european coastguard. finally, we must of course do everything we can to prevent pollution, and that is the purpose of the directive on port reception facilities, but it is still completely inadequate. and what is the commission going to do to improve this? i thank my colleagues for the confidence they have shown in me and for the pleasant cooperation, and also the secretariat of the committee on transport; i also thank the european commission for all its support and cooperation. last but not least; the luxembourg presidency has been very diligent in negotiating, and it has not been easy. i am pleased with the result, i thank you for it, especially in view of the importance for the citizens and the environment along our coasts and in our coastal waters.

on behalf of the group of the european people's party mr president, the initiative taken in new york on 20 september 2004, with lula and annan in the lead, contains good ideas on aid, trade debts, remission of debts and new forms of financing. but in 2000, world leaders signed a declaration committing themselves to the millennium development goals, which were to be achieved by about 2015. now in 2005, the world can see that in africa, a goal like "everyone to school" has not come closer but has gone further. the promise to finance 0.7% of the national budget has also not been fulfilled. in 2004, such a declaration would be a bit like "management by declaration"; i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. we must do so now and not, as far as i am concerned, by creating a new global fund. the eu is committed to the mdgs by focusing all existing aid flows - the world health fund, the european development fund, the activities of the imf and the world bank - from the north and the south on the mdgs. tough appointments, tight direction and coordination. europe, as the world's largest donor, can take the lead. at the same time, 0.5% of the national development cooperation budget should be achieved by all european member states in 2010 and 0.7% in 2015. the eu itself should set aside 10% in its own financial multiannual perspectives, channelled through a single development budget with the mdgs as a legally binding instrument. this includes debt relief for poor countries to make room for water, education and basic services; the imf's increase in gold reserves is also a consequence of this. this includes fair trade and the immediate cessation of export subsidies, while new sources are provided. not to forget about the 0.7, but to think about new sources that are interesting. taxing the negative aspects of globalisation, such as short-term capital or arms trade, is also a good idea. it's time to act. i believe that the presidency is in good hands and is happy to work with michel and the other commissioners in that direction. we shall look in particular at the multiannual financial perspectives to see what all our declarations are worth.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on development and cooperation, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 2052/88 on the common organization of the market in milk and milk products (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). the fight against poverty is at the heart of it and we will not achieve the objectives if we continue to work at the present pace and with the present means. it is well known that if we really want to, we can still catch them and we must do our best to do so. the fight against hunger is one of the most important areas of action. every day, some 24,000 people die of hunger and malnutrition, 24,000 too many. our approach can only succeed if we arrive at a coherent policy on development aid, agriculture and trade and if we meet our commitments. that is why i wholeheartedly support the resolution. i would like to draw attention to one point which is not explicitly mentioned in the resolution, namely the position of women. research shows that the role of women is crucial to the success of development cooperation policies. however, women in the countries concerned are still not involved enough in the preparation and implementation of programmes. mr president, i am impressed by the work of the hunger project. recently, they have launched a major awareness campaign in the netherlands. it is a worldwide organization that is fighting hunger in many developing countries. their approach seems to be extremely successful, precisely because they focus on women through training, education, micro-credit, etc. through them they are able to achieve a significant and lasting improvement in social conditions and a reduction in hunger. women have a key role in society in agriculture, education, family hygiene, etc., which are crucial factors in fighting hunger. i hope, mr president, that we will be able to achieve a more effective policy by means of a more vigorous and coherent policy. as the largest donor, let europe also play a leading role in working towards the millennium development goals, and do not forget to involve women in the countries.
on behalf of the group of the european people's party. - mr president, as from 1 january 2005, wto members will no longer be able to use quotas for the impact of textiles and clothing. the implications for the european market are enormous. the first figures for january this year compared with the same period last year show that a fivefold increase in imports of clothing products from china is no exception. this has, of course, major consequences for european manufacturing industry. of course, it has been known for ten years that quotas are running out and perhaps industry could have prepared better for that. but now that it appears that hundreds of thousands of jobs - some say - at least thousands of jobs are at stake, i can imagine that there is some thought about protective measures or the temporary reintroduction of part of the quotas to give the industry a little more time to adapt. the wto accession protocol of china also allows it. mr president, if the commission is considering measures in any way, it does bring a new problem, especially when it comes to quotas. there are european importers of clothing from china who have already made large financial commitments for 2005. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i therefore urge the commission and the council to ensure that any re-introduction of quotas or other protective measures will be such that those who have anticipated the new situation will not fall victim to them. finally, mr president, i also believe, and this has already been said, that we must do everything possible to ensure that china also complies with its wto obligations in respect of the opening up of its own market.
mr president, well-meaning initiatives sometimes have bad consequences. the fao writes to parliament this week: 1.- we are mentioned seven times in the european commission's proposal, but we have not been consulted once on this proposal. 2.- there are currently no specific requests from governments for european boats. 3.- the boats and the industrial fishing sector on the atlantic coast are completely different from the canoe and catamaran craft of sri lanka and india. 4.- local yards have to repair the affected boats, which they are good at, which provides money, work and livelihoods for the people there. that would be about 2,500 boats with our money instead of the 200 listed here. 5.- avoid further damage to already severely affected fishing grounds and local fishing communities. mr president, that can only lead to one conclusion. help the local fishermen in the yards, but don't send any ships, no new tsunami on the asian coasts, no matter how well-intentioned. i am disappointed that the commission is only willing to accept 3 of the 22 amendments tabled by the european parliament, which i would have liked to support. on the basis of all this, and also on the basis of the letter from the fao and the reports from the local ngos, i shall vote against this proposal.
