president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97) for a regulation on the common organization of the market in bananas. it happened before in sri lanka, azerbaijan, syria and the democratic republic of congo, recently in togo and perhaps soon in egypt. sometimes succession is preceded by elections, but the winner is determined in advance. in togo, that phase has been skipped. the gnassingbe family took power from the olympio family by force long ago and wants to keep it that way. a lightning-fast change in the constitution after the death of the head of state made it possible for the president of parliament not to temporarily succeed him and prepare new presidential elections, but for the son to take over the state as if it were the inheritance of private property. actually, the son's position is no less legal than his father's. it's the continuation of an old coup. the continuation of this process has, however, removed the hope that togo could develop into a democracy after a german colonial occupation, a french colonial occupation and a long internal dictatorship. until now, the people have had to accept that profiteers have almost always been in power. these profiteers are relying on military power and were not interested in serving the interests of their people but in taking a privileged position for themselves. this situation must be brought to an end. not just in togo, not just in africa, but all over the world. let us not be selectively indignant at togo, but let us help to end military and economic protection for such regimes everywhere. that's what this is about right now. it's not the exception, it's more the rule. that means that we must ensure that this kind of effect does not happen again and that it is a good thing if free elections can be held in togo soon.
mr president, like afghanistan, nepal was a holiday destination in the 1960s and 1970s for alternative young people from western europe who were looking for a less materialistic lifestyle, different clothing and drugs, which were much less known then than they are today. now both of these alternative holiday destinations have become a place of terror, where no one will go who considers himself a tourist or a seeker of a better world. in both countries, attempts have been made over the past decades to impose a uniform authority from above without the real support of a majority of the population. there are also foreign interests in bringing a government to power or imposing a new regime. under such circumstances, people, realizing that they cannot bring about change peacefully, take up arms. in nepal successive kings have dismissed democratically elected governments because they thought they could do better. in doing so, they have not only pitted different political parties against each other but also called for a popular uprising. rebels have taken control of much of the country, especially in the west. the violent parties on both sides are not deterred. meanwhile, official politics are struggling over how best to control the rebels: the question is whether the european union will be able to achieve its objectives by means of a uniform dictatorship of the king or by means of a parliamentary democracy. belgian arms suppliers protect the king from the rebels. this hopeless misery must end. the traditions that mr mann is celebrating will not be coming back in that form any time soon, but the people of nepal do have the right to their own choice, to democracy and to human rights. let us not in any way impede the choice of the people of nepal.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0310/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the english-speaking coastal population as colonialists whom they want to oppose. this is particularly true in liberia, which was established in the 19th century as a homecoming colony for freed african in leeuwarden, friesland, a strike is currently taking place in which 30 workers from the atoglas company have stopped work in protest against the plan of the french parent company total-fina-elf to close down atoglas. atoglas is a well-run and innovative company and independent experts have declared that there is a profitable future for atoglas. the works council fully supports this and wants to prevent closure. yet the french management wants to close the company. i cannot understand why total-fina-elf is ignoring the arguments of the works council. total-fina is a company with a social responsibility. listening to the works council is the least you should do. it is also incomprehensible to me that total-fina is blind to the innovation taking place in friesland. we can also expect french companies to have an eye on regional development in the netherlands. the european constitution emphasises that regional cohesion and solidarity between member states are a common objective. the strikers in friesland are appealing to this european objective and to total-fina. keep the atoglas open. i call on my colleagues to support the strikers in friesland.

member of the commission. mr president, the commission welcomes the report of the european parliament on the eib annual report 2003 and encourages the eib group and parliament to continue the ongoing dialogue which has developed over successive annual reports. the commission is working more and more closely with the eib group on a wide range of issues, both within and outside the european union. as a ‘policy driven’ bank, the eib needs to keep its finger on the pulse of european policy development and to react when necessary. within the union, the eib has a very important role to play in taking forward the lisbon agenda and the european action for growth. i would particularly like to highlight, as does parliament’s report, the innovation 2010 initiative, which supports innovation and research and development, and the ten infrastructure facility, which finances ten projects. the bank’s long experience of infrastructure projects, and therefore its ability to offer expert advice, can and should add value above and beyond the financial advantages conferred by its loans. i would especially welcome the environmental activities of the eib, particularly its expanded lending for renewable energy projects. outside the union, the european investment bank has expanded its operations with existing partners and also taken on new partners. i would particularly like to highlight the bank’s work in the mediterranean partner countries, where activities under the femip have grown year-on-year. as parliament has noted, we will need to ensure that the bank’s future external activities are firmly framed in the context of the community’s external relations policies. we will take this into account during the work to develop the next set of external lending mandates, which will need to be in place from 2007 onwards. parliament’s report suggests that prudential rules be laid down to ensure that eib loans do not compromise the sustainability of the member states’ public debt. i would like to point out that the existing framework under the stability and growth pact provides a detailed and powerful mechanism for ensuring that member states’ borrowing is undertaken in a sustainable fashion. of course, you will be aware that we are seeking to enhance the pact still further. among the elements of revision of the pact that have been discussed, there is the need to increase the focus on long-term aspects of public finances, and to take increasingly into account government risks that can lead to increased debt in the future. specifically on public-private partnerships, the commission has provided more detailed statistical guidance for recording them in public accounts and has stressed the importance of demonstrating their value for money. the commission acknowledges the good work undertaken by the eib in recent years to improve its transparency and corporate governance. the new eib corporate governance statement provides a useful summary of its policies across a range of corporate governance aspects. the commission encourages the eib to listen closely to the comments received during the forthcoming public consultation exercise on its public information policies. in conclusion, the commission welcomes parliament’s report and hopes that the fruitful policy dialogue between the european investment bank and parliament will continue.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr lipietz, for his report, which is balanced and positive about the performance of the eib. this is justified, since the eib has made positive progress compared with previous years. especially in terms of transparency and openness. i would also like to congratulate the eib, and in particular its president-in-office, mr maistadt, on behalf of the ppe. we also recognize that there is still work to be done in the area of supervision. the issue of the prudential supervision of the eib is therefore rightly one of the main topics in the annual report and unfortunately there is still no satisfactory solution to this issue. the question is how best to ensure prudential supervision in the future. i would like to ask the eib and the commission to present a view on prudential supervision in the next annual report. can you promise that? president. - a month or three ago, on 10 and 11 december, i participated on behalf of the european parliament in the trilateral energy conference of the nordic council, the baltic assembly and the benelux interparliamentary consultative council. the main topics discussed were the promotion of renewable energy and the future of nuclear sources. an excellent document was presented, 'sustainable and secure energy supplies', which will also be discussed in the committee on industry, research and energy at the end of this month. the parliamentary delegations at the conference have once again established that we have not achieved the objectives we set ourselves in gothenburg and lisbon. the increase in the share of renewable energy from 6% to 12% will not be achieved. the finite nature of fossil energy sources requires an intensification of research programmes. we shall do so in this parliament when we consider the locatelli report next wednesday. the combination of private and public resources is the formula for the lisbon agenda. the need for new investment options and new forms - public private partnership, if you will - is also at issue here. commissioner piebalgs can speak freely within the priorities of this parliament and on a voluntary basis - i am not talking about hidden energy taxes, which is unacceptable - but within these framework conditions. the european investment in hydrogen technology, for example, as advocated by the high level group, is necessary. we know, the oil companies have a lot of room at the moment, they are important partners and they are doing a lot, but try to pull them over the line on a voluntary basis. i would like to ask the commissioner how he is actually implementing the talks he referred to.

about here? i'd like to clarify a little. we are talking today about the annual adjustment of regulation 1408/71. this regulation shall be adapted to the changes in social legislation and social security in the member states. the court of justice's case law is taken into account, and it is examined whether the elements required for these adjustments meet the general criteria for mobility and free movement, but also those of 1408. the proposals from the member states are therefore examined in advance. we are also talking about annex 2a, and that is a review of certain specific features. it is examined whether the proposals contain elements which require or not require certain services to be exported. i would like to mention the example of dutch social assistance, which does not have to we as a group have voted in favour of this amendment. but then the legal service came and told us that we as a parliament cannot approve of something that the court cannot accept. we cannot accept a not be the last time and i think we should just send a clear and unambiguous signal. that signal must therefore be included in the implementing regulation which we should also have.
today we are discussing the application of beijing + 10 in the european union. turkey is not yet a member of the european union. as rapporteur on women's rights in turkey, i can say that the situation of women's rights is on paper comparable to that of the european member states. however, the practice leaves much to be desired. violence against women, low labour force participation, participation in education and women's political participation remain major concerns. but all this begins with the right to say this, to demonstrate. the 6th of march in istanbul showed how important it is that we still have 8 march, an international women's day. a demonstration marking women's day was violently broken up. turkey wants to be a modern democracy and wants to join the european union, but then it must also show that it is a mature democracy in dealing with demonstrations. the european union also has an important role to play in this respect. the european union must show that it is going to put the situation of women's rights prominently on the negotiating agenda. that it closely monitors progress and takes immediate action in the event of breaches.
the committee on development and cooperation has adopted a report on the implementation of the convention on the rights of the child. mr president, as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation, i should first like to thank commissioner mandelson for the words he has just spoken. the various elements he mentioned i can follow very well and also share. trade and development cooperation are closely intertwined. the system of generalised tariff preferences which we are discussing today is a prime example of this. the amount we spend through this system of trade preferences is higher than the total european development cooperation budget. the system was in dire need of revision. it was simply not used enough and its structure was too complex to comply with wto rules. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. however, in order to make the system effective, the commission must quickly come up with a revision of the rules of origin, because that is what will determine whether we can actually make it more effective for the countries. it is very important that the benefits be generous and that we do not try to re-introduce protectionism through the back door by excluding all kinds of products, as the commissioner has just said. we support the proposed early entry into force of the new system in the countries affected by the tsunami on 1 april, but we also call for a transitional period for those countries which are not yet ready. it's also a matter of good faith. finally, the relationship between the gsp and the epas, which the european commission is currently negotiating with the acp countries. the commissioner knows that trade agreements are not without controversy. it would be too rapid and too rigorous a trade liberalization. it is therefore essential that the acp countries, and especially the countries which are not among the least developed, should have an alternative if they do not wish to join the epas. the 2008 revision of the sap plus could provide a good alternative. i would advise the commissioner to strike the right note on this point.
the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council and the commission on the basis of the proposals contained in the report. this system, as mentioned earlier, is one of the most important instruments for stimulating economic development in developing countries and thus for a fairer distribution of the world. it provides trade advantages to countries that need it most. in addition, countries that perform well in the areas of human rights, good governance, labour law and sustainable development receive further benefits through the sap plus programme, which is designed to provide additional incentives for governments to work seriously and sustainably towards further stability and development in their countries. it is a review of the existing gsp scheme, and that was necessary. the possibilities offered by this system have so far been little used and efforts have been made to make it simpler, more transparent and more objective. that will not guarantee an increase in the use of the scheme, nor in the additional possibilities. this is why we are calling for a review of the rules of origin and, above all, for adequate technical assistance to help countries to see how they can meet the conditions. the gsp plus should be an extra boost for governments in terms of good governance, respect for human rights and so on, but the commission wants to grant this scheme only to countries that are already complying with it. this will eliminate the incentive for those countries which could comply with it in the next ten years, for the duration of the scheme. that's a missed opportunity. the report therefore advocates that countries which will still be eligible for sap plus in the coming years should also be able to benefit from the additional benefits once they meet the criteria. mr president, our group supports the amended report before us. it is a complex matter and i am glad that we have been able to reach a compromise with the other groups and the various disciplines. i hope that the report will be adopted in this form and that the council and commission will be able to adopt the recommendations. the commission wanted to deal with this report earlier for the benefit of the countries hit by the tsunami disaster. i hope that these countries will also be able to really benefit from this arrangement. good news is the evaluation is finally coming. we've been waiting for this.
it is difficult, mr president, to explain in the midst of this violence that the proposal i made on behalf of the epp group to reject the de rossa report nevertheless acknowledges that there are some good elements in the proposal presented by the european commission. i didn't want to undo those good elements either. the request should rather be seen as a signal to the council of ministers, where 22 countries adhere to the rules as laid down in 1408 and there are 3 member states, the united kingdom, finland and sweden, which cannot agree. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i hope that the council will take them into account. i think this signal is picked up when there are over 200 people who have agreed to a proposal as rigid as the one i have tabled.
the treaty on the non-proliferation of nuclear weapons has proved its worth. states to renounce the acquisition of nuclear weapons in exchange for the option of using nuclear technology for peaceful purposes and the commitment of the nuclear-weapon states to a gradual disarmament. in the context of the cold war, the treaty was a great success. it has helped to keep the arms race in check and now provides the framework within which the enemies of the past have reduced their arsenals considerably. recent developments, as my colleagues have already mentioned, have made it clear that an international treaty to prevent the proliferation of nuclear weapons technology is still necessary. investing in the non-proliferation treaty is still a priority. unfortunately, we must admit that the treaty is under pressure. a recent un report warned that the erosion of the treaty could become irreversible, with a cascade of proliferation as a result. the spring review conference should therefore be used primarily to reaffirm the crucial role of the non-proliferation treaty, including the objective of ultimate comprehensive nuclear disarmament. in this context, the european union, as the defender of the international legal order, has a special responsibility to ensure compliance with the convention. this means that states that evade the treaty must be dealt with more harshly. the developments in iran and north korea are of course crucial in this respect. it also means that the eu must work to close remaining gaps in the treaty. for example, the test ban, the ban on nuclear tests, has still not been signed by all the parties, particularly the united states, and the united states is also continuing to develop nuclear weapons for use on the battlefield. these are all issues which, of course, undermine the credibility of this important treaty. developments since the last review conference have also made it clear that the convention must meet new challenges. the emergence of regional arms races in which countries seek to obtain nuclear weapons technology is a great danger. we cannot therefore dissociate the non-proliferation treaty from the improvement of regional security structures in the middle east and south and east asia. finally, and this is a new threat, there is the danger that nuclear technology, or even nuclear material, could be seized by what we call 'non-state actors', in other words terrorist organisations, which could, for example, use a 'dirty' bomb to wreak havoc. we must recognize that the existing agreements to prevent the loss or concealment of nuclear material do not provide adequate guarantees. that is why we are also calling for the european union to pay particular attention to this point in the resolution we are going to adopt tomorrow. we cannot leave that to the united states alone.

on behalf of the verts/ale group.– mr president, i am replacing my colleague, mr hammerstein mintz, who is ill. i wish to begin by congratulating mr wieland for his good work as rapporteur for the annual report on the committee of petitions. this report is the first for this parliament and because of the new members it provides parliament with extra information about the committee. this committee is the most direct link between voters and parliament. it is like an ongoing dialogue, in which citizens should feel that they are being taken seriously and can have confidence in the eu. in fact petitions are a strong tool for democracy. mr wieland’s report reflects the serious need to develop the ability and capacity of the committee to deal with situations in which cooperation with the commission does not work and/or the commission does not take appropriate action. it must be stressed that, in developing the ability of the committee on petitions to deal with those situations, the committee will need the support of parliament as a whole. against that background, mr hammerstein mintz tabled a few amendments to the report by mr wieland, which he as rapporteur and the committee as a whole unanimously supported. that is very important. they point out, firstly, that under the nice treaty, parliament has the right to bring action against the commission, for example, on the grounds of lack of competence; infringement of essential procedural requirements; infringement of the ec treaty and of any rule of law relating to its application; and misuse of powers, irrespective of whether parliament’s prerogatives are concerned. thus the committee is also putting forward for parliament’s approval a statement that it is considered legitimate for parliament to exercise its powers under article 230 of the ec treaty if this is necessary in order to put an end to a serious infringement of community law that has been reviewed in the examination of a petition, and where a significant difference of interpretation persists – in spite of efforts to resolve it – between parliament on the one hand and the commission on the other, as regards the actions required by community law for the protection of citizens’ rights in the case concerned.

the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the figures are higher than half a year's salary for romanian women. if it is true, it is contrary to the european directive on the quality and safety of tissues and cells, as the commissioner has already said. the directive only allows for reimbursement of costs and compensation for any inconvenience. the amounts mentioned would be considerably higher than these. i sent out a press release on this subject last thursday and on monday i found here in my office an e-mail from a romanian embassy and a message from the british government. romania has said, and i quote: "the global art clinic which was performing illegal - note illegal - fertilization and collection of egg cells was shut down by the government. the situation is currently being investigated by the prosecutor's office". the british government reports that their organisation responsible for this, the hfea, has investigated everything and has found no evidence that anything is wrong there. the ban that was in place during the investigation has been lifted and the organisation is now reporting an increase in applications for import of ova. egg donation is not without risks and women must sign a statement that they are aware of it. why would women with such high risk donate eggs if they're not getting paid for it? when you read the website of global art clinic, you're in for a surprise. it's a mail order company for babies. you can fly in sperm that will fertilize an egg from a romanian woman. you can choose your age, your education, your height, your hair color, and so on. the embryo is then sent by courier to the prospective parents for implantation. i have the following questions to the commission. firstly, the reports from romania and the united kingdom are contradictory. does the commission know what is happening there, on what scale, for what price, and whether what is happening is contrary to european regulations? thirdly, the directive on the use of human tissues and cells must be transposed into national law by april 2006. what action is the commission taking to ensure that the compensation does not exceed the reimbursement of costs incurred and any compensation for inconvenience? fourthly, can the commission draw up a list of the relevant regulations, including amounts, in the various member states and candidate countries?
we are discussing this subject here today, mr president, partly because the european union is providing funds for research into embryonic stem cells. i have expressed my surprise here in this house before, because this research is banned in a number of member states and is not compatible with the resolution adopted today in the united nations general assembly against all forms of human cloning. in addition, there is the commercial exploitation of women who, lured by large payments, donate eggs without knowing exactly what medical risks they are exposing themselves to. it is clear to me that the trade in eggs and human embryos must be stopped as soon as possible. the commission would also do well to examine whether it is not itself partly responsible for this, because it is using european funds to encourage research which is punishable by law in some member states. it seems to me that the principle of subsidiarity is being ignored here, because there is no problem here that cannot be solved within the member states themselves. commissioner, can you tell me what you think about this trade and can you promise me that research funding will only take place if this research is allowed in all the member states of the union? are you prepared to abide by the resolution adopted today by the united nations general assembly calling for action to prevent the exploitation of women?
the next spring summit, mr president, will be overshadowed by what the referendums on the european constitution may bring in the coming months. that is not conducive to sound decision-making on economic reforms. the decision-making at the forthcoming summit is also fuelled by fears of a deterioration in the welfare state. this has become clear since the autumn summit when the wim kok report was discussed here. i would like to call for a different approach to economic reform. is it socially acceptable that people over the age of 58 should no longer be allowed to participate in the labour market? is it social to saddle the young people of today with the burdens of our system which has become unaffordable, when they themselves can never benefit from it? i therefore call on the commission to work with the member states on the necessary structural reforms. growth is needed to maintain europe's social model. at the same time, the model needs to be reformed. anyone who still claims that structural reforms are not necessary is a dangerous demagogue.
mr president, a great deal has been said in this debate on the lisbon strategy, and i would therefore like to limit myself to three points. first of all: the lisbon strategy must become the next great success of the european union after the euro, enlargement and the constitution. we have no choice. but the condition is that we give the citizen the confidence and the space to develop new initiatives and to take on new challenges. my neighbour in rijswijk is perfectly capable of making good decisions about his future and does not need to be taken by the hand by the dutch authorities, and certainly not by the european union. that brings me to my second point. we should be more critical of the role of the public sector in the context of the lisbon strategy. the question arises whether the government should be the problem solver or whether it is sometimes the problem itself. of course, the government must keep an eye on the situation and impose certain conditions, but far too often we see that the government, instead of encouraging new initiatives, is blocking them. i therefore believe that deregulation, reduction of administrative burdens and de-bureaucratisation must be at the heart of the lisbon strategy if we are to achieve the much needed strengthening of europe's competitiveness. thirdly: i fully agree with the colleagues who believe that europe can be proud of its unique european model, but we must also realise that this social model can only be maintained if we generate economic growth again. my colleagues on the left too often perpetuate the myth that social policy and economic growth are at odds. whether it is the lisbon strategy or the services directive. they repeatedly suggest a false contradiction between a free, innovative, dynamic knowledge economy and social policy. i therefore find the socialist group's abstention from voting in the parliamentary coordination group last week incomprehensible. the other groups had really tried to reach a compromise that was broadly acceptable. i am therefore pleased that the spokesmen of that group are now saying that the text before us deserves broad support. it makes last week's abstention all the more mysterious, but let's keep it at a progressive understanding. mr president, there is no better social policy than one which creates more and better jobs and creates space for entrepreneurship. we shall see whether there is a majority to support this policy of the european commission. if so, that is a welcome development.
the lisbon challenge is the most important project for the coming years. the project will only succeed if all the parties involved - governments, parliaments, social partners - are willing to work together at european, national and regional level. if we are to achieve that unity of cooperation, but also that commitment, the main obstacle to success has already been overcome. i am happy to entrust you, mr barroso, with the difficult but also central role of being the centre of this process. when we look at the lisbon objective, a contradiction is created here which i feel is nonsensical. there can be no separation between the eu's competitiveness, social policy and the environment, because without growth we cannot meet our environmental obligations either. growth creates more jobs, jobs, incomes and prosperity for citizens. more people in work is the best guarantee for the financiability of social security and for meeting the rising cost of health care, as well as reform of affordable and sustainable pensions. the economic, ecological and social policies are therefore inseparable. the commissioner verheugen calls for attention to be paid to new initiatives, better and simpler legislation, new industrial policies and more support for innovation. however, there is one thing i miss about these initiatives, and that is retraining, retraining and retraining. without continuous education of the available workforce, we cannot get people who are currently marginalized - whether they are women, minorities or people with disabilities - into work. so if we want to achieve that europe of excellence, then the same applies here: not words, but actions.
i would also like to say a few words about the stability and growth pact, mr president, because i regret that the ecofin council did not reach agreement on it earlier this week. i hope that mr juncker, who will be present both at the extra ecofin meeting and at the summit, will be able to continue his work. i have full confidence in that too. i am sorry to see that the reform of the pact is being made into a matter for the president. i am afraid that this will lead to a kind of ordinary cow trade between member states, and particularly large member states, as we have seen too often. it is time for our leaders to show real vision and leadership. my group has already stated many times that it is in favour of reforming the pact to achieve growth without endangering the stability of the euro. i think it is therefore good that a qualitative assessment of the expenditure made by member states in their budgets is made. this morning we also noted this in the discussion on the lisbon strategy. europe is not investing enough in training and research to become the competitive knowledge economy we would like to be. it should not be that the pact prevents us from doing so. in business, an entrepreneur would disqualify himself if he did not make such investments in the future. but i tell you this: in the business sector, investments are not depreciated in one year. why do we do that in the systematics of the pact? why should we not also apply a longer depreciation period to the lisbon investments? that would be a substantive reform that would create space without jeopardizing discipline. another change that must take place is that there will be more european thinking. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. it seems that the leaders at the top are primarily concerned with creating a catalogue of excuses that they can use to get out of the discipline and use for all sorts of policies. my group supports a strong role for the commission and believes that the rules of the game and the assessments should be made jointly, from a european perspective.
i agree with the president that scientific research is both useful and necessary. however, the question is whether the approach in the locatelli report is the most effective way to a more innovative europe. firstly, how effective can action at european level be if the member states have the competence in these areas. the report proposes that activities in the member states be co-financed by european union funds. for example, eu funding for students, research buildings and facilities, and communicating with citizens about the importance of science. the member states are much more effective in dealing with these issues. european money will therefore be better used for this. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the future of the european union. secondly, i find that the commission's communication on guidelines for research policy lacks a strategic statement on research which is punishable by law in the member states. i am not saying that we should support such research with community funds. i am thinking of research involving the destruction of human embryos. if community funding is allocated to such controversial research, the result is that member states do not benefit equally from european research initiatives. can the commissioner comment on this? in conclusion, the ambition of the european institutions to establish themselves in the field of research does not necessarily lead to more and better research in europe.
president. - i should like to thank our rapporteur, mrs pia elda locatelli, for her report. it has produced an excellent and interesting report. research in the seventh framework programme is important to us, especially in the context of the lisbon strategy. a good link between research centres and industry is essential. i would like to make three points. firstly, science should be autonomous, but that is not the same as being free of obligation. scientific priorities are not a hobby of scientists, but must be question-driven. social problems should be a guiding principle for the public research agenda and on that point i entirely agree with mr hammerstein. the second point is the unnecessary bureaucracy, which has been mentioned before. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. for small businesses and small institutions in particular, filling out the paperwork and answering the questions is a near-impossible task. we therefore call for a two-stage procedure consisting of a short description of the research project followed by a screening, so that those taking part in the second round can have a reasonable degree of assurance that their proposal will be honoured. third, join forces. european research funding remains only a fraction of national budgets. article 169 provides an opportunity to join forces. in the field of clinical trials for developmental diseases, experience has already been gained. we can use this experience to carry out joint research on the introduction of a low-carbon economy, on hydrogen cells, on improving energy efficiency and all that sort of thing. the social demand is also met by a combination of forces, and we must work towards that.

president. - i think that the vote will take place tomorrow at 12 noon. i do not agree with the main points of the resolution and i support the commission's proposals in principle. it starts with the c-dumping, the c-sugar. this dumping, which costs us 800 million euros a year, is a dumping which is totally contrary to what is required in the world trade agreements. we will be forced to stop immediately and we must not think we can put up with it for a few more years. the commission proposal still maintains a world price, or a market price in europe twice the world price. today, it is three times the world market price. i think that everyone who talks about free liberalization, and it should be free, has not looked at the commission's proposal very carefully. i think the proposal is understandable, but there are a number of points where it could be improved. i am particularly interested in the action plan for the poor countries. here, of course, some of these countries must invest in industries to become more competitive. i do not think it is realistic to take the money from the edf. the money we are saving in this sector of agriculture could be used for that - not only for the european agricultural sector but also for the acp countries. then something about the quotas. trade is, of course, the best way to help countries. at present, the ldcs are allowed to go from 120,000 tonnes to 209,000 tonnes. what is the objection to doubling this 1% of the european market? that would be a very good compensation for the countries concerned in the period up to 2009 and it could really mean that they are getting something. commissioner, you have rightly said: "let's stay away from everything but arms" a. let us not let this parliament become the embarrassment of everything but arms and sugar. that would remove the cornerstone of a fair world trade policy and it would mean that we would fail in doha. that can't happen. i wholeheartedly support the proposals and hope that you will improve and strengthen them.
mr president, it was clear that when commissioner fischler made his three proposals about a year ago, only one was reasonable. we must change the sugar market, there is no escaping that. the consequences for many parts of europe will be serious, there is no denying that. the future price of sugar in europe will be essential to the whole sugar policy. the commission believes that it can control this price by introducing a reference price. the commission would like to explain this again, because up to now we have always had an intervention price for the main products. how can the reference price really guarantee a price on the european markets? then the least developed countries and the "everything but arms" initiative. the commission maintains, and this also has to do with the future price, that a quota system for those countries could not be feasible. how confident is the commission in this after the experience it has already gained in the balkans? we also had unlimited sugar imports there before. the commission was then forced to reverse its course and introduce a quota system. why should this not apply to the mol countries? i agree with many that the commission should pay more attention to the peripheral regions. i would like to say a few words about mr van den berg's amendments. i think it is an extremely bad idea that the compensation for the least developed countries should be paid from the agricultural budget. development aid is not just for farmers, it is for all sectors of society.
i would like to begin by congratulating the commissioner on her participation in the debate. you can have different opinions on the course and the way in which reform is being carried out, but i very much appreciate that. i also congratulate mr fruteau and mr dole, who have done a very good job and have tabled a resolution that sounds like a bell. why is the resolution so good? the resolution at any rate shows an understanding of the need for reforms and of the many social aspects involved. the resolution also shows that pure liberalisation in agricultural policy can sometimes backfire and that it is better to reach good agreements at world level. i am therefore surprised that there are still many in this debate who believe that liberalization of world food trade is the solution to all the ills of development cooperation and poverty reduction. sometimes you wonder where that holy faith comes from. over the past decade, global trade in food has been further liberalised, with europe now accounting for 85% of exports from africa to the developed world. and what's the balance? hunger and poverty have increased, 40% of the world's children are hungry and european consumers have anything but lower prices. it will not surprise you, mr president, that i have serious objections to the amendments, including those tabled by my good dutch colleague, mr van den berg. i sometimes wonder, and i think it is good to stress this in the debate, how it is that he wants complete freedom first and thinks that the solution will come from the chaos in the world food market. i don't believe in that. i believe in agreements, worldwide and in europe, to bring about responsible reform. his idea of using the agricultural budget for development aid is, in my view, nothing more than shopping with someone else's purse. mr president, no one can go a day without eating, and i hope that this will be the guiding principle for the further debate on sugar reform later this year.
in writing. despite its opposition to the resolution on the world conference on women, the christian union-sgp is by no means opposed to the normal treatment of women in this society. the fight against abuse, circumcision, trafficking in women, marriage-breaking, and honour-bending is wholeheartedly supported. equal access to good education and good health care is also important for everyone. however, some ideas cannot be supported. the participation of women in the labour market is not rejected, but the imposition of a numerical distribution of labour does not reflect reality. capacity and suitability are the criteria for selecting candidates for a post. sex is less relevant. there must be freedom of choice for both the individual and the organisation. it is up to individuals and organisations to make their own choices regarding their personal sphere of life, internal structure and external representation, as long as no improper means or methods are used. finally, extreme caution should be exercised in situations involving medical ethics. man is not the last link in an evolutionary process, but is a creature given life by his creator. he needs to deal with that responsibly. with his own and with others' as well as with existing and unborn life.
in writing. today parliament is voting on the organization of the sugar market, sugar subsidies and market access for the least developed countries. the debate between agriculture and development cooperation has not dealt with two aspects sufficiently. sugar and public health. diabetes has become the number one disease of the population. a disease with debilitating symptoms and consequences. it is a very costly disease, socially and economically. sugar consumption is also a major contributor to obesity. it's also a growing public health problem. the eu has rightly announced that it will take up the fight against obesity and diabetes in cooperation with the who. sugar consumption should be reduced and the decline in fruit and vegetable consumption reversed. in this context, it is inconceivable that parliament should be advocating low sugar prices and that no subsidy should be available for fruit and vegetables. if we want to subsidize agriculture and keep prices down, let's do it for healthy products. the second. lisbon, please. the eu wants to be the most dynamic and innovative economy in the world within five years. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. parliament has spoken very highly of this this week. it is difficult to see how the sugar subsidies are contributing to the lisbon objectives. europe must grant full market access to farmers from developing countries. i wholeheartedly support the amendments to this effect. of course, there must be a good system for european sugar farmers and workers in the sugar sector, but the continued support for an unhealthy product and a poorly innovative sector is inconsistent. we have to stop this madness as soon as possible. that's reason enough to vote against the motion.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0310/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, security and defence policy, on the situation in cambodia. after the french occupation of the much larger colony then called indochina was ended, especially as a result of the war of independence in what is now vietnam, the americans, russians, and chinese were ready to add the smaller new states of cambodia and laos to their sphere of influence. each of these great powers tried to arm its own satellite movements and to bring them to power with military support, in order to eliminate all other forces once and for all. in a sharp response to an irresponsible american intervention, power was seized by the khmer rouge, a movement that had the illusion that the destruction of the cities and the intellectuals would bring great progress for the poor peasant population. i would remind you that the united nations recognized that murderous reign of terror and not the vietnamese-backed insurgents who put an end to it. the liberators of that time still have a very large following among the people today, but they still feel mistrusted by the outside world. the poison of the past has made normal coalition governments and the normal functioning of parliamentary democracy in cambodia still virtually impossible. the political parties deny each other the right to exist and would rather destroy each other forever. it is fitting that opposition parliamentarians are treated as enemies of the state and imprisoned. the rich western countries have made a major contribution to that toxic climate in the past. we have a moral duty to help the cambodians to free themselves from it. criticism and condemnation are badly needed, but also the lesson that the outside world has shown the cambodians in the past that they can do better.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, security and defence policy, on the situation in cambodia. we have always assumed that cambodia would make slow but steady progress towards democracy, but of course patience is running out. the situation in cambodia has deteriorated dramatically in the last year, with the latest low point being the incitement against three opposition members, including the brave sam rainsey who was here two weeks ago. it was good to see him here, but it would have been better if he had not been here and could simply have played his opposition role in phnom penh. you do not have to take my word for it, because in the next few days the un envoy for cambodia, peter leuprecht, will be presenting his findings to the united nations. his conclusion is downright sad. in his four years as an envoy, little progress has been made. president, the european union, the commission and the member states have offered a total of eur 1 billion in aid, with the phnom penh office, election observation missions, financial support for demining operations, and so on. should we not conclude that eu policy has simply failed? we must have the courage to say so. the time for compromise is over as far as i'm concerned. if the cambodian government does not respect the basic principles of the democratic rule of law, such as transparency and accountability, we must suspend our support. in any case, a dramatic reorientation of our aid is necessary.

mr president, there has been a lot of talk about the right balance of security and civil liberties, but let us see how it works in practice. last year, an agreement was adopted, against the will of the european parliament, giving the united states access to passenger name records. just last saturday, a klm flight from amsterdam to mexico flying over the united states was denied access to us airspace and sent back to the netherlands. why? because the us had found that there were two passengers on board who were on a us list of potential terrorists. how did the united states get that data? how did they get those names? the agreement on passenger name records applies only to flights from and to the united states, not flights crossing us airspace. the united states was therefore clearly breaking the rules and i would like to hear what action the commission is going to take. secondly, i very much welcome the words of mr frattini, who said that we should promote and not just protect fundamental rights, and i would like to add that we should promote values and not just make legislation. i would like mr frattini to inform us of the state of play regarding the creation of the working group on fundamental rights promised to us by mr barroso. i have not heard anything about it. i hope that this working group will look in particular at proposals to end discrimination against gay and lesbian people. for example, how about the recognition of married same-sex couples? how about tackling homophobia, which is on the rise? how about encouraging those member states that have so far failed to recognise or compensate gay victims of the nazi regime? i hope the commission will answer these questions.
i am very proud that we are finally dealing today with this report on minimum standards in criminal procedure which should apply throughout the european union. my group voted against the european arrest warrant in 2001, partly because it did not provide for the rights of the accused, who would be transferred so much more easily to other eu countries under the new system. we can end the political struggle of that time today, because we are at least united in our view that the procedural rights of suspects are a necessary complement to the european arrest warrant. there have been many colleagues who have been very active in this subject and i shall have to thank antonio costa from afar for his proposals. i would also like to thank all the other members for their good cooperation. the commission has done a very good job, mr president, and that has made my job easier. she has collected many ideas through a green paper and has already consulted people. the previous chairman of the committee on civil liberties, mr hernandez mollar, also wrote a positive report at that time. i have taken his comments as a basis in my report. criminals do not respect national borders and so the search must also be carried out across borders. but the search must also guarantee the rule of law throughout europe. this report is not just about the rights of suspects, it is also about effective crime prevention. if member states do not trust each other, they will not be inclined to extradite their own nationals easily. we saw it for example with the british and dutch aircraft spotters who were arrested in greece. you remember that from a few years ago. it then led to a lot of protest among dutch and british politicians and citizens. however, in the same december the european arrest warrant was again adopted by the same politicians, which meant that all aircraft spotters would henceforth be extradited to greece. the question is whether such a transfer will actually take place, given the outcry against it. i therefore believe that effective crime prevention requires real trust between member states and trust requires clear agreements on the rights of suspects. of course, many rights are already covered by the echr, as the european commissioner has said. however, they are regulated as legal principles which are interpreted differently in the various member states. the european commission's proposal is much more specific. the rights will also be more visible and easier to enforce. the european court of human rights has therefore been reduced in size, and this is a welcome additional benefit. there are also rights added. the commission's proposal gives a defendant, as the european commissioner has said, the right to legal aid throughout the criminal proceedings and the right to an interpreter and a translator. the authorities are obliged to inform suspects of their rights. i agree with the general outline of the european commission's proposal, but it is clear that this is a first step and that additional framework decisions are needed, for example on admissibility of evidence, on bail and on methods of investigation. i hope the european commissioner will put a stop to this. i am also glad that the committee on civil liberties has decided that a number of points in the commission's proposal should be clarified. it now provides that no one may be without a lawyer for more than 24 hours, that no hearing may take place if a defendant has not yet obtained legal aid, and that there is an independent body through which a client can complain about his or her lawyer. for the rest, it is important that not only governments but also lawyers and organisations concerned are asked to evaluate compliance and that this is then published, because only transparency will really further promote trust. i would like to say a final word on recital 8, because i know that this is a crucial paragraph for many members. there seem to be a few member states which want to retain the option of not applying these very basic rights of suspects to citizens suspected of serious and complex forms of crime including terrorism. that is what worries me, at least as a green. as rapporteur i would like to promote a compromise, but personally i really believe that member states will be much less inclined to extradite people to other eu member states if they fear that minimum rights, minimum standards are not being respected. i also believe that it is a matter of principle that minimum standards should by definition apply to everyone. when mr hernandez mollar, a leading christian democrat, was rapporteur on this subject, he never, on any occasion, advocated exceptions to the minimum standards. the question now is whether the report should be adopted at all if this exception is not broad enough. i hope that we can still come to an agreement tomorrow when we vote, because i am convinced that the rights of our citizens deserve support throughout this parliament.
the proposal for a framework decision is a follow-up to the green paper on procedural safeguards for suspects in criminal cases throughout the european union, which was presented in 2003. the commissioner said that the proposal aims to eliminate the discrepancies between the procedural safeguards available to suspects in the member states. there are undoubtedly many advantages to this proposal. i can briefly mention a few. it cannot be denied that this framework decision, by codifying minimum standards, can and will make a useful contribution to the level of legal protection within the european union. the rapporteur, mrs buitenweg, has already spoken about this. secondly, it cannot be denied that this framework decision can have a positive effect on mutual trust in each other's criminal justice systems. the latter is all the more important now that we have such a large and enlarged european union. the framework decision is also less far-reaching and therefore more realistic than the green paper, and it is good that the framework decision once again expresses the protective function of criminal law. but, mr president, there are also questions, questions about subsidiarity, because it is doubtful. the treaty on european union, unlike the harmonisation of substantive criminal law, does not contain an explicit legal basis for the harmonisation of criminal procedure. the proposal also concerns basic standards which are already enshrined in the echr. the ppe group is therefore in favour of this proposal, but not naively, in other words positively critical. we are disappointed that this proposal, with all its obvious advantages, is going to lead to a great deal of additional bureaucracy. the netherlands, my own country, has this number. the commission proposal will cost at least some 40 million euro per year for translation, storage of video and evaluation of the new system. the vote in the commission has added many additional aspects, which will increase the amount for the small netherlands and therefore also for other member states. i would like to say on behalf of the epp: the benefits outweigh the additional costs, because suspects must be well protected everywhere. but the commission has made it very clear that this far-reaching proposal must not affect the national legislation of various member states against terrorism. that is why we found mrs buitenweg's amendment to delete the report unacceptable in this respect. it then came up with a compromise, a first compromise, under amendment no 4: 'subject to the fact that they do not alter the substance of the provisions of this framework decision and are in conformity with the echr and the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, member states may adapt those provisions ...'. but even then, several member states in the european union, including spain, france and the united kingdom, will have problems with their current fight against terrorism. i therefore conclude, mr president, with the good compromise i reached with the rapporteur under no 51. it refers to the rights of the echr and that is fine. i would call on everyone in this house to support amendment no 51 and not amendment no 4 because of the broad support for the report, because i am looking not only at that broad support but also at the socialists, the socialist government in spain and in england. do not get your own member states into trouble! if amendment no 51 succeeds and amendment no 4 fails, the ppe-de group will support the report by a large majority. otherwise, we regret that this report is being misused to jeopardize the national fight against terrorism at this time. i am sorry to say that we will have to withhold our approval of the report.
on behalf of the ind/dem group mr. president, the police are your best friend. with that slogan, the dutch police once tried to improve its image and restore authority for public order and safety. the need for crossborder cooperation is evident in our time, when public order is once again at stake. national police institutes are already organising intensive contact at the level of commissioners. the fact that this is now being extended to the training of senior management is a welcome addition. the european commission's proposal to make the european police college an institution of the european union has my conditional approval. it is quite clear that this proposal is aimed at a more efficient and effective police academy. i object to the widening of the target group. the extension of the target group to all law enforcement services is disproportionate to the principles of proportionality and subsidiarity. the december 2003 evaluation found that there was no need to extend the target group to lower-level police officers and other law enforcement services. it is a very serious interference with national autonomy and will also have to make concessions in terms of content. if, as the rapporteur rightly points out, the efficient use of resources is an objective of the commission's proposal, i am surprised that brussels has not been chosen as the location. my working visits to european agencies have consistently shown that the cost of staff based outside brussels is significantly higher than that of staff based in brussels. in particular, travel costs between the place of establishment and brussels by commission officials constitute a major cost item. president, i'm going to circle back. i support mr demetriou's report and hope that the commission will give serious consideration to parliament's amendments in the directive in this consultation procedure. this will help to give a better shape to the restoration of police authority.

the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action to combat poverty and to ensure that the world's poorest people are not left behind. and when you look at africa, you have to say that there is a dramatic situation on all of these points, because instead of moving closer to those goals on the way to 2015, we are moving away from them. if this keeps up, we won't be there in a hundred years. and that paints a picture of the failure of world leadership, especially where africa is concerned. the recent report by geoffrey sachs makes it clear that this should not be necessary. with enough money, good prioritization and enough campaign focus, for example, malaria can be eradicated, children can go to school, girls can lead, and poverty can be cut in half. in september - the commissioner has already said this - the un summit can deal with millennium issues. that is why it is of course of the utmost importance that what we agree there is translated by the commission and the council into european objectives for the eu. we can do this, as the commissioner said, by means of financing at the level of the member states, but also by giving sufficient priority here in parliament and in the council to multiannual financing and to make it stick within the percentage for social spending of the millennium development goals, including especially the 20% for basic education and basic health care. the current figures - 2% for education, 6% for health, also in the edf figures - are alarming. i call on the commission and the council to take up these demands seriously, together with the european parliament, so that we can avoid confrontation and work together to put an end to it. we are prepared to give the commission room for manoeuvre only if there are firm agreements. first of all, these agreements must be clearly and properly defined, because the current 2% and 6% are really not enough. we'd have to wait another hundred years, and we don't want to!
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat malaria, including the introduction of a new vaccine, the introduction of a new vaccine and the introduction of a new vaccine. it is, of course, disastrous in itself to think that we have both the knowledge and the means and that this situation is still going on. the medicine is available and the disease is curable. simple, impregnated bed nets are the most effective and inexpensive way to prevent infection. but the reality is that only 2% of children in africa are covered by these nets. and the core is the bottlenecks in organization and distribution. geoffrey sachs has once again put his nose on the facts. the means are there, now the political will. he says "quick win", but i say, "quick win". that's only going to be structural if you're really campaigning and setting clear priorities. we've shown before, around the world, that we can. in polio, for example, we've managed to eradicate that disease by about 99 percent. the important thing now is not to have competition between different poverty-related diseases, but to tackle together the totality of basic health care in africa. of course, as the commissioner said, the most vulnerable areas, where there are no basic services and no regional structures, and where there is poor hygiene, are the first priority. we must not forget that 50% of africa's population lives in urban areas, so the problem is not only rural, it certainly occurs in the city, and the city needs another form of organization. it is always local organisers, it is always those basic health services, it is always those bottlenecks that cause problems in those systems and i hope that the commissioner is able to change that with his strategy. those local people will be happy to cooperate, mr. chairman, because those are the people whose parents, of course, have children who are going to die of malaria. they know how serious this is and they will certainly give their full cooperation.

president. - tomorrow this house will be voting on the accession of romania and bulgaria to the european union. but there are still 20 months between now and the date of accession now planned, and a president, bulgaria and romania were not immediately admitted to the 2004 enlargement because their negotiations were behind schedule. bulgaria is noted for its eagerness to build major roads and airports without regard for human health, nature, the environment and the rules of the european union. moreover, despite eu-funded projects, the large roma population is far from being in a position to do the same. poverty and unemployment are high. there is a government in power that relies on the temporary popularity of one person. the man who would have liked to be king, but lacks a large, organized, cohesive following. this strange situation will probably end after the elections on 25 june. bulgaria scores no worse than some states that were already admitted in 2004, so criticism cannot be a good reason to wait longer than 2007. the accession of romania is much more controversial. the independence of the press, the judiciary and non-governmental organisations had not been guaranteed in recent years. although romania has applied to join the european union, i have the impression that it is more inclined to look to the united states and turkey. it is so bad that the initial enthusiasm in the neighbouring country of moldova to return to romania has completely disappeared. governmental tasks have been neglected, but there is room for a wild-west economy. in an effort to extract minerals cheaply, foreign adventurers are hired to use dangerous chemicals that can seriously pollute soil, groundwater, and river waters. in neighboring hungary, which has already been hit by toxic river water from romania, there is a fearful look ahead to the gold mining project in rosia montana in romania's alba province, where cyanide will be used. romania has also signed a treaty with the united states to oppose the international criminal court in the hague. romania is the showcase of the 'new europe' of us secretary of defence rumsfeld. of all the countries admitted so far, romania has the worst score. that in itself could be a reason to reject romania's membership at this time. my group, however, defends the right of rapid accession of european countries with a low standard of living, provided they quickly comply with human rights, democratic and environmental requirements. the admission of romania, of course, makes it more difficult for turkey to be pressured to become a decent democratic country before it is admitted to the european union. it also eliminates any serious argument for further delaying the negotiations with the former yugoslav republics of croatia and macedonia, which in recent years have been hoping to join the eu at the same time as romania. we do not know how romania will develop under a new government in the near future and whether a solution to the existing problems is in sight. it is therefore right that the option of postponement should remain as a means of pressure. that should be the right not only of the council but also of this parliament.

the european council has stated that the services directive should be given a more social face. the purpose of the directive is to ensure that the conditions of employment of workers in member states with less stringent working conditions are not abused, nor to prevent unfair competition with regard to service providers in member states with more stringent working conditions. the lack of compliance with the applicable labour law will lead to undesirable situations and the concern for this has given rise to concern among citizens. the directive takes account of the fact that service providers are bound by the local labour law under european rules. the only condition is that the employment law be effectively enforced, otherwise there is indeed abuse and unfair competition. the proposal for a directive has not so far provided sufficient guarantees for this. the proposal does not place the task of enforcement on the country of destination but on the country of origin, and this creates an unworkable situation. it is not right to demand that polish authorities, for example, check whether polish workers in the netherlands are working in accordance with dutch labour standards. we need a different approach to achieve a workable system of enforcement of working conditions in all member states. the country of origin will have to declare at least that its resident will become active in the country of destination. the exchange of information is therefore a first step towards the enforcement of labour law. i assume that the commission will incorporate this obligation to notify in the directive. eight environmental organizations have rightly pointed out the consequences of this directive for nature, the environment and health. the concept of integration in the eu treaty with the aim of sustainable development has apparently been forgotten for a while. the european council has not made it clear what direction it wants to take and how the directive can be given a more social and ecological face. that was the intention of the lisbon process. the fact remains that the european parliament was the driving force behind the legislative process and not the council. however, the european council has brought the services directive to the agenda because of the specific interests of individual member states. that is what is shaking the institutional framework. it seems that the council is now in a position to give this social aspect a greater prominence, given the debate on the european constitution in france, although there are more pertinent arguments against this constitution.
the satisfaction of the summit is, in my view, totally misplaced. it was a shameful spectacle, a cow-trading of political interests and those same politicians who then cry hot crocodile tears over the cynicism of the citizen. i think that the credibility of the european union has been damaged once again. this summit should have equipped the european economy for the 21st century and should have led to greater budgetary discipline and a free market in services, but instead we are stuck with the old recipe of debt and protectionism. to call that social is a disgrace. it is said that the stability pact has been saved, but i see vague wording, escape clauses and accounting tricks, and the way in which the political leaders are handling the services directive is pure demagogy. i would like to take as an example the new countries which are not suffering from our complacency but are much more dynamic and are carrying out reforms. finally, mr president, i hope that after the various referendums and elections, long-term vision, courage, a sense of responsibility and political leadership will finally prevail.
the advantage of speaking at the end of a debate is that it can be taken up on a number of issues that have been raised and i want to do so on the basis of three misunderstandings that dominate the debate. the first misunderstanding concerns the reform of the stability and growth pact. this was not just a matter of trade in cows between member states which found it difficult to meet the standards of the pact. there was a discussion on economic policy and on the economic rationality of the pact in the context of macroeconomic policy. this may not have been very clear in the way it was discussed in the media, but it was certainly taken into account by the luxembourg presidency. i hope that the european commission will be able to use the reform of the pact as an instrument for an improved european macroeconomic policy from this more macroeconomic point of view. the second point concerns the integrated treatment of the economic and employment guidelines and the microeconomic policy, as also presented by the commission yesterday. of course, all this fits within the framework of the lisbon strategy and my group has called for streamlining these processes and an integrated approach. we must be careful, however, that there is no hierarchy of procedures and that no particular council formation, commissioner, specialised committee or integrated structure in parliament is given prominence. we do not want a supercommissioner, a supercouncil or a specialised committee which only deals with these matters. a third point concerns the services directive. i would like to
