in writing. today i did not approve romania's application for eu membership. i'm sorry, i have to make a determination. the new government is showing enormous political commitment and has made clear in a short time what it stands for. that's good news. the commission's efforts have yet to be translated into concrete actions with measurable effects. it's the result that counts. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment in the event of accidents. and yet this house has been asked to give its opinion no less than 20 months before accession. this has never been shown before and the logic of it eludes me completely. it is important that the european institutions stick to the agreements they have made, especially at a time when the union's support is very limited and its legitimacy is fragile. i am not against romania's accession. romania is europe and romania is joining the european union. i only want the accession to take place on the basis of what we have agreed with each other before.
in writing. romania is still having great difficulty in being ready in time for eu accession in 2007, thanks to the lack of progress under the previous socialist government. the situation in the area of corruption and administrative and legal stability is of particular concern. it seems to me quite possible that in due course a year's delay will have to be made. the european parliament has been put in the impossible position of giving an opinion 20 months before accession. however, at the moment we see that the new government is on track with the implementation of the required reforms, and that the european commission, as recently in the case of croatia, is taking a strict line in assessing candidate members. i therefore give both the romanian government and the european commission the benefit of the doubt. i hope that progress will continue as it is now, because only then will i be able to continue to support romania's accession in 2007.
in writing. i have thought of voting against further enlargement, not because of the question of whether romania is ready for accession, but because in my opinion the eu itself is not ready for further enlargement. this is evident from the current deadlock in decision-making and the mistrust of several 'old' member states towards 'new' member states on issues such as the services directive. this does not provide new member states with sufficient opportunities to take full advantage of the internal market. unchecked expansion without deepening will lead to administrative impotence and possibly even to an implosion. this is not to the advantage of anyone, especially not of the applicant countries, which have had to make enormous efforts in recent years to meet the criteria for admission. i express the fervent hope that, with the acceptance of the constitutional treaty, the administrative efficiency and economic absorption will increase to such an extent that it is justified for both the eu and the new candidate countries to work together on further enlargement. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
in writing. i voted with great hesitation for the accession of bulgaria and romania. i have serious doubts because the reports of corruption in romania in particular do not reassure me about the suitability of this country for membership of the european union. the way in which the council decided to finance the accession of these two countries, recognising the budgetary powers of the european parliament only at the last moment, also undermines the need to decide on the accession of these countries 20 months in advance. the geopolitical advantages of the accession of these countries are decisive for me. the european union has also been promoting membership for many years and has made major changes in legislation. it is uncertain what negative reactions a refusal to join the european union would evoke. the accession treaties also contain safeguard clauses which may delay accession. all of this led me to a cautious "yes".
for the greens/efa group it has been stressed by several speakers in this debate and in various reports and statements by the commission and the council. the countries of the western balkans are future members of the european union. but in today's social climate, that's easier said than done. we heard it this morning in the debate on romania and mr swoboda has already referred to it: there's a certain expansion fatigue. i am very much afraid that this exhaustion from enlargement will have repercussions, above all, on our balkan policy. i am still convinced that - however unpopular it may be, and i emphasize it again here - the european union needs a balkan strategy. a report was published yesterday by the international commission on the balkans, which has a lot of interesting and knowledgeable people in it. i quote from that report. it's about the western balkans. "the wars may be over, but the smell of violence still hangs in the air". if you travel around the balkans, in any country, that's the situation, and that means that the eu simply cannot afford to think: we're not in a good place right now, so let's just leave those countries alone for a while. in this new strategy, in this strategy for the balkans, which is geared to membership, two factors are important. firstly - and this is very obvious, i repeat it again - the social and economic development of the region is at present disastrous and this is the main source of instability. with high unemployment rates, it is virtually impossible for young people leaving school there to build a future on the ground. this leads to more crime, insecurity and instability, and we should not want that. if the european union is good at anything, it is in promoting economic ties between these countries and between them and the european union. the second element which we must not abandon is the adherence to the basic conditions of human rights and minority rights. although we were divided on this as balkan spokesmen on croatia, i think it has now been proved that the council's insistence on cooperation with the hague tribunal as a condition for the opening of negotiations has positive effects both in bosnia-herzegovina and in serbia. i am glad and i congratulate the council on its firmness in its cooperation with the court. now for the matter that concerns us all: the kosovo. i have just been there and i am sure that the report on the standards and what has happened so far will come naturally. i really believe that this debate on the status will take place. in that debate europe must take the lead, whether it likes it or not, because we are at the root of it. we have the reward for serbia and kosovo in our own hands. the debate on kosovo's independence must be conducted under a number of conditions which are already becoming clear: we are not going to be able to do that, because we are not going to be able to do that. but, ladies and gentlemen, to do nothing and look the other way, to pretend that it is too difficult for europe to intervene in the balkans now, that is an approach we cannot afford. doing nothing leads to an untenable and dangerous situation. let us not do that to ourselves and to the countries of the balkans!
in the 1990s it was thought that we could solve the problems in the former yugoslavia by establishing protectorates, sending in external administrators and military personnel and forcing refugees to return to areas where a different ethnic majority had emerged. the american model leads to stagnation. it forces the people there back into the role of immature children. they're gonna sit back and wait for those foreign meddlers to leave. the alternative to this stagnation is to find a peaceful and democratic way from below by taking seriously how the people in, for example, kosovo, montenegro, the separate entities in bosnia or the two large linguistic areas in macedonia themselves see their future and how they want to call themselves. states and groups of people who faced each other in the 1990s continue to reject domination by their neighbours, but they are willing to cooperate with open borders. a rapid start of the negotiations on accession to the european union, starting with croatia and macedonia, can help to achieve this. europe must now seek real solutions, with all those concerned, without prejudice.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. clear language from eufor, the european union military mission in that country. almost ten years after dayton, the former warring parties continue the ethnic struggle unabated in the political arena. the ad hoc delegation of this parliament to sarajevo and mostar last week, for example, was given a rather less than encouraging but realistic picture of the current situation in bosnia-herzegovina. this underlines once again the usefulness of the eufor mission on the ground. the religious communities in bosnia and herzegovina should be expected to make a conciliatory contribution. representatives of the council, the commission and eufor said otherwise. i would urge the council and the commission to continue to call on the ecclesiastical authorities and the reis al-ulema to bear their great responsibility in this matter. the recent arrest of bosniaks in chechnya is a clear sign. it illustrates the danger of religious extremism which eufor sources have called our attention to. a moving part of the ad hoc delegation's visit to sarajevo was a tour of minefields. the council and the commission, only the clearing of these killing fields will make the transition to a peaceful existence possible. so please invest extra in the future of bosnia and herzegovina in this way!
today we are discussing the report on the external action of the european union in 2003. i am particularly pleased that the rapporteur, mr brok, has stressed that the involvement of the european parliament should not be limited to an after-the-fact discussion. i therefore take the liberty of dealing with some of the issues that have been raised in the past year and looking ahead. the development of the european security strategy, which is also discussed here, will play an important role in this. the kuhne report - and i commend the rapporteur - gives an excellent assessment of the practical effects of this security strategy so far. i would ask the commission, the council and of course the high representative to take these recommendations very seriously. i would like to make two points. firstly, there is the military dimension. europe can only play a central role if we are also willing and able to take military action if necessary. preventing conflict escalation to the point where military intervention is necessary is and remains the first priority, while the actual use of military means should continue to emphasise interaction with other instruments: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the programme to include humanitarian intervention, the rule of law, institution building, democratisation and economic development. the first major european military mission, the althea mission in bosnia-herzegovina, is already gaining experience in combining civilian and military capabilities in this way. the development of the european defence leg has a political aspect as well as a material one. the european union must develop a political framework which will enable us to use military means when deemed necessary. the second question concerns the intersections between internal and external security, which are particularly evident in the fight against terrorism. the coherence between these two is not sufficiently developed in european policy at present. the council has rightly asked the high representative to make proposals in this area and i call on him to involve parliament in this. the brok report and the kuhne report call for attention to be paid to this. the implementation of the common foreign and security policy is unfortunately not always as common as i would like. nevertheless, my evaluation of the past few years is not so negative. the european union has played an important role in the upheaval in ukraine. the prompt reaction of the presidency and the good coordination between parliament and the council, as well as the action of the high representative and the heads of state of poland and lithuania, contributed greatly to the positive outcome of that political crisis. the involvement of france, germany and the united kingdom within the eu in the conflict over the iranian nuclear programme is also, in my view, a good example of how things can be dealt with in a european manner. the outcome is uncertain, but we believe that the diplomatic approach, the model used, is of great importance. the principles of the european common foreign and security policy, multilateralism, preventive engagement, a broad approach centred on democracy and social and economic development, conflict resolution and the cessation of the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction are at the heart of our full support. but we cannot stress enough that solidarity is a prerequisite, because it is only by coming together that europe can assert its political weight.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's energy policy is not undermined by the current crisis. the need to take account of the end of the life cycle in the design of new cars arose in the early 1990s, among others, in the netherlands, when bilateral agreements were concluded between manufacturers and the public authorities. european manufacturers have gained a great deal of experience in recycling. the manufacturers estimate the cost of the recycling obligation at an average of eur 30 per car, but the industry will recover this cost quickly. the price of raw materials is rising exponentially, mainly due to the huge demand from china. rising raw material costs make recycling especially rewarding. this environmental innovation also gives europe a head start on its competitors, since both japan and the united states lack regulations on recycling. our group can agree with the amended proposal and would like to thank the rapporteur. i also want to apologize to the shadow reporter who couldn't be here tonight. i would like to take this opportunity, mr president, to look at the automobile industry in a broader way today. the high level group on cars 21 was set up earlier this year. the high level group is responsible for formulating recommendations for the european automotive industry. this is mainly about competitiveness and employment, but also about safety and environmental performance. let me start by saying that i am a strong supporter of the sectoral approach. the lisbon process must be put into practice within sectors. the european commission is moving in the right direction with the cars 21 initiative, but i fear that the creation of the high level group will turn out to be nothing more than a deregulation operation for the automobile industry, the abolition of annoying environmental and social protection rules under the guise of improving competitiveness. i would therefore urge you to look further ahead and to show more ambition, because the environment and competitiveness are not contradictory, but rather mutually reinforcing. an industry that continually achieves better environmental performance improves its global competitiveness. i believe that environmentally friendly cars are essential to a viable european car industry. that's why i took the initiative to create a low level group on cars. in the spirit of the lisbon objective, the low level group will focus on employment, competitiveness and a cleaner environment. i will be working with interested colleagues to draw up a number of recommendations which i will present to the commissioners responsible and to the high level group in july. what is the future of the european automobile industry? the car industry has made great progress in terms of environmental and safety, but is this the right approach for the future? i think we in europe should think together about the car of the future. at least that car is lighter, smaller, more fuel efficient and more efficient. this will also be the basis of a modern, competitive and environmentally friendly automotive industry. rising raw material prices are making recycling and reuse of materials worthwhile, and technological progress in this area can make a major contribution to a good competitive position for the european automotive industry.
in writing. since the early 1990s, there has been a problem with the name of a country in the north of greece. three northern greek provinces have names with macedonia in them, and that name plays a part in the ancient history of greece as well. in the neighboring country, the resistance movement against the ottoman empire also used the name macedonia before 1912, and that also became the name of the southernmost state of yugoslavia between 1945 and 1991. it would be obvious that the self-chosen constitutional name 'republic of macedonia' would be generally applied. each state chooses its own name, without any interference from other states or international bodies. nevertheless, outside attempts are still being made to impose a different name, in which only the last letter indicates the real name. if we allow this, luxembourg should not be allowed to bear its own name, because the south-eastern belgian province bears the same name. now, not only the us, russia and china, but also eu member slovenia and future member bulgaria have recognized macedonia under its real name. the continued use of another name by the eu is unfair and discriminatory to a likely future member state.
president. - as part of the division of the colossal british colonial empire in south asia on the basis of a regionally dominant religion, the eastern part of the province of bengal was assigned to the muslim state of pakistan in 1947. the whole world sympathized with the fact that this overpopulated and constantly water-scarce area broke away from pakistan in 1971, the distant country to the west that had no interest in helping to solve the problems in its eastern flank. the new state could have made a good start by building sea walls and polishing inhabited areas that flooded from the sea during storm surges, for example, following the dutch example. that would give more room for the residents and their food supply. instead, we saw within bangladesh an intensified struggle between two parties based on family clans fighting a feud and each denying the other's right to exist. in addition, there has long been a kind of intolerant religious fanaticism that, in that form, was virtually non-existent in other parts of the islamic world until twenty years ago. that fanaticism leaves no room for religious minorities like ahmadiyya muslims and hindus. intimidation and violent attacks by supporters of the government are not punished. paramilitary groups that claim to fight crime torture and kill people. bangladesh is in danger of falling into a spiral of self-destruction. a broadly supported statement against intimidation, violence, torture, impunity and economic stagnation will not solve the problem, but it is a starting point for determining how best we can deal with this unfortunate country from europe in the coming period. that at least means solidarity with the victims.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budgetary authority is able to meet its obligations. we wish the leaders of this country and its people god's blessing for the new year. but just as we can do better, we have some suggestions for good intentions for bangladesh. unfortunately, although the constitution has declared islam the state religion since 1998, it gives other religions the right to profess, practice and propagate their faith. strange, though, that the "right of conversion" is not protected by the constitution. would it not be a good idea to better regulate freedom of religion in the constitution and to abolish the state religion again? i would like to illustrate the need for this, mr president, by giving a practical example. on monday, april 4, reliable sources confirmed that on march 8 this year, the christian dulal sarkar was murdered. he was an evangelist of the bangladesh free baptist church in jalalpur village in the south-western district of khulna. this man was just doing his job and did nothing against the constitution. on his way home, he was attacked and beheaded by ten armed muslim extremists. these muslim fundamentalists are said to have links with the jamaat-e-islami, a political party which is currently part of the governing coalition. dulal sarkar is survived by his mother, wife and five children, and his wife and family are currently fleeing from house to house to escape reprisals by muslim extremists. mr president, the motion for a resolution which we have co-signed is therefore extremely clear. we are very concerned about the growth of muslim fundamentalism with its paramilitary groups and the abuse of power by fundamentalist muslim parties. i am therefore absolutely opposed to the weakening amendments tabled to this clear motion for a resolution. i call on the council and the commission to work together with the state department mentioned in paragraph 2 to combat these manifestations of muslim fundamentalism. this can be done by linking consequences to violations of democratic principles in cooperation agreements. all this for the sake of all bengali citizens.
for people who have to leave their country of origin because of oppression, war, disasters and poverty, the mediterranean is an important gateway to europe. if europe fails to make a significant contribution to alleviating the problems in asia and africa, more and more people will risk entering europe in wrecked boats and abandoned ships. this is of course difficult for europe and especially for the countries bordering the mediterranean. that nuisance does not justify the violation of human rights. these refugees also have the right to an individual assessment of their motives and to a proper observance of the 1951 geneva convention. that is not the case for the hundreds of refugees who have been sent to libya from the italian island of lampedusa in recent months, because that country does not recognise the geneva convention, and italy's agreements with libya are not public. between the time of arrival in europe and any return to the country of origin, european law should apply instead of the arbitrariness of a country with an opaque administration. this can be done by adopting the two amendments by the liberals, number 2 and number 3, from the european united left, to make it clear that these refugees have the same rights as other refugees and cannot simply be deported to libya without any european responsibility.

member of the commission. members of the house, ladies and gentlemen. the commission thanks the european parliament for putting this question on its agenda. recognises the important role that parliament plays in protecting the rights of minorities, including the roma. this role is now strengthened by the presence of roma members in parliament. the european union is founded on fundamental rights, including the principle of non-discrimination. the roma must be able to participate fully in economic, social and political life. we must therefore work together to overcome centuries of prejudice, discrimination and social exclusion against roma. it is a huge task, requiring coordinated action on several fronts, particularly in access to education, employment, housing, as well as social services. the answer to these questions lies primarily with the national authorities, as well as with the regional and local authorities where roma are most represented. civil society, as well as the roma themselves, must play an important role. the situation of the roma is a european issue that requires a union response. this response can take many forms. the commission is actively monitoring the situation of roma in the european union as well as in the candidate countries. in preparing for accession, the situation of roma and other minorities shall be assessed within the framework of political criteria. the european centre in vienna has devoted particular attention to this issue in its research on the phenomena of racism and xenophobia in the european union. the union has established a binding legal framework to combat discrimination on grounds of racial or ethnic origin. the community legislation in force prohibits direct and indirect discrimination in access to employment, education, social protection, property and services. the commission ensures that these laws are transposed into national law and also supports awareness-raising and educational measures, including specific actions for organisations working with roma. the european union provides substantial financial support for efforts to integrate roma. during the preparation for accession, more than eur 100 million was allocated to projects for roma from the fare programme. the commission actively assists national authorities in using the structural funds to promote the inclusion of roma as well as other disadvantaged groups. a few other programs the community shall promote the exchange of experience and specific projects in the fields of employment, education, training and research within the union and in certain third countries. the commission shall endeavour to ensure that the situation of roma is taken into account in all union programmes and policies that can play a role in this area. to this end, a group has been set up, comprising representatives of all the directorates-general concerned. the group aims to exchange best practice, coordinate policies and engage in dialogue with external participants. the last meeting of the group was used to exchange information with one of the roma meps. the commission also works with other international organisations working for roma inclusion, maintaining regular contacts with the council of europe, the osce, the world bank and other major actors.in the lisbon strategy, the european union set itself ambitious targets for growth in job creation, social cohesion and sustainable development. the social exclusion of a significant part of our fellow citizens on the grounds of their membership of the roma community threatens our ability to achieve these objectives. we have a common task to fight against the social exclusion of the roma in the name of fundamental rights, we have a task to fight in the name of social cohesion and socioeconomic development of the union. the commission thanks the european parliament for its support and efforts in this area.
on behalf of the verts/ale group .– mr president, with the enlargement of the european union, the number of roma citizens has increased from 7 million to 9 million. since enlargement is an ongoing process, in future the eu will host up to 12 or 15 million roma. for thousands of years the roma have been living in diaspora, but being united in the eu they become our fellow citizens. the european union means an end to years of war and the beginning of cooperation. but how can we cooperate when people are excluded? mostly, the roma issue is connected with unemployment and a lack of education, healthcare and housing. however, the core issue is racism against roma, more specifically anti-gypsyism or roma-phobia. can we accept a situation where millions of our fellow citizens lack basic human rights and suffer from police harassment, where thousands of school children remain illiterate and the unemployment rate exceeds 70%, where attempts are made at ethnic cleansing by poisoning people and trafficking in women and children, including in western eu countries, which, contrary to the 1951 geneva convention on refugees, also expel roma refugees? the roma also face a lack of political participation, as a result of which there are not enough roma representatives to draw attention to their issues. today’s practice is not only a human tragedy: the roma have been turned into a political tool. what happened to the millions of dollars and euros donated by the us and the eu to eastern european countries for roma projects? structures set up for the purpose of helping the cause of the roma often use this cause as a pretext. in reality most of the money is spent on salaries, trips and on buying citizens, administrators and politicians. this process is defined as corruption, but actually it is manipulation. there are people in this world who do not want a dynamic europe; they practice a policy of divide and rule by maintaining poverty and establishing control over eastern european countries. the roma, europe’s largest and most vulnerable minority, are, against their will, victims and participants in this global game. it is therefore also necessary from a purely political point of view to reinforce the roma population. their political participation in elections is important. we have to target roma as voters and as candidates at all political levels. the roma-to-roma approach, as developed by the osce, is another effective way of consolidating the roma’s position. all european organisations should start hiring roma for roma and non­roma related jobs – i have a roma assistant. anti­gypsyism is a shame and a threat to the eu.
the rapporteur. i would like to begin by apologizing to my dutch colleagues for not speaking in dutch but in english, because that is the language of the financial markets. it also has the advantage of being able to address the commissioner in a language he understands. madam president, when the commissioner's predecessor mr bolkestein started his term, he met with a well-prepared, ambitious set of proposals for the internal market for capital. he presented them in 1999 as the financial services action plan fsap. the commissioner, i have the impression that you are less fortunate with the heritage concerning the internal market for services that mr bolkestein left for you when you came here to brussels, and which bears his name, or perhaps an ominous nickname. but that is not our subject for today; we will have plenty of time to discuss this further. i would like to praise mr bolkestein for having diligently steered the fsap through during his term in office. the commissioner, i am very much looking forward to the follow-up plan in the green paper that you are to present next week. in the report before you and in the debates we had in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, we take a positive view of the way the fsap has been dealt with, but of course we are aware that many of the intentions laid down in the rules still have to be proved in practice. major attention should be focused on the implementation and enforcement of the almost 40 sets of rules that have been adopted. unfortunately, there were some misunderstandings about my report in the form in which it has been presented to the plenary today. although a broad consensus was reached in committee and we had good compromises, some imperfections slipped in during the committee vote. since they concern the main thrust of the report, i would like to discuss them first. the most annoying mistake is paragraph 2 of the text before the house today. it suggests that we should go along with the plea of some lobbyists and journalists that brussels legislators should commit themselves to a regulatory pause. but that's really not my cup of tea. this paragraph 2 absolutely does not fit into a report that points to so many developments in a dynamic financial market that may pose serious systemic risks and endanger a safe and efficient internal market for financial services. therefore, i am clearly against tying our hands with a moratorium on legislation. we should stay alert and the commission should be very proactive. of course, this new legislation should be thoroughly assessed as to its necessity, its costs and benefits and its wider impact, but less regulation is not always better regulation. let me turn to some other elements of the report, such as the lamfalussy process. with the well-known disclaimers that you are used to from the european parliament, the report is quite positive about the lamfalussy process. i tend to see the level three committees of regulators in particular as allies for the public interest and the protection of consumers and expert users. i note that they are increasingly aware of their important role in harmonising the technical rules and the supervisory practices between regulators in different member states. the himalaya report presented by cesr asks for an improved toolbox. we say in the report before the house today that this is a good starting point for further debate. some important issues have to be resolved in the coming period. then there is european-level supervision. in the report we speak positively about a move towards consolidating supervisors. the industry wants to reduce the need to comply with many different supervisors, but on the other hand i can understand the hesitation of regulatory authorities in host situations to give up their competences and to rely completely on foreign supervisors. that's why i insist on taking a further step. seeing the growth in cross-border activity and the concentration of large players in the financial markets, it is time for integrated supervision at european level, possibly via a two-tier system with european-level supervision for large cross-border players without prejudice to the level playing field between cross-border and local players. not everyone agrees with me on this, but at least in the report we agree that this should be an option that the commission should also consider further. then there is the issue of consumer protection and involvement. here we stress that the protection of consumers and the guaranteed availability of basic financial services to all consumers should remain a basic condition. i do not think that we should aim at complete harmonisation in relation to retail financial products and service provision, because consumer protection is too different in the various member states. in the report i propose looking at the option of a 26th regime for pan-european financial products, such as mortgages or insurance products. they may meet the needs of internationally mobile people to work with the providers they are used to in another member state. mrs berès, the chairwoman of our committee, will speak on the issue of better cooperation in a global setting.
i would like to begin by thanking mrs christa klaß for the good cooperation i have had with her. the committee on agriculture has made two main points. the essential point is, of course - as the previous speakers have said - that the committee on agriculture is of course also in favour of clean water. we must do something about this and we can therefore in principle support the proposals in this groundwater directive. we believe, however, that it is essential to introduce different measurement methods in europe which reflect the differences in climate and which also reflect the differences in soil types. the situation on clay soils is different from that on peat soils and the situation on peat soils is different from that on sand soils, and this can also vary from finland to the boot of italy. that will have to be taken into account when we measure the 50 milligrams. the second point the committee on agriculture wanted to take into account is the existing legislation. as far as nitrates are concerned, there are likely to be two directives in the near future: the commission has already adopted a proposal for a directive on nitrates. for example, if a farmer in europe follows the groundwater directive and he is below 50 milligrams, with 30 milligrams, for example, then he is still bound by the nitrates directive, and it may be that a farmer with 3 cows per hectare does not comply with the requirements of the nitrates directive but does comply with those of the groundwater directive, because if we measure that nitrate, he is below 50 milligrams. i do not think it makes sense that we should tell that farmer that he should reduce his cattle units per hectare. it's either one or the other. the committee on agriculture has therefore proposed that the need for the nitrates directive be reviewed in 2008. the two amendments on this subject were rejected by the committee on the environment, the liberal group has tabled them again and i would strongly recommend them to you. thirdly - and i am speaking for the liberal group at this last minute, mr president - it is all right to have clean water, but what we have to consider is how much it costs. what are the socioeconomic consequences if we introduce certain legislation? the liberal group has tabled a number of amendments on this point too. i would naturally like to see them adopted. it seems to me absolutely logical that if we introduce directives that does not come at the expense of everything. we need a timetable where everything is realistic and affordable.
president. - in the eleven years that i have been in this parliament, i have not often seen the rapporteur himself present a minority position on a report. i think it is rather strange that mr klaß has done this now. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. i can only emphasize, mr president, that the amendment by the committee on the environment is a positive one for me. groundwater is essentially a national issue. in the treatment of the water framework directive, it was recognised that groundwater should be given a separate treatment. i believe that, from the point of view of subsidiarity, there can be no legal basis for this subsidiary directive other than article 175 (1). groundwater is a natural resource in danger and deserves attention through the exchange of good practices and the promotion of better protection. however, there is no place for rigid european-wide standards. we already know that it is particularly difficult to measure objectively the content of a substance in groundwater for a region, and the difficulty is many times greater for a member state. for example, the chloride concentration can vary from 20 mg/l to more than 10 000 mg/l within a distance of 10 km. finally, mr president, i would like to draw the attention of members to the problem of the discharge ban. amendment no 43 is crucial to this. the way in which drinking water is extracted in some member states, namely by infiltration of surface water, makes it extremely important that no absolute discharge ban is introduced. i therefore ask you to vote against amendment no 43. amendment no 103 by the alde group is acceptable. i am very much looking forward to the vote, mr president, but i cannot guarantee that i will be able to agree to the result. that depends on which amendments are adopted by the members.
so, mr president, there seem to be other lists in circulation, but that maría esther herranz garcía has not taken the trouble to warn me... i hope you don't count that. i would like to thank our rapporteur, mrs christa klass, for her report. why am i saying thank you? because i know that she had a different opinion on a number of points, but she thought constructively with us to the end and was also willing to support positions that were important to members from other member states. this directive is important because it is about achieving an ecological balance. groundwater is also important for agriculture, industry and drinking water. in the water framework directive we have adopted a river basin-based approach. that also means something for the assessment of the groundwater directive. this means that this directive cannot contain uniform standards for certain substances. it does mean that you have to come up with uniform measurement methods and so on. another point: i have tabled an amendment to item 110. that amendment concerns indirect discharges, article 6 of this directive. what did i mean by that? i come from a province where we have floods on a regular basis. we're making room for the rivers. that means we want to widen that river and we're going to take some of the sediment out and use that for the accumulation. if we give a majority to this water framework directive and especially to the amendment tabled by our green group, the compromise amendment on paragraph 94, that would mean that we would have to pack everything that comes from that river, that we put aside for the safety of that province, of our netherlands, that we would have to wrap it in foil and that would cost 100 million euros just for the small region where i live. that can't be the point, so i'm asking you: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.
mr president, today we are faced with a major political challenge. we have an obligation to prevent pollution and the depletion of natural resources. in the netherlands, 60% of drinking water comes from groundwater, which is reason enough to prevent pollution as much as possible. we need to set high standards for the quality of our groundwater. but how sharp should the limits be? should we impose it in europe or leave it to the member states? in the netherlands there is a certain amount of concern about european rules. the impact of european rules is often felt by farmers, builders and businesses. air quality, fertiliser, nature protection, all directives with far-reaching implications and difficult to explain to someone whose building project is blocked by abstract european rules. "there you have that annoying europe again". the labour party's position is clear: we do not want common limits in groundwater. harmonised limit values are not useful because the chemical composition of groundwater varies widely between member states due to natural conditions. excessively strict limits may lead to the prohibition of human activities in certain areas. the pvda does not want the netherlands to be closed down, and we do not see political support for unnecessarily intrusive rules. that is why we are against amendments nos 111 and 112. but rest assured, the labour party is as green as ever. we do stand behind a powerful common standard of conduct. prevent or reduce the introduction of pollutants into groundwater. this means that any contamination of groundwater with a number of well-defined substances is unacceptable. we also want to see a serious effort made to implement the polluter pays principle. my amendment to convert the cost of pollution by weed killers into a levy has found broad support. and that's good. because many municipalities find manual weeding too expensive. but the cost of pollution has not been included in the chemical controls so far. if that happens, the use of harmful substances will decrease. finally, i would like to draw attention to a few amendments relating to nitrates and the manure problem. i cannot understand why, at a time when the nitrates directive is being worked on in various member states, a message is being sent that it may be superfluous. we therefore do not support these amendments and we will gladly support amendment no 110 by mr oomen-ruijten.

in writing. there is a great deal of criticism within my gue/ngl group of this proposal because it is selective. this criticism has caused some of my colleagues in the group to vote against it. no one denied that it contains many valid comments on human rights abuses outside the european union, but it is striking that countries such as israel, turkey and the colombian government are spared. on the other hand, there is criticism of cuba, venezuela and the colombian guerrilla movements. in venezuela, where the majority of the media is controlled by the opposition, there is a lack of press freedom. this approach creates the impression that allies can never make mistakes and that others can never do well. both the left and the right have a long tradition of selectivity in their defense of human rights, with a lack of human rights being particularly important as an argument against a political opponent. let us stop doing that and also take seriously the criticism of the mistakes of those for whom we have sympathy instead of trivializing them. if the right wing had not just voted against the improved eu policy in the face of the us isolation of cuba, i would have voted along with scandinavian and german colleagues despite the criticism.
in writing. only 7 of the 25 eu member states have a population larger than the roma population group as a whole in the current and future territory of the eu. they are a stateless people and are everywhere considered a minority, although there are communities in which they are the majority. isolation and discrimination have left many roma as outsiders, with low educational levels that contribute to unemployment and behaviour that easily annoys others. we have a long history of driving these people from one state to another and denying them citizenship rights because their forced relocation can make them re-entry as foreigners. individuals may have the opportunity to integrate and be accepted by the majority, but the group as a whole does not. not only are there problems in some of the new member states that joined in 2004 and in the two countries that join in 2007, but also in the old member states, greece and italy, for example. newcomers are denied housing, education and income, so that these people can only survive by committing criminal acts. that must really improve after the 90% majority for this resolution.

the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. that is one of the things we are talking about in this directive, both in the original commission proposal and, of course, in what is now before us: the council's common position. the bathing water directive dates from 1976 and since then a lot has changed, not only in thinking about the environment, but also about what exactly, at what level and how to regulate. of course, one can ask, as i have done, whether it is still necessary to define at european level the standards to which bathing water and water on the coasts and in lakes in the european union must conform, whether we should establish that at european level and whether european action in this area brings added value. this parliament decided at first reading that this is the case, but, more importantly, the member states have done the same. the member states too, of which you might say: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of bathing water is not compromised. who are we to say that this is not so? why do governments choose the new bathing water directive? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. the old directive was based on monitoring and measurement rather than proactive and prudent management of bathing water. the commission's proposal, i think, was very right to change this. the new directive must be workable, flexible and focused on bathing water management and deregulation, and certainly less administrative. for example, measurements should be more flexible: if the bathing water in a particular location has been clean for years, you should be able to measure less. that is what the commission proposed and that is what parliament has adopted and then the council, so that is absolutely progress. the directive is also popular with the general public, which is not unimportant in this age of referenda. it is one of the most familiar directives among the citizens of europe, and every year they await the new figures that come out. it has also been a successful directive, with only 60% of bathing water a decade ago and 95% now. that's very good. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the quality of water is not compromised. i once took the test and looked at a random place in europe, where a leading member of the commission comes from, namely athens, to see what the bathing water quality is like there. we did some research on the internet this afternoon, but unfortunately we couldn't get any further than the quality of the year 2003 as the most recent data. of course, that does not say whether it is still safe to swim in athens or not. it is quite possible that this information could be found on the website of the greek ministry of health and welfare, but then i would not be able to read it because the information is in greek. that is why parliament says: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. at the moment, the information is lagging behind and is only difficult to find on the internet, apart from the fact that, i hope, most tourists do not take their laptops on holiday or at least do not take them to the beach. the health risk is one in eight according to the standards of the old directive. one in eight swimmers is at risk of getting sick by current standards. these health risks should not be underestimated: these include gastrointestinal disorders, skin, eye and respiratory diseases and inflammation of the external auditory canal. the standards proposed by the commission and the environment committee of the european parliament make one in twenty swimmers sick, perhaps still too many, but still a step forward, whereas the common position of the council is satisfied with one in nine. i understand the governments' position: they want the system to be affordable. i think that the european parliament should also take this into account and i regret that there was no substantial agreement between the council and parliament at second reading. there are a number of very interesting amendments, mr president. as regards the removal of the 'acceptable' category or its adjustment, i believe that the adoption of amendments of this kind would at least put us in a good position as parliament in conciliation to come to a solution with the council that is better for swimmers, better for consumers, that we can say: yes, the quality of bathing water is really improving significantly, as the european commission wanted in its original proposal. on the other hand, we must also find a solution where we can say: yes, it's also making governments better, it's making it more workable, it's deregulating, and it's also affordable, because that's an important point, of course. it is also for this reason that i think we should not bring recreational waters under this directive, but perhaps we should do so in the future. i think that this is a good subject to discuss, but i do not think that this is the right time to bring it up in this way. one last note: i have noticed that the commission has attached a statement to the council's position, which is somewhat grating. you will certainly want to continue to look at values in the future. i think that's very useful, because we have to keep adjusting, of course, in the future. i hope you will do so in accordance with the ordinary procedure and in consultation with this parliament.

the rapporteur. - mr president, as is well known, the terrorist attacks in the usa and spain, as well as the increase in crime in general, have led to the adoption of all kinds of security measures in europe. commissioner barrot just said it. the airports were the first to be affected, and now, of course, the seaports are also affected. european ports are essential links in the transport chain, linking trade and passenger flows by sea and by land. they are also often the hub for the transshipment of dangerous goods for major chemical and petrochemical production centres and are located near cities. terrorist attacks in ports can therefore lead to serious disruptions of transport systems, create a domino effect and cause harm to people in ports and to nearby residents. action was simply a requirement. in addition, it is better to settle this matter ourselves than to be confronted unsolicitedly by american inspection teams, to name but a few. commissioner barrot has just said: firstly, the international ship and port facility security code, or isps code, was converted into eu binding rules by means of a regulation, namely regulation 725. however, this is not all-inclusive. it de facto only regulates the security of the wet part of the port and so in 2004 an additional directive was introduced to regulate the security of the entire port area. that is the directive on the table this morning and which we are going to vote on later today. i would like to focus on two of the key issues which i have advocated as rapporteur. mr barrot has just dealt extensively with a few other matters. the two points i want to discuss relate to ensuring a level playing field in the european union. firstly, the financial basis of the proposal. who's paying what? the differences between some member states are considerable. in the context of a level playing field, i think this is absolutely undesirable. the final agreement therefore calls on the commission to present a study on the distribution of costs between public authorities, port authorities and industry as soon as possible and by 30 june 2006 at the latest. under no circumstances should the additional security measures lead to distortions of competition. secondly, i have been working towards a uniform interpretation and harmonised application of the rules in all member states. it is not possible, of course, that in one member state an extra lock is accepted while in the other member states five guards and six cameras are made compulsory. i'm exaggerating, of course, but still. i have therefore called for a bit of meta-monitoring by the european commission. this was a difficult point in the negotiations, particularly for the council. this is surprising to me, because it is the member states who complain most about the lack of a level playing field, but in the end it is all good. we have found a formulation which is acceptable to all concerned and which gives the commission sufficient resources to carry out the necessary meta-monitoring. i have also tried as rapporteur not to create an extra bureaucratic institution and proposals to do so have been dropped. i am, of course, pleased to inform you that i have reached agreement very recently with my shadow rapporteurs, the council and the commission. this compromise is formed by amendments nos 13 to 75, which include the ppe-de group, the pse group, the greens/efa group and my own group, as well as the council and the commission. if we actually accept this compromise later today, the directive can be completed at first reading. the amendments nos 10, 11 and 12 tabled by the uen group seem to me to be sympathetic, but i would still recommend that you vote against them. they failed to do so at an earlier stage in the committee on transport and tourism. the burden of these amendments has already been covered elsewhere or would lead to an unnecessary christmas tree effect. the agreement now before us is a very workable compromise for all parties involved in the implementation of the measures. a smooth conclusion at first reading is therefore very much desirable. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs, the commission and the luxembourg presidency for their commitment and cooperation. i have appreciated it immensely.
the security of vulnerable places, such as ports, is essential for society, and this applies both to the ship-wall relationship and to the rest of the port. ports are not identical in size, location, character and organisation. the member states are rightly left to determine for themselves the usefulness of these rules. i welcome the fact that the member states are able to implement these rules in the most appropriate way. the uniform rules for all ports would reduce the diversity and increase costs. a second important point is the enforcement of these rules. experience elsewhere teaches that enforcement is easier said than done. limited manpower is not an ingredient for optimal inspection. it is therefore appropriate that the commission should monitor the implementation of this directive with a watchful eye. finally, i should like to thank the rapporteur for the succinct way in which he has managed to bring this dossier to a successful conclusion, by adhering to the main points in consultation with the council.

i would like to thank mr florenz and mr wijkman for having initiated this debate. commissioner, global warming is happening much faster than we thought. we are faced with the dual task of adapting to climate change while at the same time limiting climate change. in the face of this enormous challenge, the outcome of the climate conference in buenos aires was particularly disappointing. it is of course good that the kyoto process was just being kept on track by a seminar. and of course it is good that the eu has got its way and that the seminar in bonn next week will also be looking to the future, but this is a shameful result. we must recognize that the eu has become increasingly isolated. the urgency felt in europe is not felt in other parts of the world. the eu will have to be constructive in bonn, to build a much closer international coalition, and good, productive and substantiated proposals will have to be put on the table. commissioner, i would like to put to you four points which we believe can help to create a constructive international coalition. firstly, we must do much more work on adaptation and aid for adaptation, especially for developing countries. adaptation is not easy, but aid must not be limited to major disaster relief. the change in the way of life is a major one and it requires credible programmes. secondly, the transfer of technology. mr wijkman has already spoken about it. we must abandon our ideological reservations about the clean development mechanism and instead invest fully in technology transfer. we must also carry out further research and development in the field of environmental technology. i would like to return to this in the debate on the seventh framework programme for research. thirdly, we will have to focus much less on the national approach, the objectives per country. we must put more emphasis on sectoral objectives. in the discussions we had as a parliamentary delegation in buenos aires, a sectoral approach emerged as a viable way forward. we will have to sit down with the steel, cement and paper industries. i believe that aviation is a sector which should be given priority. competition in all these sectors is global, so global objectives based on the best available technology are obvious. a sectoral approach is also very much in line with our lisbon strategy, which was revised at the last spring summit. finally, although this is a little more controversial, i would like to draw your attention to the role that the wto can play. the european union should open the discussion on trade measures against countries that do not meet their climate commitment. green trade policy is not directed against developing countries, but against the richer countries, and that can in turn bring support from developing countries. commissioner, we expect the european union to have an ambitious international climate policy and to have the widest possible international support among other countries, industry and citizens. good luck in bonn.

the problems of the global textile industry divide europe into a northern and a southern camp. in short, the arrival of cheaper textile imports is in direct competition with the maintenance of the domestic textile sector. although i understand that commissioner mandelson has to choose between those who support and those who oppose measures against china, i think it is too late to start an inquiry. the commission should have used the past few years to develop a vision of a global textile market without barriers and a policy in this area. i believe that the discussion on the temporary imposition of safeguard measures should cover the period after 2008. otherwise, in three years' time this parliament will be having exactly the same debate. the period until 2008 should be used to start a dialogue with the chinese authorities. these talks have a dual purpose, namely to underline the importance of a stable global textile market and to emphasise china's wto obligations. the wto gives china rights, but also obliges it to address, for example, limited market access in the services sectors of banking and telecommunications. i wish the commissioner much perseverance in his negotiations with the chinese partners.
