president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, many consumers are concerned about their health, cholesterol, blood pressure or are struggling with excess weight. the food industry is playing on this. there's a proliferation of all kinds of claims. this morning i had a sandwich that's good for my gut flora, smeared with butter that lowers my cholesterol, and a little egg with it that's high in omega 3. my soda this afternoon was light. fine, i guess, because i don't know. it is therefore of great importance that this bill requires that the claims made by food manufacturers be scientifically substantiated. we must prevent consumers from being misled. consumers are often willing to pay more for a product that claims to do something for their health. but then the consumer must be able to trust that it is true. deception comes in many forms. article 4 states that health claims may only be made on products meeting a certain profile. and rightly so, because fatty croquettes don't get healthy by adding a little bit of vitamin. 0% yogurt is not good for the line if you've added a whole can of sugar. a sugary lollipop with vitamin a doesn't make a healthy food. that is why article 4 is of great importance. the ppe-de group's argument that article 4 is a problem for smes is not acceptable to me. even a small food company knows what ingredients go into the bread or snacks. my group stands with the consumers and with the benevolent companies. i hope that a majority of this house will follow this line and not abolish article 4. i think it is a good thing that the commission has not yet taken a decision on this. it is clear that the drawing up of these profiles is not easy. but it is also clear that in the united states and in some companies, there is a long history of nutrition profiling. so, liberals, come on, don't keep asking for studies, but make a decision and don't leave the consumers out in the cold. i would like to draw your attention to three other matters. first, health claims for children. we see no reason to ban health claims for children as long as those claims are scientifically substantiated. it is good to do research on good nutrition for children. but we do want advertising geared to children, even though this is strictly outside the scope of this proposal. a second point is alcohol. there's a vote against the ban on claims for alcoholic beverages. we have to do it within our group. i'm disappointed in you. when you overlook all the problems with alcoholism, liver damage and brain damage, it's hard to approve health claims for alcohol. with alcohol, warnings are in order. we should not encourage the consumption of alcohol. a third and very important point is whether claims should be subject to the authorization procedure or whether notification is sufficient. producers must provide convincing evidence of the validity of their claim when applying for a licence. the burden of proof is on the authorities when they give notice. that means that a claim is allowed until the authorities have been able to prove otherwise and that is how we get the essence out of the bill and that is what our group is against in any case. i call on all colleagues to vote tomorrow for consumers and for benevolent companies. we must not give in to the pressure of companies who are afraid of being exposed. the single market is not a license to mislead consumers. let the results tomorrow make it clear to the europeans that europe is there for them.
the commissioner is presenting a legacy which he has received from his predecessor and he is proud to defend it with enthusiasm. the consumer is, after all, at the heart of this proposal. and that's important too. it is important for consumers to know that health, nutrition or food claims cannot be false or misleading. that is the beauty of this proposal and the alde group fully supports it. on the contrary, it is of course true that those communications which are scientifically proven - which are true - are useful to consumers. i don't want to talk about it as "that's advertising and advertising is actually a little bit dirty because that's what makes profit", no, that's also important to consumers. consumers want to be able to make a choice and that choice must therefore be offered to them. but it must be scientifically proven, and that is not something we should just allow, no, i think it is something we should welcome, because it increases consumer choice and it encourages innovation, and that is also important. now i know there's a gray area in the middle, because what do you do with these health claims that are on a product that is actually not healthy in itself? i would say that this is a case of deception, and article 7 already provides safeguards against this. i am not sure that we need european food profiles for this. nutrition profiles could be a good idea. i know that member states do. the commission has not yet been able to convince us that this is possible at national level, but whether it is possible at european level is not yet clear. i am currently campaigning for the constitutional treaty in the netherlands. i do not know whether mrs corbey does the same - apparently not - but if i were to say there 'don't worry people, brussels is going to tell you what you can and cannot eat' then that treaty would be voted down altogether. that is not a message we can get across on the basis of arguments like we have had. so i want a full argument. we as a group will not vote largely for the deletion of article 4, but we do think that the commission should come back to us with a better argument and that is what our amendment no 107 is about. finally - i am already over my time - very briefly, we are in favour of the least possible bureaucracy, so we opt for the notification procedure which we believe provides sufficient guarantees for consumers and involves less embarrassment. and finally, obesity does need to be discussed, it needs to be addressed. but that is not part of this directive, but rather of the food labelling directive, which we would have liked to have had by now. if you do that, commissioner, i think we can talk about obesity as a problem much more effectively.
i think the commission's proposal is a good proposal in itself. the original report by mrs bortone, in fact, adds a number of improvements to it, as far as i am concerned. this is a matter which has been on the table for several years and which is now being dealt with by its second rapporteur. the vote in the committee on the environment also showed that this is not an easy subject. for a moment, i thought mrs. bortone would throw in the towel this time, too. the vote in the committee on the environment completely eroded the european commission's proposal and the rapporteur's report. article 4 on nutritional profiles, which is very important, was simply deleted by one amendment. this article was intended to avoid putting a health claim on foods such as chips, chocolate and alcohol, which are not healthy foods in themselves, which could lead the consumer to conclude that they are healthy because they contain calcium, for example. so this is really a public health issue, and the food industry, thanks to its lobby, has taken the hit. the environment committee has therefore rejected the principle that a claim must be based on the true nutritional value of a product. in order to defend this position, it has used the same argument as the food industry, namely that there are no bad or good foods, but only bad diets or eating habits. the commission has also rejected the proposed authorisation procedure and replaced it with a notification requirement. this means that the producer or importer of food is only required to notify a food agency that he uses this or that claim and is not required to provide scientific evidence. my group has tabled a number of amendments which correct this situation, which is unacceptable to us. we want to get article 4 back in. finally, i should like to say that if this article on nutritional profiles is not reintroduced, even in a lighter form, we will vote against the proposal.
deputising for the author.– madam president, i will avoid making this a purely dutch discussion, and speak in english so that others can participate directly in this interesting debate. the possibility of a takeover of the london stock exchange by deutsche börse or euronext has ignited debates in the european parliament over whether such a takeover can be assessed at national level alone. at present, the two cases are being dealt with by the office of fair trading and the competition commission in the uk. legally speaking, the takeover of the lse may be the responsibility of the relevant member state’s competition authorities, since european competition rules do not apply as long as the national regulators deem the relevant markets to be their respective member states and the turnover level to be below the thresholds set for european competition rules to apply. nevertheless, the committee on economic and monetary affairs has tabled this oral question to the commission to ask it to play a more proactive role. we would like the commission to consider the different scenarios that might arise and assess the effects that they might bring to competition in, and the pricing of, listing services, trading services, information services and, not least, important post-trade services, clearing and settlement. we are aware of the detailed studies undertaken by the competition commission in the uk, prepared by the british office of fair trading. the standpoint of that assessment is, however, primarily or maybe even exclusively the impact for the uk markets, and we would like to see that broadened in the commission’s assessment. you may remember the shock caused in 2002-2003 when some traders in dutch blue-chip equities challenged euronext amsterdam to lower prices and to providing improvements in services, by looking for an alternative trading venue with deutsche börse and the lse. this showed that competition really makes a difference. this characterises the situation we have at present of an open integrated european market with crossborder competition. the question is whether different scenarios increase this competitive tendency, or go in the opposite direction and tend to create monopolies - situations for which, by the way, ex ante competition regulation might be a more appropriate answer. another related issue is the aspect of regulation and supervision. from the moment the possible take-over of the lse appeared on the pages of the financial papers, speculation started as to where the headquarters of the merged entity would be based and which regulator would get the power to supervise it. that was more than speculation. i tend to believe there was truth in the stories that the issue of the regulatory regime was a major subject in the merger talks. who knows, it might even have been part of the background of the rebellion against the deutsche börse leadership, which you in germany have such nice nicknames for. as the rapporteur of subsequent reports on prudential supervision and the fsap, i would not dare to express my preferences for one or other national regulatory regimes. my far greater concern is that there will be counterproductive competition between national regimes, instead of further coordination and cooperation. that is one of my strongest arguments for a european level supervisor. in the reports i prepared in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, and which were adopted in the previous plenary sitting, this is again stressed as an option for the near future. such a european level supervisor should execute the application of the european regulatory framework of the fsap developed in previous years. as regards the part of the financial market which is already supranational and operates crossborder and often cross-sector, one of our main messages is to create real convergence in the implementation of the european directives and real convergence of supervisory practice, thereby avoiding regulatory arbitrage and possible competition on other factors for establishment such as fiscal and cost aspects. therefore, the possible effects of the commission’s ambitions as regards integration of the financial markets and the regulation and supervision of different scenarios should also be part of the assessment we are asking for. in that context i would ask you to focus your attention on scenarios in which merger activity is not restricted to eu countries alone. there may also be conceivable scenarios in which the new york stock exchange and a newly formed one joint with the lse or, on the other hand euronext and deutsche börse might get together. there might then be a disintegration rather than a further convergence of european regulations. this should also be taken into account in your assessment and expectations. it is regrettable that this debate is being held at such a late hour, but i thank the commissioners for being here and wish mr mccreevy, who was supposed to be here, all the best with his health. i also hope, of course, that we can continue the debate in the committee.
the rapporteur. president, a social europe in the world economy, jobs and new opportunities. under this motto, the european commission presented the social agenda for the coming years in february. and we shall try today to respond to the initiative laid down in the documents. i hope therefore that the commission, and the council, will take up positively the points which we are putting today in the report on which we are going to vote. sometimes i feel that when you're involved in social policy, you're considered a softie. social policy is no longer relevant in this europe. but if we want to achieve the objectives, if we want to regain confidence in our society, if we want to ensure that people who are currently out of work can get a job, if we want to achieve that, then we need the social model we know in europe. when i look at the treaty, the new constitutional treaty, and this is the debate we are having in france and the netherlands, i see that the social market economy is being used as a guide and has consequences for all the policies and for all the policies we are implementing in the european union. and if we know that, then we must also translate the social market economy into every policy we put forward here. this means that when we look at the social agenda together, achieving the lisbon objectives, i.e. getting a job, creating good and quality employment, is of the utmost importance to us. that also means that we must ensure that small and mediumsized enterprises, which are the engine of more and better jobs, are also given every opportunity. that is lisbon. but lisbon also means that we must ensure that people who are now excluded, have a disability or have a disability which prevents them from combining work and professional life adequately, that these people with the social environment we want in europe also have an opportunity on the labour market. it also means that people with a disability who have the remaining skills will still have a chance on the labour market thanks to additional opportunities for retraining or further training. we have been talking for years now - i think for several decades - about encouraging retraining, retraining, upskilling, being always active to train for the labour market, and in fact, with one exception, far too little is being done in this area. i therefore think that training should receive the greatest possible attention from the commissioner. then the social market economy. we need an economy that's made for everyone. so that also means that we have to look very closely at the demographic effects of our society on our social security system. if we know that we are going to face a de-greening and silvering of our workforce in the coming period, then that also means that we have to adapt our social protection systems to these demographic factors. the european commission is analysing well, but it is not translating this into an absolute policy that this report lacks. and i hope that with the emphasis we will put on the vote in the near future, we will succeed in making the social agenda more practical.
president. - each year in may we debate here the guidelines for economic policy in the european union. today it seems more like we should be talking about ethics than economics. mr goebbels' report contains many points with which i can agree. i agree with the integration of social policy and the concern about the lack of progress on investment to meet kyoto. and that is where mr goebbels should have left it. on one important point this report goes far beyond my means, and i agree with mrs lambert. the rapporteur, in amendment no 12, calls for more objective information on the controversial research into genetically modified organisms and embryonic stem cell research. i think we have better things to do than promote controversial research. it is essential to me that stem cell research should focus on the value of human life, and research on embryos which are destroyed by it does not fit into that. in previous texts parliament has made clear its position on the place of stem cell research. the text of this resolution is therefore not in line with the texts adopted earlier. on 10 march, for example, parliament adopted a resolution on the trade in human eggs, paragraph 15 of which states explicitly that embryonic stem cell research may be left to the member states where it is permitted. please note that the member state is not required to carry out this examination. that is a clear distinction which is missing from the text of this resolution. i shall not support this resolution if amendment no 12 and amendment no 24 by mrs van den burg are adopted unchanged.
mr president, several speakers from my group have already said: we are also strongly in favour of the integrated approach of these guidelines. i would like to say that this should not be a superficial discussion, but that it is important to go into the depths and, in particular, to give the trade committees a role in their specific field. in the committee on economic affairs, we in our group are stressing the need for stronger economic coordination, and i would also like to suggest to commissioner almunia that we look at how we can bring about more coherence in the discussion of these package guidelines and the annual budgetary reports on convergence in the context of the stability pact. the third point i would like to make is that it is important to give the european parliament a full role in these processes and also to ensure that we have sufficient time and space for our input in the coming years in terms of procedures. that is more a technical question of procedures and timetables, but i hope that we can reach good agreements on that next year, because this year was a bit difficult. i think it's important in terms of content. i think that the commission has a much better chance of getting parliament on its side in the search for a genuine european perspective. and that is my last point, i am concerned about the way in which the council is still working from that european perspective and i would therefore ask the commission to take care of that. in this case i would like to address myself specifically to mr spidla, because i was extremely disturbed by the way in which a position we took in the last part-session on the working time directive was dealt with. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think that is not the way the commission should deal with parliament.
mr president, several speakers have asked mr špidla about the working time directive and the way he dealt with parliament’s position in the last plenary. i did not hear a response on that.
mr president, this is not really a point of order. i just want to draw the house’s attention to amendment 8, which calls for services of general interest to be excluded from the requirement of notification under state aid policy. we actually adopted a report on this matter in february – as a matter of fact it was my own report – and this amendment would be exactly the opposite of the conclusions of that report, which was adopted with a solid majority.
in writing. i thought i should vote for article 29 which deletes the nutritional profiles proposed by the commission. i understand the different position of mr maaten, since he is supposed to be the shadow rapporteur of the alde group, and to be the one to put forward the agreed group line. the starting point for nutrition and health claims on foods should be that there are no good or bad foods, only good or bad eating habits. food claims should be scientifically substantiated and not be required to fit a government-imposed profile. moreover, uniform nutritional profiles are at odds with the variety of lifestyles and eating patterns in the member states of the european union. the introduction of nutrition profiles as a condition for the authorisation of nutrition and health claims made on foods therefore infringes the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. another objection to the concept of nutritional profiles is the disproportionate burden it imposes on small and mediumsized enterprises, which are notably heavily represented in the food industry.
in writing. i voted against the resolution on working conditions for mobile workers providing interoperable crossborder services. this resolution welcomes a council directive which makes a social partners' agreement into law. this agreement between cer (industry) and etf (trade unions) is not necessary, we already have a working time directive (2003/88) which applies perfectly to mobile workers. in addition, the cer represents only those railway companies which have national monopolies in the member states. the erfa, which represents smaller companies, was not recognised by the commission as a social partner. the new directive places impossible demands on these very small railway undertakings. for example, a mobile worker who spends one night away from home is required to spend the next night at home. that is overregulation in itself, but also a barrier to new entrants to the market. new players in the market that actually offer crossborder freight transport. in short, the eu cannot use this directive if it wants to make a modal shift from road to rail and if it wants to make the eu more competitive in the context of the lisbon strategy. that is why i voted against it.
in writing. the vvd delegation has decided to vote against the oomen report on the social policy agenda for the period 2006-2010. the report highlights a number of issues which do not need to be dealt with at european level, such as the proposal for a legislative initiative on a european minimum income. the vvd also sees little in the way of promoting a european model of education. typical examples of unwanted brussels interference and undermining of national sovereignty, unacceptable to the vvd. the request to the european commission to draw up a voluntary framework for crossborder collective bargaining is also unnecessary. the social partners are already operating at european level and are much better able to organise this among themselves. the report also calls for the introduction of a new system of taxation of capital. many of these laws are a suffocating blanket for entrepreneurs, preventing them from starting businesses. this could have a boomerang effect on employment, which i believe is never the intention of social policy. the vvd believes that the best social policy at european level is the creation of jobs.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. mr president, first of all i would like to thank mrs rothe for her efforts in writing the report. the economic committee's opinion stressed the need for a cost-effective approach to energy saving. we have also proposed a number of amendments and on some important points they are happily accepted, but the final proposal does not go far enough as far as the economic committee is concerned. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. the different levels of energy efficiency between member states should be taken into account. i am happy with compromise no 107, because it brings more flexibility to national objectives, or at least it can bring more flexibility. that is a good thing in itself, but on the other hand we should not maintain the mandatory targets per member state and thereby leave the door open to uniform mandatory targets per country. so, mr president, i think that we should not follow that commitment. secondly, it is important to ensure a strong role for smes in the energy services market, as the contributions of smes to the reduction of energy consumption are significant. the compulsory provision of free energy audits by energy suppliers would be detrimental to independent companies and smes and it is therefore good that it has been abolished. thirdly, the economic committee therefore believes that we must strengthen a cost-efficient approach. it is very important that energy saving is accompanied by a good cost-benefit analysis. the proposal to introduce benchmarks will certainly encourage the dissemination of best practice, but it is important to set up any benchmarking system in such a way that no paper-thin lump is created that negates the added value of the benchmarks.
president. - mr president, to start with, we are saying today a resounding 'yes' to a european approach to more efficient energy use, but i would first like to congratulate mrs mechtild rothe and the european commission on the proposal and report on energy efficiency. it is in the interest of all of us to use energy more economically and to increase energy efficiency has only winners. greater efficiency means reducing greenhouse gas emissions, and in doing so, we help meet our kyoto commitments. consumers and public authorities save on their energy bills, and companies that use energy efficiently increase their competitiveness. innovation is the key word here and i wholeheartedly support the top-runner principle of amendment no 96. critics who consider this proposal to be unnecessary interference from brussels in internal affairs are forgetting that without outside pressure, unfortunately not enough is being done to make our energy use more efficient, but mr piebalgs' top-down approach must, in my view, be supplemented by the reverse approach. amendment no 45 challenges companies to come up with all kinds of proposals for more efficient energy use. i also wholeheartedly support the commission's efforts to make the public authorities of the member states serve as role models. governments are in a prime position to make investments in energy efficiency that are not profitable in the short term, but pay off in the long term. whether it is the purchase of clean and efficient service vehicles, refuse trucks, electronic equipment or energy-efficient buildings, the government is in a prime position to tackle this problem. i also believe that we as european institutions have an additional responsibility in this context. we must not only spread i think that we need to send a very clear signal to the european countryside today. europe must remain visible, tangible and recognisable, even when there are major changes in the market. what has been shown in the past week, ladies and gentlemen, is that the market, the market, the market, the purse alone, does not bring europe closer together. that market, market, market, doesn't reach it is a difficult dilemma, mr president, and it is a bit like the chicken and the egg, which came first: the chicken or the egg. we must first have democracy fully established with democratic and legal controls or we must first take decisive decisions which also make citizens support the interests of european democracy en masse. the answer, of course, is that it must be both. mr oreja has already said that terrorism and the fight against it are essentially crossborder and that we must also work together better in this respect. but that's hard. because with 25 countries, there are also 25 vetoes. and decisions are trained, there's a woolly compromise or they're simply not executed. and i think countries should now leap over their own shadow. but at the same time we must also recognize that these are very sensitive decisions taken in the council of ministers, decisions which affect civil rights. and that's why it's wrong for them to be so mistaken. i think that the council should continue to meet in public and vote in public. and that does not require any amendment of the treaty. but that's just a matter of opening doors. and i hope that parliament will take joint action to consult the council on this. it has been said many times that terrorism is an attack on our fundamental freedoms and that we should not make the mistake of undermining those civil rights ourselves. another example that is often given is privacy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. and the proposal for the storage of traffic data is indeed out of all proportion. if the council approves it, the european parliament should go to the court of justice. it is also not very encouraging that the council is trying to circumvent democratic control by including this decision in the third pillar instead of the first. because, indeed, if we want to protect democracy from terrorism, we must not destroy it ourselves.
i would like to thank all the rapporteurs for the work they have done on these reports. the reports we are discussing today repeatedly stress that the defence and promotion of human rights is the most important and best strategy for preventing and combating terrorism. commissioner frattini has indicated that the fight against terrorism should be a matter of strengthening human rights, and i fully agree with him. defending and promoting important values such as democracy, freedom, pluralism and human dignity are crucial in the fight against terrorism. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the radicalisation and polarization which are so often both a part and a consequence of terrorism are at least as great a danger to the european union as terrorism itself. we politicians must be very aware of this and at all times try to keep our heads cool, we must be realistic, we must not unnecessarily stir up fear and avoid becoming ourselves part of the hysteria which only fuels the tensions which feed terrorism. at any rate, we should not fall into the trap of following the bizarre reasoning that terrorists use to justify their inhuman actions. we must take measures which really increase the freedom of all citizens and stay away from measures which only increase the appearance of security. two of the measures discussed here deserve, i think, extra attention in this respect. first, the idea of criminalizing terrorism at the international criminal court. i wonder what problem that's a solution to, exactly? do we really have to treat the perpetrators of terror the way we treat former dictators like milosovic? one thing i know for sure, the murderer of theo van gogh, a famous filmmaker and a highly-publicized columnist and opinion maker from my country, the netherlands, would love to be able to use the platform that such a case would offer him. so i don't think that's a good idea at all. then the storage of traffic data. if there is one example of measures that lead to only a semblance of safety, this is it. a lot has been said about this. so i will not go into it. it is disproportionate, it restricts our freedom and i think that is precisely the freedom we need to promote in the european union. there are risks involved, you can put all kinds of restrictions on the access to that kind of data, but let's face it, anything that's on the internet is open to anyone, no matter how well you secure it. so there are risks involved that are likely to outweigh the benefits involved. i don't think we should do that. instead - because the internet is indeed a very emerging means of communication for people who want to deal with terrorist recruiters - we should have our security services specialise in participating on the internet, in reading, in discussing, in simply actively following what is happening there; that would really help! looking back at the websites that the bomber visited doesn't seem like the best strategy. we must try to prevent these bombings from being carried out. i think that such a measure - the legal basis has been discussed here on several occasions and the way in which this decision is taken is also a flagrant breach of democracy - undermines confidence in european democracy; the dangers of this have become painfully clear in recent times.
mr president, i wish to begin by expressing my regret at the french and the dutch ‘no’ to the constitution, because the eu will now have to fight terrorism with one hand tied behind its back. the citizens have given a very clear signal in the debate on the referendums. i would, therefore, call on the council to work in the spirit of the constitution, and that means respecting three key principles. the first is democratic control. that means that the council should not ignore and sideline the european parliament, but should take its recommendations on board, even if it is not yet obliged to. the other two aspects mentioned by many colleagues are proportionality and effectiveness. again, in these areas, the council should think twice about certain measures. its own report on the implementation of the action plan to combat terrorism shows that there are considerable gaps in implementation. before adopting new measures we should be looking at implementation. in the case of the peer review, for example, only ten out of twenty-five countries have so far submitted their implementation reports! how can we take new steps if we do not even know if the old ones work? i now turn to three specific issues. firstly, data retention. a lot has already been said and again i would call on the council to act in the spirit of the constitution and not ignore the european parliament. it should also not ignore the signals given by many countries – the example of the united states has already been mentioned. but i would add to those examples the case of the dutch parliament, which wanted to adopt a similar data retention measure. however, once it found out how that would work – or, rather, not work – in practice, the dutch parliament reconsidered it, because it realised that the data retention proposal was simply not workable. the council should not ignore this. it is regrettable that the council is not present on this occasion. with passenger name records, there have also been several incidents. we were promised an evaluation a year after this entered into force. i should like to know from the commission when we can expect the evaluation. thirdly, we would like to know more about the sitcen, the situation centre. what exactly is it doing, what kind of information does it deal with and will it report to the european parliament? finally, i ask the european parliament to show its colours. we have talked a lot today about the protection of personal data and fundamental rights. i call on this parliament to adopt not only all the reports on antiterrorism measures but also the moraes report on anti-discrimination policies and minority rights, because fundamental rights are for all citizens.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. president, i would like to congratulate mr böge on his balanced report. there are still some pain points here and there for the agricultural sector, but it is a very balanced approach. and, mr president, it is european agriculture that makes the multiannual budget possible, because it is the only sector that is going to make a big contribution. we're going from 36 to 26% of the budget. i think that is a huge leap forward for all the other sectors involved in european policy. two pain points remain. we have an agreement from brussels which indicates what growth is allowed in that budget. at the same time, i note that there is no financing for the accession of romania and bulgaria. and now that european agriculture is already producing so much, i cannot imagine that the brussels agreement should also finance the accession of bulgaria and romania with the 26% allocated to it. in an extreme situation, because that agreement and the rebates to it have to be paid by the 15 old member states, it could lead to farmers in the 15 old member states receiving 15% less premium in 2012 than farmers in the new member states. that can't be the reality. mr böge also suggests that we can fight evil with the devil by solving this problem through partial co-financing. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. secondly, we are very much attached to the common market and its preservation. in any case, cofinancing of compulsory expenditure must not lead to the renationalisation of agricultural policy. one more point concerning the council. there's a lot of debate about the net payments. i would suggest to the council that the net contributions of the member states should be reduced to take account of own resources. in my country, that is half the net contribution and that may solve the problem.
the rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on budgetary control. president, we are grateful to the rapporteur for including one of the main recommendations of the committee on budgetary control in his report for the time being; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budget is properly implemented. this is important because the main expenditure is in the member states. we all know that the european court of auditors has not issued a positive statement of assurance for the last ten years. the second important point made by the committee on budgetary control is that, although we have a budget, there is a huge backlog of expenditure every year. these are known as the ral and we therefore think it is very important that the n + 2 rule be maintained. it is not possible for member states to put money forward and then the commission does not know how to spend it.
i have been campaigning a lot in recent weeks for the future of europe and i am very sorry that the referendum in the netherlands ended in a 'no', but of course it is not strange. if a country is told year after year that europe is too expensive and that it is a disgrace how much the netherlands pays to brussels, then you cannot expect them to stand up and say, yes, let us embrace europe wholeheartedly. the dutch accountants sowed the seeds of euroscepticism and reaped the rewards on 1 june. what is the conclusion of the dutch government? on behalf of the alde group .– i should like to join with those who have congratulated the rapporteur on pulling off what was not a very easy job. sometimes we have a rather schizophrenic attitude to immigration. on the one hand we continuously stress the need for more economic integration, while at the same time we further tighten the rules on immigration. the result is illegal immigration, human trafficking and abuse of asylum policies. we have shameful scenes in europe of misery, poverty, and even of people drowning on our shores. that is unacceptable. we need to accept that europe has become an immigration continent. we should actually be welcoming immigrants, people who are trying to improve their lot. those people are not criminals, they are showing initiative. people like that have built the united states. they are dynamic and strong and we need them. these people are trying to improve their lives and that is not a crime. we need proper european immigration policies with real possibilities for legal immigration. at the same time we need integration policies. immigrants should be fully included in the community. they should be enabled to participate fully in social, cultural and political life, whether by actual voting rights or mechanisms for representation. my group is very happy to support the report as it stands. it is balanced and will give the necessary support to the european commission in setting up its policies.

for example, in the case of long waiting lists in one's own country or the lack of specialist knowledge. i would therefore also like to take this opportunity to advocate a greater role for the european union in health and health care. if someone is sick, he should be able to get medical care abroad. keeping borders closed to medical care for all sorts of administrative, procedural or financial reasons is ultimately not in the best interests of the consumer and that's why we're here anyway. the right to visit a doctor abroad has already been recognised by the court of justice, so why not, as john bowis rightly says, enshrine this in european law. we are in a modern society with generally well-educated, mobile and able citizens, and those people should be able to choose where and how they want to be treated. opening borders to patients also encourages the improvement of the quality of healthcare in the home country, the reduction of waiting lists and the provision of specialist knowledge. that said, i would like to emphasize the positive role of the european union in health care, and the presence of the commissioner at this unchristian hour is all the more appreciated. the health care system in the european union is an exception to the rule that the european union is often associated with the negative image of bureaucracy and the making of senseless and unnecessary rules. we are doing a good job here and we are of course going to vote with great enthusiasm for the report by john bowis.
i am grateful to the president-in-office for his reply, and i would like to congratulate mr bowis on his clear report and his clear position on a topical problem. unfortunately, health care in many countries is not optimal. worse still, in many countries quality and accessibility are in question, and waiting lists and waiting times are increasing. under normal circumstances, patients benefit from care close to home, at least in their own country. in the situation of declining quality, it becomes attractive for patients to look beyond the borders. recent court rulings encourage the patient to seek his salvation elsewhere. i fully agree with the rapporteur's choice to give priority to improving the quality of health care. shopping across the border is not the answer. of course, in border regions it is practical to make joint arrangements for cross-border treatment. and of course, patient out-migration can also be an incentive to invest more in your own health care, but it's a cumbersome way to boost quality. it is therefore not appropriate to regulate patient mobility in the services directive. the committee on the environment has rightly removed patient mobility from the services directive. but, commissioner, there is an urgent problem. there is a great difference in quality between member states. the disparity in survival rates for bladder cancer, for example, is frightening. in austria and iceland, the survival rate for bladder cancer is twice as high as in poland. instead of inventing procedures, laws and regulations to guide the free movement of patients, the commission should invest as proactively as possible in the exchange of knowledge. bladder cancer patients in poland also deserve high-quality treatment. all bladder cancer patients want to benefit from austrian knowledge and expertise. so let's take that step forward and not invest unnecessarily in mobility that patients don't want anyway. waking up from a drug overdose in a strange environment is no fun for anyone. so, quality instead of mobility.
mr president, for two months i have been actively involved in the campaign which in the netherlands has produced 62% of the votes for 'no to the constitution'. as the largest organization in the no camp, our socialist party stood up for the voters' right to change that text. we did not oppose the possibility of a european constitution, nor did we oppose new or future member states, nor even the fact that the netherlands has now become the most extreme net contributor within the european union. we were concerned with the content of the constitution. the text is too much a recipe for an american-style europe, with its emphasis on free and unadulterated competition, liberalization of services, armaments, ties with nato and possibilities for intervention outside the territory of the european union. chapter 3 does not belong in a constitution, but should be part of normal legislation which can be reviewed by the european parliament, with the influence of the national parliaments. then elections become the means for voters to force change and remove the feeling that ordinary people can no longer contribute to policy change. the commission has also proposed that the council should be able to adopt the draft constitution, which is the only way to achieve the objectives of the treaty. with these arguments we have managed to persuade many people who are complaining about the policies and the interference of the european union, but who were inclined to stay at home because they had no hope of improvement, to come to the polls anyway. instead of the expected low turnout with a majority for "yes", it became a high turnout with a majority for "no". that constitution is dead now. but if the voters in other member states are offered a referendum, i expect that the majority will also reject this constitutional text. that 's how the people get the right to amend ... mr president, the voters in the netherlands and france have given us a clear message. stop, we're drifting along without any clear direction. in the netherlands, 62% of the electorate voted 'no'. it's time to do something positive with those voters. the "no" voice came from two different directions. on the one hand, there is the right-wing voter who has a clear anti-european sentiment and says 'no' to the european project. but on the other hand, there is a 'no' vote from the progressive electorate who are in favour of european cooperation but who consider this constitution too intrusive and too unsocial. these sentiments are also reflected in the progressive voters who did cast their ballots with some hesitation for the constitution, because they felt it was a step forward. we must now try to unite those progressive voters from the 'yes' and 'no' camps in favour of a clearly defined european project. of course, other countries must also have a say in the european constitution, but the netherlands and france have made their choice. not this constitution, not a second referendum on the same subject. we must not now go into isolation or make a pass at the scene, on the contrary. we want a reform strike in europe to show the voters that we have heard their concerns, both for the 'yes' and the 'no' voters. this reform must be based on a broad and open debate on the direction and speed of european integration. how many countries are there? how do we make europe strong and social? perhaps the council, on 16 and 17 june and then under the leadership of tony blair, can begin to think about how to save the important elements, namely chapters 1 and 2 of the constitution, the principles of europe, improved voting weighting, strong subsidiarity, openness, improved influence of national parliaments and the rights of citizens in a simplified treaty approach. otherwise, the procession in echternach threatens to be nothing but a step backwards, without a vision of realistic european cooperation, and as barroso rightly said: "europe is actually the right answer to many of the criticisms voiced by citizens, and that is why i am determined to continue working".

in writing. as members of the socialist party in the netherlands, we have no objections in principle to the possibility that the european union could have the maximum of 1.26% of gross national product, as laid down in the treaties. money collectively spent on democracy, the environment, public services, social security and international solidarity is money well spent. a society which fails to have the necessary resources is in permanent crisis. we have no sympathy whatsoever for those who want to achieve tax reductions through a retreating government. we are still critical of eu spending. money is being unnecessarily pumped into regions in the richest member states, a lot of agricultural money is going to big farmers and export subsidies, the disaster fund is suddenly being expanded with a terrorism fund, the european parliament is unnecessarily costing a lot of money through high expenses reimbursements and the constant moving between two cities, many cross-border problems for which the eu could be useful are not being addressed and a lot of money is going to opaque spending and fraud. as long as this situation persists, we have little reason to actively oppose those who want to reduce expenditure to 1%, even if we reject their often selfish views and arguments.

mr president, no is no. the dutch vote is final. these are not my words, but those of the dutch prime minister balkenende in the volkskrant today. after the rejection of the proposed text of the constitution by the voters in the netherlands and france, most national governments have opted for a pause. does this merely provide a pause for the administrators to reflect on their tactics or does it provide an opportunity to change and improve the constitution through a broad societal discussion of the general public and civil society organizations? i am surprised to see that the european institutions have been the first to start reflecting and thinking. it is disappointing to see how many heads of government are committing themselves with all their might to a text which is, in fact, out of the question. in this way, the reflection on europe's future is unduly hampered. the summit meeting of 16 and 17 june also demonstrated once again the bankruptcy of this constitution. the optimistic tones of a common destiny in the preamble sound grim to the ears of someone from a so-called fat and well-fed country unwilling to do anything for poor countries. in this sense, madam president, a reflection on the subject of 'diplomatic language' is of primary importance for paris. the impasse over the eu's multiannual budget is also linked to the current institutional crisis in europe. the debate on the future budgetary resources of the union closely corresponds to the debate on the european constitution. it is impossible and undesirable to identify policy priorities and provide for their financing in a new multiannual budget while the reflection and reformulation of the mandate of the european union is still in full swing. the european union must take seriously the concerns of citizens about the speed and the direction of the european integration process. the citizens want a healthy european cooperation in which the national interest is not immediately dismissed as egoism or nationalism. the citizens want a europe that does not lose itself in federalist dreams but tackles transnational problems in a forward-looking way.

president. - one of the unfinished business of european cooperation is european and monetary union. when it was founded in maastricht, there were still enthusiastic europeans with a vision, like jacques delors. his objective with monetary union was twofold: the european council in madrid in december last year, in the context of the european council in brussels, called for the creation of a european monetary system, which would create stability in exchange rates and inflation trends, and take a step towards a political union in which prosperity and well-being for european citizens would be guaranteed. the first one went very well. we have a stable euro that has withstood major currency crises and inflation that is so low that economists are already worried about it. but we seem to be further away from that second aspect, political union, these days than ever before. although, sometimes crises lead to surprising leaps forward. the member states which abandoned their national currencies for the single euro without entering into further political union and adopting a single macroeconomic and fiscal policy needed guarantees to prevent the savings of one member state from being undermined by the irresponsible borrowing of the other. they did not want to interfere too much in each other's political choices for fiscal policy, and so they created the stability and growth pact with a corresponding sanctions system, a pact based on mistrust. it set quantitative standards for the net result of annual government budgets, the size of the national debt, etc. at the end of the last century, that worked, but in the economic situation of recent years, those rules proved too rigid. so, in pure rational terms, it was logical and sensible to change those rules and to put that goal of stability and growth back at the centre. but at the same time a number of large member states got into trouble and they are opposing the commission's intervention and the sanctions of their fellow finance ministers. the european commission's rightful adaptation to make the pact more intelligent thus coincided with the prestige battle between member states. commissioner almunia was mainly focused on sensible reform but the eco-feminists were mainly concerned with creating space for their own excuse to break the rules. so there has been a lot of heated debate on this and generally the criticism has been directed mainly at the latter. the european central bank, for example, was very negative about the corrective part, but could be found in the preventive part of the pact: take account of business cycles, long-term debt positions, etc. the storm's gone. the pact itself has not changed, but we are now dealing with a state of those budgets. the curious thing is that we have adopted the regulation on the controversial corrective part, that it is only subject to an advisory procedure and that it has now entered into force since the last vote, but that we now have a regulation on the other, preventive part at second reading. let me put that aside for a moment. the commission has therefore adopted a number of amendments to that regulation, but the result of adopting those amendments by a qualified majority would be that the regulation could not enter into force. i would therefore recommend that we do not do that, but that we now accept that in parliament, as the commission has wisely done, because then those two regulations - to which we attach more importance than to the first - can come into force at the same time. let us at least give jean-claude juncker, who has had such a difficult time in recent times, the success and the result he has achieved during the luxembourg presidency.

in writing. our party, the socialist party in the netherlands, voted against the draft membership statute on 17 december 2003. we did not oppose a unitary wage, but what was proposed then was far too high. we also thought it important that this salary could be subject to national taxation and that the travel allowance should be effectively limited to actual expenditure. our experience now is that you can pay back more than half of that annually. in the dutch parliament we asked the government not to approve this document in the council. the amendment which we are voting on today is still not what we want. the salary must be less than €7000,-- and we flatly reject article 9.3. we are aware that a negative vote cannot bring about any further improvements. now that the maximum has been reached, we consider it a victory in our long struggle against misrepresentation. acceptance of this proposal is a defeat for those who have resisted for years because they found a status beneath their dignity, salaries and allowances too low and financial controls too intrusive. by voting against, they can make it clear that they want to be shameless pickpockets.
mr president, the two items – the lauk report on the european central bank, and the report on the communication strategy on the euro – were originally to be debated jointly. that made sense, because president trichet of the european central bank would have been present at the debate. when, at a later stage, the two items were separated, it meant that president trichet would be present only for the lauk report and therefore would be unable to discuss the communication strategy on the euro with us. that is a pity both for us and for president trichet who, i understand, is keen to be part of that debate. it makes more sense to have a joint debate on the two reports to enable president trichet to participate on both reports, rather than on the annual report of the european central bank alone. that is the reason for our request.
the commission's communication on the communication strategy for the euro has been somewhat broadened by the committee on economic and monetary affairs into a discussion on the current state of the euro and what we should do with it. this debate is taking place at a time when the whole political debate on europe is under pressure. i don't think we can turn a blind eye to that now. i believe that many of us - myself included - felt that when the euro was introduced in 1999, and particularly in 2002, it would bring europe closer to its citizens. that citizens, especially those in the euro zone, would hold europe in their hands every day and that this would help people to feel more connected to europe. i think, however, that we must conclude that this did not happen. i have seen in my own country, but i also see in other countries in the euro zone or countries which could join, that the euro has in some respects even proved to be a block. i am very sorry about that, but it is a finding we have had to make. that's actually a stupid situation, because if we look at how the euro works, that's great, of course. cheaper financing options for all, interest rates are lower than ever, and this encourages investment and consumption, and offers real benefits to consumers, for example through low mortgage rates and cheaper loans. there is increased price transparency within a stricter management of monetary policy by the european central bank. this will lead to a stable environment for the euro and low inflation. look at the countries of the 2001-2002 conversion, where inflation was only 2.3%. a great success. the euro has increased the resilience of euro area economies to financial crises. without the euro, 11 september or 11 march 2004 or the accounting scandals of 2002 would have caused much greater turbulence. that's not what happened. we owe that to the euro. the exchange rate risk has been removed, travel within the euro area is easier, all kinds of hassle has been eliminated and the cost of exchanging currencies has been eliminated. consumers benefit from the strong euro. commodities and oil on the world market are paid in dollars and the favourable exchange rate, at least until the referendums in france and the netherlands, ensures relatively low fuel and food prices for european consumers. there is an increase in trade within the european union, 10% for all countries combined and 17% for some countries such as the netherlands, spain, belgium and austria. i am not even mentioning the fact that the euro enjoys a high reputation on the international financial markets, as is shown by its important role as an international reserve currency. despite all these successes - and i think this shows that the euro is perhaps one of the most successful, if not the most successful, project of the european union to date - we see that many ordinary consumers apparently disagree with us. that means that we have a major problem, similar to the problem we also had in the constitutional debate. what is the basis for this? i think mainly the problem of inflation. president trichet, you said something about this in our monetary dialogue on 14 march which i thought was very good. i think we have not communicated well to the public about inflation and have lost some of our credibility in countries which have now adopted the euro. i've noticed in my contacts with civilians that this is almost beyond repair. when i say: "inflation has been very limited", the answer is: "you are lying". if i say, "yes, but statistical agencies and eurostat say that", they say, "no, but i'm not sure". "yes, but they're lying too". the credibility is limited. we have obviously not been able to explain how inflation, which was real in 2002, has disappeared. consumers are not crazy. we have not been able to explain to them what caused the inflation and so it is all blamed on the euro. we, with all our institutions, must take the blame. of course, all the stability pact stuff is added to that. i therefore welcome the amendment by the epp colleagues on this subject. the socialist group also has a good amendment on the lisbon process, which is not getting off the ground. it is important that we resolve these issues, because the new member states will soon have to deal with the euro. the commission has already made a number of amendments to this effect, including those tabled by the committee and by the members from the new member states, which i believe have enriched the report. it is important that we strike a good note with them when the euro is introduced in those countries, but also with the citizens of the present euro member states, because we are faced with their judgment. that is also why i ask the european central bank to report to us annually. not just in banking jargon and economics about how well the euro is doing, but also about what the euro has really meant to the average consumer. it is regrettable that the ecb, or at least mr gonzález páramo, has informed me in a letter that this raises a great many methodological problems. i understand that, and this letter was also written for the crisis we're in. i would, however, be very grateful if the european central bank would reconsider this request. i think the european commission should also jump into that hole. it is too easy to think 'the euro is here, let's just do it'. the average citizen will not accept that. it's a problem we can solve. given the way you always appear to us in committee, mr trichet, i think you are the one who is in a very good position to solve this problem and, above all, we, parliament, the ecb and the commission, could do very well together.
president. - mr commissioner, i am very disappointed that we are not making any progress on air quality. action is really, really needed. the air quality needs to improve. the commission has worked very hard on a good strategy, but at the last minute it is turning its ears to industry, and therefore to the most conservative part of industry. the world needs clean technology. the chinese, indians and koreans also have problems with air quality. european industry can respond to this with innovation and a spirit of innovation. the environment is an important part of the lisbon strategy, as john bowes said earlier. environmental innovation is helping the european economy to move forward. this was recognised by the commission in february, but unfortunately it is not being put into practice at the moment. how can citizens have confidence in a european commission which refuses to tell polluters to produce cleaner? how can citizens have confidence in a europe that tolerates 370,000 premature deaths a year due to air pollution? what kind of europe is it that refuses to take action to improve this situation substantially? is that the europe you want? improving air quality is no easy task. the netherlands shows how difficult it is to meet the standards. the netherlands has made a great effort, albeit late, to comply with the european commitments, but it is clear that resource measures are needed to guarantee clean air. we need clean cars, clean diesels, clean production processes, but we must also note, unfortunately, that the rules of the internal market make clear policies difficult and sometimes even impossible. in view of the gravity of the situation and the commission's inability to act, i call on the member states to abandon the rules of the internal market in the overriding interest of public health. this is not a simple request, because i am very concerned about the internal market, but i am convinced that public health must be given priority over the market. especially now that the commission refuses to take the lead.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i share the commissioner's analysis of air pollution with my colleagues. however, i am disappointed, as mrs corbey is, that the thematic strategy has not yet been published. our colleagues are now asking for a date for that publication. we read in the newspaper that commissioner dimas was furious. i have noted this with pleasure, but i have not yet noticed much in this debate of mr dimas's fury. we are not going to be able to do that, because we are not going to be able to do that. if the european commission does not do that, you have a problem. there's no time to lose for the induction now. euro 5 pm two and a half is an extremely good strategy and belongs in the thematic strategy. if you want to regain the confidence of the public, you will have to make sure that you do everything possible to tackle and combat crossborder air pollution effectively. we can do that with the thematic strategy, but then we should not wait any longer and now strike nails with heads and go through. commissioner, we will support you in this, i promise you that, but we also expect you to do your best.
i am grateful to the president-in-office of the council for his reply. i would like to thank in particular mr rocard and mr mccarthy, who have done almost a mer à boire with this directive which has caused so much tension and on which there are a number of currents in this house representing the different opinions on this subject. the aim of this directive is mainly to prevent the trivial patents that have been passed on to the european patent office in munich in recent years, including software as such, which can apparently be patented. i understand, and we all agree, that the common objective of this directive is to make this impossible. in principle, therefore, the purpose of this directive is to improve and strengthen article 52 of the munich convention. as a lawyer, i went over that article again. legislation cannot be clearer than article 52 of the munich convention. if there is one simple thing, it is this article. the problem is that it is misinterpreted or at least interpreted differently in all the member states, which creates enormous legal uncertainty. it is very important to prevent trivial patents, because we have to protect and strengthen innovation and research, and therefore also competition and employment, within europe. without a reward, we have no inventors. so i think there should be a fair system of reward for inventors who can protect their ideas, their intellectual property. if not, i fear that after the labour-intensive manufacturing that is already leaving for china, a lot of research and development departments of companies will also leave. mostly from multinationals, followed by small and medium-sized enterprises. if that happens, i think we should regret that we in europe have such great difficulty in legislating, that we show a great lack of initiative and that we dare not legislate to strengthen our global competitiveness. i think it is a pity for our children, for our posterity, if we do not dare to do so. i hope therefore that a strong directive will be drawn up and perhaps that will be the case. what's the biggest problem? the main problem is that this house, the european commission and the council do not have democratic control over the european patent office, and that is what we want. that is why we liberals have tabled an amendment, amendment no 65, which calls for the whole of this directive to be rejected and for the commission to come up with a european community patent so that this house can control the european patent office. it is then covered by european rules and i think we have a legally correct medium and a legally correct organisation. we can then, and this is particularly in the interests of small and mediumsized enterprises, ensure harmonised legal procedures and clarity. at the moment, if a small company wants to sue because its patent has been infringed, it costs millions and a small company can never afford that. i hope that tonight we as the liberal and democratic group will reach a well-balanced position, but i hope that the whole directive will be voted down tomorrow so that the commission can come forward with a well-founded proposal for a european patent, embedded in a directive such as this, so that we have a harmonized and balanced directive.
we have a proposal on patents for software-related innovations, mr president, because the current practice is not optimal. different views on whether software can be patented or not make the legal framework less clear and the advent of new legislation in this area is understandable. the question is whether the common position offers a balanced solution which is desirable for both large and small enterprises. unfortunately, even experts have not been able to say whether the directive will promote or hinder innovation in smes because of the obstacles they face. the fear of undesirable effects among small innovative enterprises could not be eliminated. three aspects are of particular importance to smes. first of all, the accessibility of patents. the directive does not provide a solution to the high costs of obtaining a patent. secondly, the extent to which smes can protect themselves if they hold a patent. it could be that another company is breaking into it. is it practical for them to monitor that continuously? in addition, they do not have the human and financial resources to protect themselves when accused of infringing the patents of other companies. the directive in this form risks a considerable legalisation of software-related innovations. thirdly, there is the problem of high licensing costs when small businesses use software from a patent holder. in addition, it is unclear what effect the directive would have on the use and development of open standards software. the european parliament has tried to address these concerns with amendments, but the amendments have received little response from the council. the common position is now not convincing enough and the council's willingness to accept it seems to be nil. the common position must therefore be rejected.
the way in which the software patent proposal has been put forward is, in my view, a scandal. large companies like microsoft see money in software patents and they have helped draft the european commission's proposal through the business software alliance. this was discovered by chance, because the name of the author had been forgotten to be removed from the document. the commission's proposal was based on the intellectual culture between the administrators of the patent offices, the patent lawyers of large companies and the patent administrators of the european commission. the european parliament took corrective action in 2003, but the council of ministers, through dubious voting procedures, got the proposal back on the table. there was no qualified majority of member states and requests from several member states to reopen negotiations were quickly ignored. the debate on software patents is also questionable in the netherlands. while the parliament was misinformed by the government, dutch officials worked on the proposal within the eu committees. the dutch minister ignored parliament in the decision-making in the council of ministers. i therefore support the amendments rejecting this proposal. the european parliament is making a fool of itself in my view by tolerating the way in which this bill has been brought about.
mr. chairman, in 1991, microsoft was a small company that had to outdo the big ones. then bill gates told us that the industry would come to a standstill if all our old knowledge were subject to patents. some giants might then impose unlimited payments on newcomers. now that his company is one of the big ones, his lobbyists are telling a very different story. for a long time, opponents have been taking action against software patents. they have perseverance and good arguments. i agree with the foundation for a free information infrastructure. this foundation says that software patents threaten the freedom of software development and that software patents are bad for innovation because they promote monopoly and cause higher prices. software companies, internet stores, schools and consumers are affected. do we want to see computers handed over to one or more software providers? tomorrow, at least 367 votes are needed to amend or reject the council text. i call on all opponents of software patents to come out in force. and then we'll choose open source and creativity over concentration of power and monopoly.
in writing. the vvd members of the alde group abstained from voting on the directive on the control of potato cystitis, because they consider that this directive is not proportionate to the seriousness of the problem and, moreover, does not comply with the principle of subsidiarity. the implementation of this directive will entail high costs due to the many sampling operations required by the provisions of the directive. the directive will also lead to high administrative costs in connection with the data registers to be set up. in addition, it is likely that these costs will be shared differently between the public sector and the private sector in the member states, leading to distortions of competition.
in writing. the european parliament and the council have had intense and emotional debates over the past seven years on the presence of plasticizers in plastic toys. i regret that the debate was dominated by emotion and not by reason. i was very disturbed by this at first and second reading. the common position is not based entirely on scientific grounds. the tightening of the common position adopted today by parliament and already accepted by the council is even further from what can be considered acceptable on scientific grounds. i cannot therefore agree to this tightening. but i'm also realistic enough to see that a counter-vote is no longer relevant. that is why i abstained on the compromise amendments. for further explanation, i refer to my contribution to the debate of 5 july 2000. the arguments i used then are just as relevant today.
in writing. the rapporteur seems to be blind to the great disadvantages that the euro has brought, including in his own country. in the netherlands, the introduction of the euro was followed by large price rises, a fact that mr maatens' party colleague, finance minister zalm, has denied for years. it is also clear that this minister lied in parliament about the devaluation of the guilder. it is not surprising, then, that the dutch people are not happy with the euro. my party called for a referendum on the single currency at the time: the commission has already made a number of proposals. and now mr maaten thinks he can make up for this lack of respect for the opinion of the people with a propaganda campaign? then there are the campaigns to be carried out in member states which have not yet adopted the euro. this is the best part. member states decide for themselves what kind of currency they want. we would not like it if russia joined our campaign to introduce the ruble into europe. let the swedish, british and danish citizens decide. unfortunately, the dutch people were not allowed to express themselves in this way.
in writing. the only advantage of the euro is that people do not have to change money when they go on holiday in another euro country. for the rest, most people in euro countries see mainly disadvantages. they have to pay higher consumer prices and the value of their savings is falling. they are angry at the continuing cuts in public services that are being implemented to limit public spending and thus meet the requirements of the stability pact. mr maaten shows in his report that he is a lover of the euro, a 'euro-phile'. it is laughable that he calls the euro 'the most successful european project ever'. does he know that the euro was the reason for some people to vote against the eu constitution? how does he explain that member states without the euro are doing much better economically ? his priority is to support the governments of sweden, denmark and the uk in convincing the public of the success of the euro. such a weak offer is similar to the "yes" campaign for the eu constitution in the netherlands: the euro would be a fantastic product but people don't understand it yet. it is short-sighted and patronizing to prey on serious criticism of people with transparent campaigns.
report drawn up on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy. mr president, today is a great day for the european parliament. we are today emphatically pursuing the line of cohesion mr president, in our market economy, large, often international, companies are the ones who set the tone. reducing costs through specialisation and scaling up are key words. the natural advantages of the regions determine the location of the establishment. as a result, not every activity can be carried out profitably in every region. the circumstances often outweigh the financial incentives of the government. i therefore have no illusions that european structural policy will be able to perform structural miracles. it is too unrealistic, too inflexible and too limited in scope. this does not exclude the possibility that european structural policy may, under certain circumstances, contribute to a temporary reduction of regional and local economic bottlenecks. practice has shown that. the reports of our colleagues are therefore worth at least some of our support, with special attention to some of the bottlenecks. i am thinking in particular of the possibility of including private resources in the calculation of cofinancing, the necessary involvement of regional and local partners in the preparation and implementation of the plans, and the effective and efficient use of the available resources within the time limits set for this purpose. any reduction in the total budget by the council will have to be borne by all the regions. in the future, we will have to focus on the knowledge element rather than the production element. that is our strength, as practice shows. let us therefore leave room at every level to exploit this strength and to use the stronger regions to improve the situation in the weaker ones.
