in writing. the vvd is pleased that an overwhelming majority of this house has supported the alde amendment to reject the common position. the vvd is against a sectoral approach to european patent law. the vvd favours an integrated approach and calls on the european commission to come forward with a proposal for a single community patent. this provides more legal certainty. the current cumbersome and costly system of granting patents can thus be replaced by a more effective regime. we call on the european commission to start the debate as soon as possible in the legal affairs committee on the harmonization of existing european patent legislation on the basis of article 95, which would also give parliament a co-decision in this area, which would also allow democratic control of the epo. we also call on all the lobbying organisations to use the same heavy artillery to persuade the french government to abandon its opposition to a single official language for the community patent in order to offer a competitive and innovative future to french youth. the lisbon objective is not just a matter of words, but of deeds.
the european union is not a party to the conflict, but it is a party to the peace process. this safe haven was an indefensible island in the midst of the much larger serbian republic which was and is considered by another population group as its own safe haven. the commission has not yet taken the necessary measures to ensure that the inevitable surrender does not turn into a retaliatory act and even into a massacre. my group does not believe that the final outcome would have been really different if there had been a centralised common foreign policy of the european union or if there had been a mandate that would have allowed the use of more force by dutchbat and support by aerial bombardments. if we want to guarantee the peaceful coexistence of the three peoples in bosnia, we must seek solutions which are agreed by all concerned and not by one or two groups which we prefer. only a federal model which recognizes diversity, like belgium and switzerland, can do justice. we must start from the reality that the north and east are strongly oriented towards serbia and the south-west towards croatia and that these areas together constitute the majority of the population. that is why bosnia-herzegovina needs open borders with its neighbours. any attempt to create a centralised unitary state can only lead to a permanent struggle for power between the various groups of the population. no one should be able to subjugate another group. that must be the lesson of srebrenica and the chaotic functioning of the dayton peace agreement.
mr president, we are of course thinking today in particular of the most horrific event in recent european history, the murder of thousands of men in the muslim enclave of srebrenica in bosnia-herzegovina in 1995. the story of srebrenica has had a huge impact in europe and the world, but especially in my country, the netherlands. dutch blue helmets then proved unable to provide the protection that citizens, residents and refugees needed. and ten years later, that debacle is still the subject of conversation in the netherlands. the feeling of horror and sympathy for the victims of this mass murder and their relatives is great. our thoughts go out to those who are most concerned that the co-responsibles for this crime, the then bosnian president karajic and general mladic, are still at large. but remembering must also go beyond sympathy for the victims. we have to learn from the lessons and try to look ahead. the first is our commitment to the balkan countries, which we must support in overcoming the experience of the wars they have experienced. with the past in mind, we must also look to the future. the european union must provide support within a clear framework in which the prospect of integration into the eu, as promised by the european council, plays an important role. eu support will help to build peaceful, stable and prosperous societies. it must also be clear that the main bearers of the future are first and foremost the balkan countries themselves. fortunately, there is now peace in bosnia-herzegovina, but the past has not yet been fully dealt with, and this is an obstacle to the functioning of the state and society. important matters such as the return of refugees and the arrest of war crimes suspects are being hampered. for me, coming to terms with the past is also an important part of working towards the future. a second lesson is broader, and it is a lesson that has been much discussed in the netherlands. the 'never again, not in europe, but especially not in the rest of the world' means that vigilance is required to prevent dormant conflicts from escalating into bloody and hopeless situations. we must be more alert and act more quickly and, where necessary, more forcefully when scenarios like srebrenica threaten to develop, to prevent european troops from ever again being in a situation like dutchbat was in ten years ago.
the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. how could what happened in srebrenica happen and what are the lessons we should learn from it? srebrenica is not only a painful past, srebrenica is also a painful present. residents who still cannot return and languish in refugee camps. children who are being presented with a totally one-sided picture of what happened ten years ago in school, which leaves out a much-needed reconciliation. the identification of victims is so necessary to help relatives accept the terrible fate of their loved ones. identification is expensive and therefore often too slow. what is needed now is not only attention to what happened ten years ago, but also attention to what is happening in srebrenica today, attention, understanding and, where necessary, money to save srebrenica today. the second srebrenica is fortunately not as likely to happen in the balkans today, but the wounds that were inflicted on the entire balkans at that time can only be healed structurally if the balkan countries know that they are welcome in europe. i therefore support the position of the european commission, which has said that the prospect of membership for the balkan countries remains. the balkans are in europe, srebrenica is in europe, let us never forget that lesson.
president. - my group's view of the people's republic of china and the government based on the island of taiwan has been simple for a long time. in 1949 an old, incompetent profiteering clique that was unable to provide a solution to poverty and injustice was rightly driven out by the people. they had fled to an island where the majority of the population did not feel chinese and which was long excluded from china until 1945. on that island, they established a military dictatorship that attempted to launch attacks on the mainland. eventually, that old regime lost its international recognition and was expelled from the united nations. in the meantime, on the mainland, all sorts of interesting experiments were taking place, such as the industrialisation of the countryside and a radical reorganisation of agriculture. the arrival of factories, railroads and dams helped the country move forward. unfortunately, in those experiments, like the great leap forward and the cultural revolution, great mistakes were made because of inexperience, and many people lost their lives. that seemed like the price of a good future. the chinese are now, under the leadership of the heirs of this revolutionary regime, still a country with a future, but the political line has been completely changed. the people's republic is now a super-fast-growing country, but one with great inequality, with death penalty and no democracy or freedom of association. the model is still similar to what has been abandoned in south korea and taiwan. taiwan has developed from a military dictatorship to a parliamentary democracy increasingly similar to europe and japan. the democratic process also includes the desire to have a non-chinese, independent taiwan. in seeking a solution to a 56-year division of what is internationally recognised as chinese territory, we will have to take account of these new developments. only in this way can we help to find peaceful solutions for the future.

on behalf of the pse group.– mr president, i wish to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs kauppi, on her well-balanced report, which is supported by most of the political groups and a large majority of britain. my group was not very happy with her approach. in the explanatory statement of this report you can still find a reminder of her approach, which was very much anti-legislation and principally and vehemently against a directive on clearing and settlement. my group and the ppe-de group do not exclude the need for a directive. this was also the starting point in the andria report from a previous parliamentary term. we have chosen not to engage in an ideological battle about whether to have legislation, but to list pragmatically the issues to be taken into account politically, to assess the necessity, feasibility and desirability of legislation or other forms of regulation, and to wait for the factual impact assessments that the commission is currently carrying out before making up our minds definitely. this is a more pragmatic and wise approach and i am happy that we managed to achieve it. nevertheless, i wish to repeat some concerns. i should like to see a decent clearing and settlement market, particularly in relation to possible consolidation of stock exchanges in europe. i have noted that the bundeskartelamt has just given the green light for a possible merger of deutsche börse and the london stock exchange, although this is no longer a real option. the uk competition commission will very soon deliver its opinion on this possible merger or on the other combination of euronext and lse. however, we have already raised the question of whether the european commission should be more proactive and not leave it to the national competition authorities to judge the developments, because the financial markets – in particular the post-market activities – can have an enormous impact on these markets. at least we should monitor the various aspects from the point of view of competition and the impact on the smooth functioning of the european financial markets. my group does not exclude ex ante competition rules as a consequence. unbundling of different elements in the clearance and settlement value chain is not something we propose at this stage, but i would like to have more assurance that cross-subsidies do not exist in this industry and that custodian banks are free to transfer securities outside their home conglomerates. let me finish by saying that cesr – the committee of european securities regulators – has already been active on this issue, which has not always been welcomed by our committee, because what it was doing was not very transparent. it must be said that at least it has been proactive. i hope we can improve on the cooperation and the further elaboration of this regulation on clearing and settlement.

i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr president, for all the work she has done in cooperation with her colleagues. i would like to make a few comments on the proposal which will be put to the vote. it seems that some members expect very much support for nature policy from the european union. i share that hope, because i too believe that the european union is making a positive contribution to our continent by creating networks of nature reserves. but we must not expect all the good in europe, especially when it comes to the financing of these nature reserves. it is the national authorities, assisted by european experts, who designate these areas. it is therefore acceptable that part of the funding for the conservation of these natural areas should come from the european budget. however, ultimately the responsibility lies primarily with the member states. in my view, it is quite understandable that the funding is falling short of what is needed, as is also emphasized in several amendments. we must only do and finance in a european way what the member states cannot do themselves. we should not turn it around by wanting to pay for everything and then see if the member states want to contribute something. i therefore advocate giving more weight to funding from the member states and only taking action when eu funding is really justified.
president, the global textile sector has undergone a profound transition since the abolition of import quotas in january 2005. the spectacular growth of chinese textile exports is paralleled by the deep crisis of the textile sector within the european union and in developing countries such as bangladesh, cambodia and laos. i share the rapporteur's concern about the strong growth in the volume of chinese textiles on the european market and call on the commission to monitor closely the implementation of the bilateral agreement between the european union and china for the period 2005-2008. i would stress that this agreement is only the beginning of a fair and well-functioning global textile market. the european commission must continue its dialogue with beijing to make it clear that a liberalised world market can only function on a level playing field. the commission has not yet taken a decision on the application of the principle of equal treatment for men and women. there can be no question of ignorance, given the precision with which beijing points out to the european union its wto obligations. the rapporteur rightly calls attention to china's compliance with labour directives. in many factories, workers, including many children, are forced to work under miserable conditions. the european commission must pursue an active policy to improve the labour situation in china. this is of great importance not only to chinese workers but also to textile workers in neighbouring countries. textile companies in these countries are being forced by chinese expansion to reduce production costs even further in order to remain internationally competitive. i am disappointed, however, that the rapporteur is too one-sided in his view of the future of the european textile industry in the light of a euro-mediterranean area. the european union should not focus on cooperation with one region in the complex global textile market. moreover, the conclusion of a trade agreement with a country like syria is unthinkable in view of the current human rights situation. mr president, the european commission must clearly identify the opportunities and threats within the global textile sector without import quotas and formulate a clear policy for the european textile sector after 2008. only a commercial and innovative european textile sector can maintain a competitive position on the world market.
president. - i too offer my condolences to all the victims. since 1 january 2005, wto members are no longer allowed to apply quotas for the import of textiles and clothing. the impact on the european market is enormous, both for importers and for producers. it is good that the commission has sought a solution to this dilemma in dialogue. this measure gives european manufacturing industry some breathing space. that's all it is, and that's all it can be. the wto does not allow it and it is not expected that this will change. european producers will now have to anticipate the new situation. what is needed is a strategic response to the long-term problems. the european textile and clothing industry, and not only the industry, is facing a structural problem. there are many products that are produced or can be produced in china, or perhaps in other countries, at lower cost. speaking of china, a big problem is that china doesn't follow wto rules. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the free movement of persons. this allows it to produce cheaper, which leads to unfair competition. this really needs to be dealt with. ensure that existing rules are respected. as for the poor countries, many poor countries have built up a huge dependence on the textile and clothing sector in recent years. they too have difficulty competing with china and are losing jobs. the least developed countries must not be the biggest losers. furthermore, the interests of importers and producers who anticipated the situation from 2005 onwards and have now in some cases even made large financial commitments are not sufficiently taken into account. they must not be the victims of a rescue operation for a part of the manufacturing industry. they have a right to predictability and legal certainty. finally, mr president, of course we can protect our trading position, but always in line with the wto, with a responsibility towards poor countries and with a responsibility for fair trade and respect for human rights and the environment. this should apply to all wto members. my compliments to mrs. saifi.
rapporteur.– mr president, i should like to clarify that, by way of this report, we recommend that the european parliament reject the agreement on the transfer of passenger data between the eu and canada. that does not mean that the agreement cannot go ahead, even if we vote against it, because we are only being consulted here. you may recall that the european parliament last year rejected a similar agreement with the united states on grounds of both substance and procedure. the agreement was signed nevertheless and this house subsequently took the commission and the council to court. the case is pending and a court ruling is expected by the end of this year. i need to underline that the agreement with canada is much better in substance. we recognise that a good deal has been negotiated by the commission. however, the commission chose the identical procedure as it did in the case of the agreement with the united states. both the european parliament and the national parliaments are effectively being sidelined. the commission should have chosen the assent procedure instead. the committee on legal affairs unanimously endorsed the view that the commission has chosen the wrong legal base. if parliament were to accept this procedure now, it would basically undermine the pending court case. i reiterate, by voting for this report, the council can still go ahead with the agreement. in other words, rejection by the european parliament does not in any way jeopardise the agreement but, at the same time, parliament remains coherent with its earlier positions and will not undermine the court case. therefore, i kindly ask you to vote for this report. (applause)
president. - when my group is concerned about zimbabwe, we do not think in the first place of the white farmers who became rich in colonial times, but of the great black majority who still have a much lower income. president mugabe has played a major role in the freedom struggle against colonial rule and the white minority regime of ian smith. in that liberation struggle and afterwards, he has always presented himself as the hero of the black masses. in practice, he did far too little to improve their lot for years. he seemed to have become a moderate driver, only radical in his dislike of homosexuality. he was hardly more recognizable than the former leader of the independence struggle who gave the poor hope of a vastly changed society in which the equality of all people was paramount. it was not until long after he came to power that he became strongly radicalized. he is especially radical against anyone who threatens or could threaten his autocracy. one by one, the political opposition, the rich peasants, the poor slums and the market merchants fell victim to his intimidation and his activities aimed at their destruction. in a previous debate on zimbabwe, on 16 december 2004, i suggested that mugabe had launched these campaigns to compensate for his loss of popularity. he tries to win and mobilize new followers by his unexpectedly decisive action. now, his approach no longer seems like a clever tactic, but the behavior of someone going insane. instead of providing the poor with better homes, he drives them out of the cities by demolishing their homes. this is going to look suspiciously like the insane regime that pol pot wanted to impose in cambodia 30 years ago. my group regrets that the resolution before us is an exhaustive critique of our neighbour south africa and the african union, but that it does not seek to resolve the problems with them. as long as europe is seen in africa in its old role as a colonizer, we lack the moral authority to contribute to improvement.

president. - mr president, we have already established in the previous debate that zimbabwe is hell, but the same is true of guatemala, and has been for a long time. since the coup d'état of 1954, which deposed a government that had wanted to free guatemala from poverty and backwardness, almost nothing has ever gone well in guatemala and there have been continuous governments in power that did not care about the weakest groups in society or human rights. they have a long tradition of using violence and other forms of intimidation against anyone who wants to improve the situation. it is in keeping with the backwardness of the last half-century that the law does not criminalize child trafficking, that government documents are forged, that many murders go unpunished, that human rights activists are severely hampered, that 56% of the population lives in poverty and that the indigenous population has little opportunity to participate in political decision-making. not only in zimbabwe, but at least as much in guatemala, there is every reason for the european union to contribute to radical change. my group therefore wholeheartedly supports the resolution.

in writing. the conflict over chinese textile imports into the european union is a clear illustration of the effects of the global free market economy. competition leads to a race to the bottom in terms of working conditions. chinese workers work six long days a week. in these inhuman conditions it is no wonder that they outstrip european clothing production. european workers then find themselves on the streets, making it a 'double loser'. in addition, in the poorest countries, 70-80% of the workers in the clothing sector are women. free trade is only acceptable if countries are somewhat equivalent to each other in socioeconomic terms. until then, the fierce competition between companies will continue to claim new victims in the form of mass redundancies and unacceptable working conditions. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. the undersigned wish to emphasize that high environmental and safety standards are more important than competitiveness. it is therefore unacceptable that the high reach standards for chemicals should be downgraded in order to remain competitive. human welfare is always more important than economic success.
the budget is a matter of priority, and the committee on development and the social democrats have made the millennium development goals a central priority. one in five of the world's citizens does not have access to basic education and health care, and the gap is widening every day despite all the promises of government leaders. europe is the world's largest donor and can make a difference. that is why we are not asking for more money in the first place, but we are asking for the prioritization of the millennium development goals within the eu aid budget. at least 35% of the budget must be spent on this and at least 20% on basic education and basic health care. within the various geographical budget lines we have set aside 20% for these two sectors and we want to give that to the european commission only if they submit to us specific plans for the basic education and basic health sectors in those regions. in 2003, only 2.3% of the development budget was spent on education and 5.2% on health. that is unacceptable. today, with the help of our colleagues in the committee on budgets, we want to bring the millennium goals closer. "make poverty history" is what i'm saying.
i agree with the president-in-office of the council on one point this afternoon, mr president, and that is that we must be as economical as possible with money and that we must have a realistic budget. as regards the question of budgetary austerity, the president-in-office of the council is probably also aware of the statistics showing that over a ten-year period european expenditure, which is the responsibility of the european parliament, has increased by 8%, while expenditure by the member states has increased by just over 23%. i do not think it is the council's job to teach us a lesson in the matter of sound budgetary management. and then the discounts, as always, they're predictable. there will always be shortages in agriculture. i understand that this year there will be cuts because certain budget lines are high and can therefore be cut. since the amsterdam treaty we have had the ad hoc procedure. we shall receive - as the council itself says - a realistic estimate in october. why not wait for that? the council also wants to reduce the number of pilot projects and preparatory actions. why would you do that? there are some very good measures and, especially at the moment, with the bird flu, we will have to do more in this area. finally, i would ask the president-in-office of the council whether he thinks that the council is keeping to the agreements reached in 2004 on the regular information of parliament by the council on the common foreign and security policy, particularly at high level. i think the council has not kept its word.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) mr president, it is really important that children receive the right medicines in the right dosage and in the right form. the commission proposal is of great importance to children, to parents and to industry. pharmaceutical manufacturers should be fairly compensated for the research they carry out on medicinal products for children. i think that it is a very generous compensation, but in many cases a six-month extension of patent protection is extremely generous. i've been looking into it with a reputable british innovation company, and research into childhood indications usually costs around eight million euros. the revenues from six months of patent granting are a multiple of this, about 200 to 300 million, and there are even much higher amounts mentioned for blockbusters. i therefore call for proportionality: 3 months, but if the turnover is less than 100 million, an additional 3 months can be obtained. 3 + 3 is also 6, but only for companies that really need it. these amendments were tabled by me and linda mcavan and have been signed by 44 colleagues from our group, from belgium, estonia, france, portugal, greece, poland, hungary, the united kingdom and the netherlands. we believe that the reward should be commensurate with the cost incurred. that is a very simple and normal principle. it is not worthwhile to make huge profits on the backs of sick children. and it's always good to think about who's paying for it: that's the drug users and the insured. the cost of health insurance is rising, with medicines accounting for about 10% of total public health expenditure. the bill for medication does not have to be unnecessarily expensive. money is better spent on health care than on fun perks for industry. of course, the pharmaceutical mr president, terrorism is not a new phenomenon. it is an old phenomenon, used to disrupt a society and cause unrest and instability. unfortunately, acts of terrorism are already a well-known phenomenon in several member states, dating back to before islamic terrorism. for example, the fight against terrorism in the 1970s and 1980s was therefore accompanied by the views on data protection that prevailed at the time. our time calls for new methods and techniques to deal with terrorism, and in doing so we want respect for individual freedom and the guarantee of security for our society. i wonder, therefore, whether in the light of the old ways of detecting and combating terrorism, our notions of privacy are not going too far. i understand that many of my colleagues are reluctant to give their approval to far-reaching measures. but the limitations and objections raised here in parliament do not make it easy to work with data. i would therefore ask commissioner frattini whether there are any concrete measures that could be taken to increase confidence in the proposals of the european commission and the council. i am thinking of data storage in closed computer systems that do not communicate via the internet. i call on you to make clear and concrete commitments that will take parliament's mind off the matter.
mr president, mr frattini and mr clarke, europe must provide its citizens with security, but real security, not just a false sense of security. we do not need grand statements. we urgently need action: closer cooperation and exchange of information; full and rapid implementation of agreed measures; not just more data collection, but also much better use of the available data. europol and eurojust must finally be allowed to play a full role in combating crime and terrorism. i welcome the coherent vision set out by mr frattini, with a proper balance between security and liberty, stressing the need for european cooperation for more effectiveness. frankly, i find it much more convincing than the one-sided wish list for tougher measures by the presidency. the member states argue that they take these measures to protect our democracy, but they do so by circumventing parliamentary scrutiny because it is terribly inconvenient. i also firmly reject any suggestion that the convention on human rights should be watered down. i believe that we are on a slippery slope here. to speak of striking the right balance has become a commonplace. no-one will say that the want to strike the wrong balance, but tellingly the presidency's paper does not even have a chapter on civil liberties. let us not forget that people are nowhere safer than in our open, free and democratic society. i should like to raise three specific issues. with regard to passenger data, mr clarke insisted on legal safeguards against abuse, but we all know that the pnr agreement with the us lacks precisely that. eu citizens have no proper means of redress in the event of mistakes and abuse. no-fly lists are another concern. on what basis are people included on such a list? who has access to these lists? if someone is on a list for the wrong reasons, how can he be taken off the list? the usefulness of data retention for investigations and prosecution is undisputed, but the need for blanket surveillance has still not been convincingly demonstrated. furthermore, much more needs to be done in the area of human intelligence, that is, the old-fashioned spies, rather than focusing exclusively on modern technology, even if that is sexier.
i think that we are having a very important debate today on the measures we must take together, particularly to combat terrorism and terrorists. i appreciate the pragmatic approach, the focus of minister clarke when he says: "we must get a number of things done in the six months that i am president of the council responsible". but i also very much appreciate the commissioner's comments on the social context in which we have to deal with the problem of islamist-inspired terrorism in particular. i myself would like to say something about the partly political aspect of the fight against terrorism, because i think there is a major entanglement between domestic and foreign policy when it comes to terrorism. and the threat of terrorism we are facing is a paramount issue where our external and internal policies are intertwined, not only in the physical sense when individuals and groups from outside the union come to us with terrorist intentions, but also more so because we are dealing with networks and ideas that have little attraction to borders. these ideas often explicitly focus on the relationship of europe or the west to the rest of the world. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem, and i am sure that the commission will take them into account in its proposals for the future. we cannot meet these threats with measures focused on domestic security alone. we must therefore give them a place in our foreign policy, and that goes beyond mere cooperation with the police and security services of third countries. we must also look at our relationship with and our policy towards, in particular, the countries in the muslim world. the time has come for a different kind of realism from that which has hitherto governed our policy. in many countries, corruption and mismanagement pose a much greater risk of radicalisation than politics itself, and we must therefore make the democratisation and modernisation of governance the spearhead of our politics. we share this objective with many americans, but we also need to avoid the radical approach that many americans, particularly neoconservatives, are taking on the democratization of the middle east. it would be worthwhile to look at the example of the osce from europe, to see whether this organization which we have developed in europe would also work in the middle east. and, of course, consistency in our actions is essential, both in terms of the policies of the various member states and in terms of consistency between words and deeds. if we can contribute to solving problems in that region in this way, it will also benefit the relationship between different communities in our own community. our foreign policy must provide us and the majority of moderate muslims with arguments in the debate with radical elements. we must translate the dialogue with the countries in the islamic world directly into our dialogue with the muslim communities within our own society. only in this way can we isolate the small minority who now think that they can achieve something with horrific acts of violence within their own faith community. our foreign policy is an essential piece of the internal security puzzle.
the balance between individual freedom and the security of society as a whole is an essential part of our democratic order. but this balance must never be beyond criticism or beyond discussion. i agree with secretary clarke that in this time of increased threat that balance needs to be reviewed for the protection of collective liberty. it is indeed important that a crossborder approach be adopted, because, as elmar brok has just said, insecurity and crime do not stop at the border. you are making a comment on the approach to asylum, to smuggling, to people and to drugs. these were the core elements of the constitutional treaty, and i am glad to hear that you are interested in them. i hope that in this time of what you call the european wake-up call, the british presidency will at least make a full effort to implement these key points of the constitutional treaty. then there's the balance between freedom and responsibility. as our group chairman, hans-gert poettering, has said, most muslims in europe are very attached to our european values. but if you want to tackle the small individuals who are not attached to that, you have to be bold enough to draw the line more clearly. i would like to make two points in this connection. firstly, we must have the courage to expel radical imams who come from abroad, from outside europe, to brainwash people here. your government in england has made proposals along these lines at national level. so does my government in the netherlands. do you not think that, given that we have open internal borders, that this clear approach should be followed throughout europe? will the commissioner, together with the presidency, promote this? second, the recruitment for the jihad. that's a problem, because we have freedom of thought. but the recruitment of people for jihad will have to be tackled more forcefully. is it not time we started to criminalise the whole of recruitment as such in our member states? we can hardly handle this now. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information it receives. some countries ban organizations, some do not. do you not agree that throughout europe, really bad organisations should be dealt with in the same way everywhere and that terrorist organisations on the european list should be equally clearly thwarted throughout the union? now look at the practical, everyday life. i'll give you one example. hizb ut-tahir, a radical muslim organization that is against democracy and for radicalization. this organization, which is active in europe, is strictly prohibited in germany. but in the neighbouring netherlands or austria, this organization is also active and is not hindered. this can't be the europe of 2005! can we expect you to deal with such organizations more europeanally, to draw a clearer line there and to finally dare to draw a clear line with regard to terrorist organizations such as hezbollah? the last point concerns the transfer of information. as president of the council, you have the right to ask our parliament to reconsider data retention. but may we then address what the terrorist coordinator gijs de vries says every time, namely that he is unable to get intelligence services to exchange information because of the lack of cooperation from member states? let's be honest here: he says that the problem, particularly on the british side, with the british secret service, is that this information is not exchanged at european level. can the president-in-office of the council state that he finds this unacceptable and that the british presidency is making it a priority that in future information be exchanged automatically between the secret services when it concerns the safety of us all, as agreed after the attacks in madrid and new york?
mr president, it has already been said that the two speeches that we have heard this morning differ somewhat in the approaches they reflect. mr frattini spoke about attacking the political and social structures of terrorism. he spoke about dialogue between cultures and religions and about the promotion of fundamental rights and freedoms, not just as a goal but as a strategy to combat terror. mr clarke's words reflect a more practical approach: he outlines concrete and practical measures to strengthen the police and justice systems. both are necessary and both are equally valuable. terrorists have struck in new york, madrid, london, turkey and amsterdam. when i was in morocco just after the terrorist strikes in casablanca, the streets were dominated by enormous billboards with the text ne touche pas mon pays – do not touch my country, do not touch my neighbours, do not touch my fellow citizens. this is also the response that we, as europeans, need to give: 'do not touch europe'. it does not matter who you are or what excuse you think you have found for your perverse and nihilistic, violent ideology. if you hurt our fellow citizens, we will find you and you will be punished. mr clarke is right, the lack of faith in the european union structure reflects a lack of trust in its ability to act. in order to be effective, however, we need so much more than a strong police force. we need a fundamental understanding of the problem we face. we need a joint insight into the networks that recruit young men and women in our inner cities and in our prisons. we need a common stance on democracy and rights and a common strategy on their promotion in europe and beyond. we need to abandon the simplistic idea that promoting security automatically means limiting fundamental freedoms. we also need to forget the simplistic notion that limiting privacy automatically promotes security. in order to convince european citizens of the value of our measures, we need to take the measures that we can prove are necessary. the case for further data retention has not been sufficiently proven; the case for human intelligence and for understanding the problem has. we need a dialogue on the place of islam in society and about the future of multiculturalism – we need frattini's approach. however, we also need the down-to-earth approach that i read between the lines in mr clarke's words. 'do not touch my country' – it does not matter who we are, what our religion is or where we are from: if you hurt our fellow citizens, you hurt us. do not touch europe or the values it represents, the values that allow us all to live together in peace.
on behalf of the ind/dem group mr president, a political party cannot carry arms, that is not the case in any democracy. these are the wise words of the high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy, mr solana. but they do not correspond to the reality in the gaza strip, the west bank and lebanon. for both the palestinian islamic terrorist movement hamas and its lebanese strategic ally hezbollah are now eating out of two wallets. they are fully involved in the day-to-day political business and are perfecting their military formations. with this dual strategy, hamas and hezbollah are not only sharpening internal palestinian and lebanese political relations, but are also deliberately aiming at an armed confrontation with the state of israel. what a sad result of the unilateral israeli withdrawal from gaza: the european union is also concerned about the intifada, or the continuing armed chaos in the palestinian territories, and the looming prospect of a new lebanese civil war. a true middle eastern nightmare, especially when we take seriously the current hamas media campaign. the european union is not prepared to accept the palestinian people's right to selfdetermination. given the critical situation in the israeli-palestinian conflict, i am very curious to see what the council's position is on hamas and hezbollah. are these islamic terrorist organizations - let us say openly warring parties - quasi-normal political interlocutors for the european union? i would much rather hear what concrete steps the council intends to take to dismantle the so-called military branches of hamas and hezbollah. i am also entitled to ask the council to do so on the basis of un resolution 1559 on hezbollah and the european union's own list of terrorist organisations as regards hamas. this is the opportunity for the european union, the council and the commission, to realise its ambition of being peacemakers in the middle east.
i would like to begin by congratulating my colleague mr queiró on his report and the excellent cooperation which has resulted from it. it is very good that a vision for sustainable tourism is now being developed at european level. and in the knowledge that most of the measures have to be taken by the member states, we must also recognize that there are problems that europe has to be concerned about. tourism is a very important sector for the european citizen and also for our european economy. this is an ambitious report and i would therefore like to know whether the commissioner will be willing to deal with it and what proposals and measures the commission actually intends to take. it is not just a task for the commission but also for the council. the report also calls on the council to speed up the decision-making process on the reduced vat rate. i have been very vocal in this respect, because the application of the reduced rate of vat is very important for the tourist sector. the jobs involved are very high and the reduced vat rate for hotels should be made permanent for all member states. they should also be able to reduce the vat rate for restaurants in a structural way. it is a pity that the council is not here because i was actually very curious whether the council expected to reach agreement on this before the end of the year. perhaps the commissioner can still comment on the report and perhaps we as parliament and the commission should put a little more pressure on the council to finally take a decision, because these proposals are very important for employment, especially in this economic age. i support the call for a quality classification for the tourism sector, but we must make full use of the expertise of that sector. finally, our committee is not called the committee on transport and tourism for nothing, as mr queiró has shown by this excellent report.
i would like to thank mrs svensson very much for her report and i would like to mention two points. the dutch parliament will debate further measures against female circumcision at the end of this month. the government has taken over from the public health council a number of good advice on the prevention and prosecution of circumcision. that shows the importance of the attention paid to this subject and to the report by mrs svensson. the netherlands is known as a progressive country, but a clear policy on circumcision has taken too long to be implemented. however, the dutch government still does not opt for a reporting obligation for health workers, because it fears that citizens will avoid the doctor if he becomes part of an enforcement process. but we cannot be so permissive in dealing with this serious form of abuse and we therefore support the call in the svensson report for the obligation to report. in addition to the differences in health care between men and women and the need for a gender-specific approach to health complaints identified in the report, there are many other areas where women and health have a special relationship and where gender-specific effects can be expected. these should be identified before new regulations are introduced. in the netherlands, the parliament decided two months ago to have a gender impact assessment carried out for the new health care system to be introduced on 1 january. i recommend that the european commission also have such gender impact assessments made in policy areas that are of real concern to women and that it submit them to parliament before new healthcare legislation is adopted. that can be seen as supplementing paragraph 11 of mrs svensson's report.
president, mr svensson has produced a very interesting and comprehensive report, which deals with many aspects of women and health. i'm not a doctor, but i understand that a lot of comments are pointing the finger at the sore spot. i am impressed by the concern expressed in the report. the report shows a great deal of compassion for the individual who cannot get justice in any way, especially when it is the result of abuse, crime or addiction. i wholeheartedly support the call for an accessible health care system. however, in stark contrast to all the positive aspects of this report, i find paragraph 21. whereas the report pays great attention to the well-being and health of adults, paragraph 21 below does not guarantee this for people at an earlier stage of their lives. it seems as if the rights and care of adults are more important than the same issues for people whose existence is just beginning. to me, the life of every human being is equally precious, from the very young to the very old. so i would like to advocate that we remove those elements that differentiate between care at different stages of life. in this way, the positive message of this report can also benefit people who are not yet able to defend their own interests. i hope, mr president, that the members will remove this stain from this excellent report.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group.– mr president, i would like to make a few comments on the report on behalf of our colleague, mr maštálka, who, due to urgent duties in the czech republic, could not attend this part-session. first, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr bowis, on his report. it is a well-balanced, well-written and well-presented report. secondly, we are talking about a problem that directly or indirectly affects three-quarters of the world's population, and its possible solutions. this means that immediate practical steps need to be taken and financial and other help provided. third, these diseases require a complex approach. cooperation is needed between international organisations, ngos and the private sector, as well as dedicated individuals such as researchers and petitioners. for maximum effectiveness, there needs to be complete dedication, attention and support, both financially and in the form of know-how and experts from the industrialised countries, and close cooperation with developing nations, based on partnership. fourth, the problem of neglected major diseases cannot only be associated with poor developing countries. some of the european union's neighbouring states are not considered developing countries and yet their epidemiological situations and possible solutions are similar in nature to those in the developing world. belarus, ukraine and moldova, as well as russia, have serious problems with hiv-aids, tuberculosis and other diseases. we should include them in our debate and when considering financial and other forms of aid. my fifth point is that, although we have discussed the threat of bioterrorism in our committees and at our part-sessions, i wonder whether we are prepared for the inevitable spread of some of the diseases mentioned in this report. there are often disaster scenarios that link the spread of some tropical diseases and their mutations to global warming. there are even warnings that some diseases such as malaria might affect our continent. do we want to wait until our lives are directly affected before we act? people in the developing world are human beings too and it is our duty to help protect them. we should not narrow down the issues presented in this report to the accessibility of drugs and cures for diseases, but should also take into account the situation of those less fortunate than ourselves. thank you for your attention.
the commission is therefore proposing that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. nevertheless, hiv/aids, malaria, tuberculosis and various other diseases are still not receiving enough attention. in some cases there are not enough safe and effective medicines and if they are available, their price is often such an obstacle that they are not accessible to the sick. the bowis report underlines that as a result, poor people are almost exclusively affected because there are no viable markets for the drugs. and, moreover, lack of investment in research and development can lead to a strong rebound, as we have seen with tuberculosis and malaria. joint efforts at international level are essential to change this. this shows the importance of the forward-looking approach, which is also advocated in the bowis report. some diseases are not always given sufficient attention in developing countries themselves. nevertheless, there may be initiatives in europe to support the fight against the diseases. i'm also thinking about leprosy. should the report not have paid more attention to this disease? is the european commission currently taking various initiatives to combat leprosy? one point of interest in the bowis report is that we are making sure that the approach of the european union in cooperation with the member states is realistic and transparent. however, the call for obligations on governments and industry imposed by the international community says relatively little about concrete improvements in the field of work. health is not enforceable, but it is the result of improving care that is borne locally by the population. finally, visibility is important. despite the strong interlinkage between disease and poverty, it is not prudent to confuse the fight against specific diseases with the objective of poverty reduction in general. transparency is crucial for parliamentary scrutiny of the nature of policies and their effectiveness. i would like to thank mr bowis once again for his stimulating report.
author. mr. president, china today is still ruled by a party that calls itself communist. the communist party and its leadership have not been communist for many years. today, the only thing that counts is the pursuit of economic growth and export advantages on the world market. this is achieved by low wages, poor working conditions and the failure of independent trade unions. this is very similar to the dictatorial model that was used to industrialize south korea. the extent to which the mass of the population benefits from this is only apparent after decades. in the meantime, a society has emerged with great differences between people in terms of control and income and with great neglect of nature, landscape and the environment. it has nothing to do with the protection of man and the environment, which is our priority as socialists. these chinese leaders are no longer communists, but unfortunately they have retained the worst features of the communist state tradition. they did not tolerate independent organizations and other power centers alongside them. people may have a religion, but they don't have to get organized. the leadership fears that independent churches may become a source of opposition. a mixture of atheistic tradition and power politics leads to the government deciding which religions are allowed and under what circumstances. it is well known that the official catholic church is not allowed but only a nationally controlled variant. less well known is that the jewish religion, which was practiced before the 1949 revolution in the major cities of the east coast, is not allowed at all. we must not allow this china to threaten its neighbours or violently suppress domestic opposition. the maintenance of the arms embargo must therefore be taken for granted. there should be no question of european union member states being allowed to sell arms to china. all attempts to lift the arms embargo are reprehensible. no one is interested in a new arms race that will waste money and create new threats. that is why europe must also commit itself to disarmament.
author. mr. speaker, a short, telling quote from a letter from an anonymous chinese protestant: "i really want to get spiritual food". the author says that he can no longer receive this in the official protestant church, because it is under the ideological control of the state. it is precisely this governmental violation of religious freedom in the people's republic of china that has led to a growing stream of thriving, unregistered protestant house churches. they face a wave of arbitrary government repression with china's new religious rules coming into force on march 1 this year. rough disruptions of worship services, heavy fines, arrests, demolitions of private homes, beatings or reprimands in the vein of 'teachers cannot believe in christianity.' shocking was the account of the august 7 raid on a protestant house congregation in shenyang province . among the thirty churchgoers were about a dozen women. they were to undress and then pose nude. women who resisted were viciously beaten. recent research into the responsibility of the central government or local authorities for violations of religious freedom in china has brought out a bright light. some local authorities sympathize with and protect fellow christians for their loyal contribution to local economic and social development. a practical example for the chinese central government to follow, because it always determines what exactly are normal religious activities and only those are legally permitted. i would ask you, commissioner, or your colleagues or the council to explain this ominous criterion for the fundamental right of freedom of religion to the chinese authorities very carefully and very clearly. what are normal religious activities? above all, i hope that you and the council will work for the release of all those who, regardless of their religion, have been imprisoned or suffer from the lack of freedom of religion in china.
the commission is also aware of the fact that the council has not yet adopted a decision on the application of the principle of subsidiarity. in that view, lebanon, jordan, israel, palestine and parts of iraq should also be part of syria. in syria, france and britain, who conquered these territories from turkey in world war i, are blamed for needlessly fragmenting it and thus making impossible the return of a larger syria, as it existed before the ottoman empire. to be fair, syria was a leader in the formation of the united arab republic more than 40 years ago, along with egypt and yemen. when this project failed and the golan heights were lost to israel in 1967, the soil for an authoritarian and militarist regime was created in syria. the regime is intolerant of ethnic minorities, christianity and different interpretations of the regime's version of islam. it is also intolerant of internal opposition, suspicious of its neighbours and even more so of great powers far away. the syrian territory has supported violent movements of exiles from other countries over the years and there is a suspicion that political assassinations in lebanon have recently been organized from syria. the european union is seeking intensive relations with all the states in its immediate vicinity, including all the countries bordering the mediterranean. if turkey ever joins the european union, syria will become a direct neighbour, just like iraq. my group believes that economic or military considerations alone should not play a role in further intensifying contacts with syria. the european union must put more emphasis on the release of political prisoners, the right to opposition and the democratization of the administration. how does the commission do that ? we must understand the circumstances that have brought syria to its present bad situation, but we must not rest on our laurels. the democratization of syria now takes priority over the intensification of trade relations. the criminal regime of the al-assad family should not be rewarded for bad behavior. with that attitude, we contribute to a democratized syria. the recognition of a palestinian state is also important.

mr president, i am quite right to be modest as someone who was born in the netherlands and also born in freedom after the second world war. i think that today is the moment for people who have not lived under the yoke of communism to pay their respects to those who, in particular in poland, rose up in 1980 against a regime which we all found reprehensible. it was a very special episode in the history of poland and it is very good that we are commemorating it here in this parliament, in strasbourg, today. it is an important part of polish history, but it is not a polish affair, because the example of solidarność and solidarity has also caused a lot of discussion, impact and movement outside poland. i would say, 25 years of solidarity, is also in my country, the netherlands, 25 years a concept. what was so special was that the workers in gdansk did rise up in a shipyard, went on strike, but were also supported by a group of leading intellectuals who were willing to stick their necks out and oppose the regime in poland. i think that this was actually an example of the best of the workers' movement as we have known it in europe, the democratic workers' movement which has also meant a great deal in our countries, when it comes to freedom, equality and emancipation. i think that was also a source of inspiration for many, not only in eastern european countries but also in western europe, which also often led to surprise, at least for us in the netherlands, about the courage of the strikers in gdansk to oppose the communist regime in such a way. we now know, and of course all this has been said in retrospect, that the work of solidarity was also the beginning of the end of a fantastic revolution in the history of poland; we can now say that poland has finally found its place, free in europe, where it belongs. it is poland that should be the focus of attention, in my view. the polish quest for freedom and its history enrich our continent; the story of the polish plumber becomes even more poignant in the light of this historical role. solidarity is still alive in poland and in the hearts of poles, as is evident today, but it deserves to be remembered in europe as a shining example of european solidarity.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to play a full part in the development of the world economy. people must not be subordinated to the state or to their employer. we must be free to make our own choices and organize society from below. that was the ideal of the socialists in the 19th and early 20th centuries. they realized that in a society where one group oppresses another, no one can be truly free, not even the people who are part of the oppressing group themselves. in that view, those old socialists, including their great thinker karl marx, would certainly have applauded the resistance of the workers in poland in the 1970s and 1980s. any authoritarian regime that cuts wages and benefits and maintains the privilege of a small group deserves to be overthrown by the resistance of the masses. usually, workers' resistance is directed against the profit-hunger of big business or against a government that considers itself right-wing. in poland, it was not like that at all. the state was based on socialism, on the ideas of marx, on the communal ownership of the means of production and on the equality of all. it was not the product of the workers' struggle but of the demarcation of military spheres of influence between the victors after the second world war. the people have experienced the reality of that moment as the exact opposite of what socialists say they are striving for. that's part of the reason i was a speaker at a meeting in the netherlands in the mid-1980s in solidarity with the suppressed independent trade union movement in poland. i have never shared the view that the workers should be satisfied if their rulers call themselves socialists or communists. the legacy of solidarność is now being invoked by people with very different views, both inside and outside poland. for some it was a return to the conservative poland of the pilsudski period between the two world wars, for others it was a necessary step towards replacing the caricature of socialism that had been built up after 1945 with a genuine socialist democracy. the first view seems to have won for the time being; even with this outcome, which i do not regret, i consider resistance to unacceptable governance an inalienable right of all people and solidarność an inspiring example.
i understand, mr president, that i can also use mr goebbels' speaking time. i do not want to say too much about the content of this dossier, as a number of colleagues have already done, but i will say a few words about the procedure. but first, a personal note that i must take to heart. for outsiders, this is an incomprehensible technical dossier with no less than 800 amendments, a package of at least 10 cm thick. what are they doing in brussels now, you might ask? where is the outrage, where are the sharp pieces in the press? but yes, this is not about protecting workers from skin cancer from too much sunlight - which is something anyone can talk about. in the last session, everyone spoke of shame. no, this is about protecting invested capital. is that such a different and more noble goal? can europe intervene in this matter in any detail? so no selective outrage this time. fortunately, that goes for me, too: i have nothing against this file. but let us not double-standard the protection of workers in the future. all right, then, this report. i am glad and also proud that we were able to prepare this complex dossier, including the extension to trading book rules, in such a rapid and efficient way in our committee on economic affairs. all those involved deserve great credit for this. the united states in particular is watching with wonder and envy our resourcefulness. this is the way it has to be, to make europe competitive and attract investment for growth and jobs, that is the lisbon strategy. however, the way we are doing this now, with this gigantic number of amendments and technical annexes, is not a way that appeals to me and not the way that i think is consistent with the quest for better regulation. we have developed a more intelligent approach to financial market regulation in the so-called lamfalussy procedure. legislation on guidelines and technical details is delegated to the committees of officials in the comitology and to the european supervisory associations, which in turn do so in a very intensive dialogue and consultation with market parties and other stakeholders. this is necessary not only to relieve us as employers, but also to enable us to respond much more flexibly and adequately to developments in these dynamic markets. i am a strong supporter of this approach and believe that it can also provide good solutions for better regulation in other areas. the paradox is that in this capital adequacy directive we have not yet followed the lamfalussy method, but are now fixing everything, including the annexes and mathematical formulas, at the level of the joint legislators, but we want to condition and tie to a deadline the possibility of introducing this dynamism and flexibility after the directive has entered into force. it is not because we are against the method - i repeat that explicitly - but because the lamfalussy procedure still lacks a fundamental condition: the call back right for parliament. i think that needs to be emphasized again and we want to do that with this directive to increase the pressure to obtain that formal right of call back. how this happens is not important to us, the problem was already there in 1999, before conventions were drawn up to prepare for treaty changes, and the problem must now be solved structurally. the council is moving and we hope that the ecofin ministers will also make it clear to their colleagues from the general affairs and foreign affairs councils that a solution must be found, whether it is to the left or to the right. i think that should be the clear signal of this discussion.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. a few points: we have been able to create more room for national solutions in audit committees, which are supervisory committees within the company. we have stressed once again that corporate governance is a matter for negotiation in the member states and that there is no room for unambiguous solutions from europe. we have succeeded in making rotation of accountancy the rule and rotation of accountancy offices the exception. we have been able to reduce the administrative burden on smes in particular in a number of areas, and the commission has promised to present a report on liability before the end of 2006. commissioner mccreevy has just said 'i will have a further study done in the future', but we have agreed that a report will be on the table by the end of 2006 and i hope he will also give me his assurance that this will happen; we are also anxious to discuss this matter in this house. it is a problem, the liability, there are great differences between the member states, we will have to find solutions here. all in all, a very important piece of legislation which, in my view, has become a little more pragmatic and also more applicable in practice in the last six months, that is what it is all about. there is one more important hurdle to be overcome, and that is the comitology, as you have already pointed out, commissioner. we have drawn a line on this, including with the radwan report. i think that the same interests are at play here. i would like to say again, in response to a comment made by one of my colleagues just now - 'i do not want to play games of power between the institutions' - that in my view this is not a game of power at all, but simply a modern form of legislation. we must legislate in broad outline and leave the details to the commission and to experts, but that can only be done on condition that we as parliament have the right to revoke it, the right to call back. that, i think, is just the most important thing. i remember that just before the referendum in the netherlands on the constitution, which as you know went wrong, there was a big article in one of the dutch newspapers and the headline above it was: "in brussels, the officials have the power". it was all about executive committees where behind closed doors, all kinds of legislation is being implemented here. that is the typical example which only serves to increase the disgust of the citizens of brussels, where everything and everything else takes place behind closed doors. so i think that if we are going to talk about this comitology - and i am glad that the council has announced that it will take initiatives - we will have to make very clear improvements in this area. i also agree with the deadline, the horizon of 1 april 2008, which is also mentioned in the radwan report. i would point out that we are making an exception to article 26, which deals with the adoption of international accountancy standards, because we are dealing with international agreements in this respect. of course, if there is a new regulation on comitology, the right of call back will also have to apply to this type of accountancy standards. i am not going to end, mr president, without thanking my colleagues for the very constructive way in which they have worked together. i think we have had six months of very useful negotiations. i would also like to thank the commission and the council for their constructive approach and i think we can be very pleased with the results achieved.

the liberalization plans, mr president, have a profound impact not only on railway staff but also on the quality of services provided to passengers. the close cooperation between national railway companies and their neighbours is much older than the european union. this makes possible the rapid cross-border long-distance trains with the corresponding ticket sales. that european success is now being undermined by increasing competition. then railway companies first rejected the least profitable and least subsidised tasks. while everyone thinks we are uniting europe, they are retreating to mass transport in the large urban areas and to a few main high-speed routes within a single member state. many crossborder routes are now only served by stop-over trains. night trains are being phased out or disappear altogether. only in germany is european ticketing and information at a high level and only the brussels-paris cross-border high-speed line is frequently used. elsewhere, it is becoming increasingly difficult to buy tickets to far-flung foreign destinations. the number of stops along the way is increasing, and waiting times are increasing. there is less and less attention paid to the proper handling of passengers on delayed trains from a neighbouring country, who are particularly in trouble at night. for the passengers of international rail transport, it is an annoying maze. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the railway sector is not left behind. i have shown on many occasions over the past six years, by means of practical experience, how this rail transport is falling into disrepair. i therefore welcome the european commission's proposal to finally provide some counterbalance to the negative effects of liberalisation and commercialisation. unfortunately, the rapporteur, mr sterckx, has not taken the crisis in international rail passenger transport as a starting point, but rather the annoyance of railway companies who prefer self-regulation to a european enforceable obligation to achieve the german level of quality together. his proposals reduce the improvements for cross-border transport and unnecessarily increase european interference with domestic rail transport. this proposal misses the opportunity to improve the position of international passengers and to stop the deterioration which is already taking place. my group is concerned that the regulation, as mr sterckx wants it, does not sufficiently solve the real problems and that it will have to be revised within a few years. to avoid this, my group is tabled a number of amendments aimed at ensuring that european railways act as a single entity towards the public and that shortcomings can be corrected more quickly.
