member of the commission. mr president, firstly i would like to thank parliament and the committee on international trade for putting this very timely and important issue on the agenda and i would also like to express my personal appreciation to the rapporteur, ms lucas, for the report. the commission highly appreciates this report and sees it as a very valuable contribution to its policy making. china's rise is a challenge, but it is also an opportunity. both aspects are well reflected in this report. when the report says that chinese expansion will bring enormous beneficial possibilities but also raises legislative concerns for european industry, i can only agree, and it is in precisely this balance that we have to act. let me give you a few facts regarding the latest strengths of our trade relations with china. it is clear that china is a big economy and will gradually become competitive in more and more industrial sectors. just like the eu or the us, china is likely to produce a broad range of industrial products. the challenge for europe is not only to trade airbus planes for tee-shirts, but increasingly to trade similar products within the same sector, so that european firms prosper as a result of their products' distinctiveness and quality. for example, parts of europe's textile industry remain very competitive. at present europe's overall trading position remains strong due to its ability to sell upmarket products. the eu is the first exporter of goods and services and the first direct investor abroad. this is a key point for the linkage between competitiveness and the eu social model. the eu produces value-added upmarket products and can sell them abroad due to settled non-price factors such as innovative features, quality and reputation, continuity over time or related services. however, the eu position is under challenge. the european industry is losing ground in high-technology products. it is trailing behind in several high-tech products, whereas china is rapidly catching up. the eu position is also at risk due to the geographical orientation of its exports which are strong in countries where demand is static, but less well-positioned in rapidly growing areas such as asia. it is more a race to the top than a race to the bottom. eu companies cannot afford to miss market opportunities in these dynamic areas which already account for half of world import growth. europe's future is to focus on what we do well. the eu has a strong global export profile in high-quality and high-tech products and services which now account for about half of european exports and a third of world demand. however, continued innovation and investment in this comparative advantage is crucial. better respect and enforcement of intellectual property rights at international level is therefore of the utmost importance. addressing barriers to eu exports in third countries also accounts for the bulk of the potential to improve the competitive position of eu industry. the eu stands to win from the further opening of markets worldwide. by negotiating the removal of tariff and non-tariff barriers and promoting regulatory dialogues with our trading partners where appropriate, the eu can open new markets for its exporters. therefore the commission's lisbon strategy, the strategy of growth, jobs and competitiveness set out last february, rests on three pillars: opening markets to drive productivity and innovation; investing in education and research to enable us to compete; and reforming labour markets and welfare states to get people into jobs, improve employability and skills and extend opportunities to those whom economic developments are leaving behind. we acknowledge the efforts china has undertaken to implement its wto commitments as it enters into its fifth and last year of transition after its accession. we also have to make clear that unsolved issues have to be addressed. in sectors such as automotives, telecom and banking there is a clear call for action on the chinese side to get fully in line with its commitments. on our side we must use the whole range of instruments at hand to push these cases forward both bilaterally and multilaterally. for good reason, this report gives special attention to lack of intellectual property rights implementation in china. this report and the public at large are right to say that while we acknowledge china's competitive advantage in particular in labour-intensive production, it is only just that our intellectual property is equally acknowledged and protected. we are not only speaking about cds and handbags, but also machinery and hi-tech products. china has made progress in adopting adequate ipr laws; we now have to focus on issues of implementation. the conclusion of a customs cooperation and mutual assistance agreement between the european commission and china, which entered into force on 1 april 2005, is an important concrete development in this field. the fight against counterfeit and piracy, including the protection of intellectual property rights and security of the international supply chain, are the main eu priorities for closer cooperation in the customs area. these issues, among others, will be discussed at the first meeting of the joint customs cooperation committee on 14 november 2005. joint activities are already ongoing in the area of intellectual property rights where a working visit of a team of eu custom experts is scheduled at the invitation of the chinese customs. more generally, the newly established eu-china working group on ipr will concentrate on concrete business issues. we stand ready to assist china on this way forward. because, as the last oecd report has shown, it is very much in china's own interest, i am grateful that parliament strongly backs our report on this issue. as an advocate of high social standards the commission believes it is in the interest of all countries to ratify key ilo conventions and other relevant international agreements. we believe that high social and environmental standards are fundamental for sustainable development and cooperate with our trading partners to this end in all relevant instances, be they bilateral or multilateral. the commission supports china's recent efforts to take greater account of environmental considerations and is actively engaged with china on ways to develop further environmental cooperation including technical assistance in the area of trade and the environment. we believe the carrot of cooperation to be much more effective than the stick of clauses and conditionality in trade agreements. on several occasions this report mentions the need for in-depth knowledge of opportunities to do business with china. the commission has taken the initiative and launched a tender for a study and a seminar on the future opportunities and challenges of eu-china trade and investment relations, also comprising ten sectoral analyses. the result will be presented during a conference in 2006 and will be yet another important input for our way ahead. i also want to mention the prospects of negotiating a new framework agreement with china, reflecting the full breadth and depth of the strategic partnership between china and the european union. this framework agreement will of course have an important and forward-looking agenda on the trade and investment side.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs. mr president, on 13 july the committee on foreign affairs of this house adopted almost unanimously, let us say with one abstention, its opinion on the report by mr lucas, on behalf of the committee on international trade, on the prospects for eu-china trade relations. the committee on foreign affairs concentrated its opinion on the question of the arms embargo and the observance of human rights in the people's republic of china. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting is based on the principle of the free movement of goods. and there are three main reasons for this. firstly, there has been no substantial progress in the respect of human rights in china. second, the adoption of the anti-secession law in march 2005 by the chinese parliament leaves open the option of military intervention against taiwan. thirdly, the committee on foreign affairs stresses that the lifting of the arms embargo could lead to a serious undermining of regional stability in east asia. the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs is clear on the protection of human rights in china. it is deeply concerned about the continuing violations of such fundamental rights as freedom of expression, freedom of assembly, freedom of religion. it is not without reason that the committee here relates directly to the ongoing council discussion on the possible lifting of the arms embargo. if the council were to decide this unexpectedly, it would certainly not encourage the chinese authorities to finally be honest and serious about respecting human rights. what remains, mr president, is a considerable loss of international prestige for the european union in respect of its own fundamental values. as rapporteur for the committee on foreign affairs, i hope that her position on the lucas report will be echoed and followed by the european institutions.
mr president, there is a great fear of china in europe, but this is not the time to be passive and conservative. we have to accept that sometimes jobs will be lost to emerging economies. we must show solidarity with colleagues around the world. they too have a right to employment and to increased prosperity, but the european social model requires that we provide adequate opportunities for people who lose their jobs here, help them to find a new job, and provide training and retraining. they must not be victims of these rapid developments. the european fashion shops and importers whose orders were stuck in queues for long periods as a result of the import ban also deserve compensation for their financial losses. in the meantime, the european union can and must take advantage of the opportunities that prosperous economic growth in other parts of the world is creating for us. new consumer markets, positive prospects for the tourist sector, also suitable for lower-skilled workers. european companies have the right to protect their intellectual property in law, but certainly also in practice, and the right to access chinese markets. instead of taking protectionist measures to protect these jobs, the eu should defend the interests of its companies by upholding china's wto obligations. above all, the eu must come out of its shell, stand up for the standards and values that we hold dear. fair trade, freedom of association and labour rights. in china, working conditions are miserable, workers are not free to form a union. trade unionists are arrested and put in jail for years. tens of thousands of fatal industrial accidents each year are not uncommon in the chinese workplace. workers are forced to work countless hours overtime, and child labor is used. as one of china's largest trading partners, the european union has a responsibility to address these shortcomings.
in writing. in the coming decades, the end of the fossil fuel supply is approaching. as a major energy consumer, europe must prepare for the shift from fossil fuels to alternative energy sources. the turmes report focuses unilaterally on wind farms and biofuels. these so-called renewable sources of energy are not even part of the solution. such energy sources have already in the past consumed a disproportionate share of the european budget without ever being or becoming profitable. it is time for europe to turn its attention to renewable energy which will eventually meet the needs of our industries and consumers and to break free from the grip that the environmental lobby has been placing on us for years. we must stop being naive and dare to invest in nuclear energy and fusion. in recent years there have been gigantic scientific breakthroughs in these fields, but europe is in danger of missing the boat in this field because thinking about nuclear energy has remained stagnant here for the past 20 years. we cannot afford to do this, just because in china, for example, a new nuclear power station is being built every six months.
president. - after a summer of difficult negotiations between commissioner mandelson and china on the global textile sector, it is time to replace sectoral ad hoc policy with a sound vision of the long-term trade relationship between the european union and china. the enormous growth rates and the prospect of china becoming the world's largest exporter should not prevent a factual analysis of european trade relations with china. the opportunities of the chinese market must be seen in the right perspective. china is primarily focused on capturing the growth potential of domestic demand itself. beijing is particularly keen to promote its own activity, not shying away from encouraging price alignment or dumping temporary surpluses on the world market. another problem in the trade relationship with china is the large-scale infringement of intellectual property. china is too much in the tradition of the chinese philosopher confucius. it says that imitation of the master is commendable. the european union must make this a top priority. a climate of piracy and counterfeiting not only harms european companies but also hinders the innovative nature of local chinese companies. the european union should press for an expansion of the intellectual property expertise within the chinese judiciary. i believe that europe must dare to compete with emerging economies like china, but on a level playing field. even the world's largest exporter has not only rights but also duties.
president. - in the first years after the french colonial period, almost 50 years ago, tunisia seemed to be an example of a modern and tolerant democracy. health care and women's rights have since been favorably compared to other arab countries. the only negative factor in this positive development was the glorification of the man who considers himself the father of the fatherland, habib bourgiba. his authoritarian traits were later adopted to a greater degree by his much less popular successors. these successors constantly point to threats against which the state must protect itself. in order to maintain a stable state free of opposition and a free press, freedom and democracy have been progressively restricted in a number of ways. the result is that non-governmental organisations cannot operate freely and that people who are critical of the situation are sent to prison. europe still holds a soft view of tunisia, but it is time to make clear that the country is developing in a direction that will take it further away from europe. i think that my group should be able to say that more clearly than in the resolution we are now proposing.
member of the commission. mr president, the european commission is devoting great attention to the human rights situation in tunisia and is using all the means at its disposal to encourage the tunisian authorities to make progress in this area. we are particularly concerned at the breaches of fundamental freedoms, such as the freedoms of expression, association and assembly. the european commission, notably on the basis of the provisions of article 2 of the association agreement, has systematically raised the importance of respecting human rights at the highest level in a bilateral context with the tunisian authorities, including at the last meeting of the association council held on 31 january 2005. the european commission delegation in tunis is closely monitoring the worrying situation regarding the tunisian league for the defence of human rights, the significant difficulties faced by a number of other ngos and professional associations, as well as the independence of the judiciary. direct and regular contact is maintained with the different sectors of tunisian civil society and the tunisian authorities to overcome the current difficulties. the commission fully participates in the eu démarches to support human rights in tunisia, notably the recent eu declaration and the troika démarche on the obstruction of the activities of the tunisian league for the defence of human rights. the entry into force of the eu-tunisia neighbourhood action plan marked an important step forward, since it includes extensive commitments in the fields of human rights, democracy and governance. it establishes as priority actions the pursuit and consolidation of reforms guaranteeing democracy, respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, and the independence and efficiency of the judiciary and, most important of all, it provides for the creation of a human rights sub-committee where compliance with these commitments will be assessed and monitored. the establishment of an institutional dialogue framework of this kind, which is expected to be in place by the end of the year, should enable human rights to be discussed in a more structured manner. concerning the reform of the judiciary, a meda project on the modernisation of justice is being finalised with a view to specifically addressing the existing shortcomings and the need for better access to justice. a number of human rights-related projects are also being funded in tunisia under the european initiative for democracy and human rights. most of these are experiencing serious problems in their implementation, as the tunisian authorities are deliberately blocking access to eu funding for ngos, including the tunisian league for the defence of human rights. all efforts by the european commission to unblock this situation have been unsuccessful so far. i should like to reassure you that the european commission will continue its efforts to ensure that the human rights provisions of the association agreement and of the eu-tunisia action plan are duly implemented, as well as to impress upon the tunisian authorities the need to allow ngo access to eu funding.
president. - more than a year ago, on thursday 16 september 2004, this house also debated the worrying situation in vojvodina, the threatened position of the hungarian minority in that serbian province. a day earlier, a hungarian girl from subotica/szabadka made a shocking discovery. ready to go to school, 13-year-old klementina sötét opened the front door that wednesday morning, september 15, 2004. she backed away because she thought she saw blood on the door. clementina ran in with a start. upon closer inspection, the word "death" was found written in red and cyrillic on the door. there was another knife in the middle of the door, about 15 inches long. on the newly painted walls of the sötét family home, it read: "hungarians, you will die". as far as i know, the sötét family is currently living in a refugee camp in hungary. she could not stand the anti-hungarian atmosphere in vojvodina. we're a year ahead of schedule. sadly, not much further in the figurative sense. the hungarian human rights foundation reports in its report last month that since the end of january, when a fact-finding mission of the european parliament was in vojvodina, no less than 32 new incidents have occurred there, specifically targeting minorities. sure, that's depressing. i know. especially for the hungarian minority in the area. but this house has an additional reason to speak out openly for them, and not just in the defensive sense of preventing and punishing acts of ethnic hatred in vojvodina. the european parliament is also in favour of a positive interpretation of the rights of national minorities. i therefore maintain my questions to the council and the commission from a year ago: what is the situation with regard to language teaching and other cultural facilities (think, for example, newspapers, magazines and other media) for the hungarian minority in vojvodina? and how much money does the serbian government spend on public services for this specific ethnic group? mr president, i look forward to the hearing in this house on the political situation in vojvodina. a fine initiative by our delegation for south-east europe under the inspired leadership of its chairman, mr pack. the serbian authorities also learned that the european parliament would only give its consent to a stabilisation and association agreement between brussels and belgrade if the hungarians and other minorities in vojvodina could establish a genuine democratic state governed by the rule of law.
president. - on the map of the former austro-hungarian dual monarchy of 1856 hanging on the second floor of this building, everyone can see how ethnically divided vojvodina was then between hungarians, croats, germans, slovaks, romanians and serbs. when hungary was considered one of the losers of the first world war in 1918, it had to cede not only a number of territories predominantly inhabited by other peoples, but also typically hungarian territories such as northern vojvodina. since the division into serbia in 1918, vojvodina has steadily become more serbian and other groups have moved away or been expelled. since 1989 the autonomy of the large hungarian minority has also been severely curtailed. after the series of violent conflicts in the states of the former yugoslavia between 1992 and 2001, everyone hoped for a peaceful coexistence of the survivors. unfortunately, in vojvodina, where bridges have been bombed but no ethnic war has been fought, non-serbs are being harassed again by their neighbours. serbian refugees from croatia, bosnia and kosovo are housed in homes of others who can no longer support themselves in vojvodina. it's not even so much about deliberately organized state violence. we see violence and intimidation by ordinary citizens everywhere where there is a feeling that many of our own people are being excluded by state borders and that many others who were once seen as occupiers have been left behind in their own countries. the opinion of the serbian public is that the serb population of northern and eastern bosnia and northern kosovo are administratively unjustly cut off from the motherland. as a compensation, the aim is to make the areas that are recognised as serbian territory fully serbian. anyone who opposes it is seen as an enemy and associated with former turkish, german, hungarian occupations. the resolution we are proposing reiterates the concern expressed a year ago. but let us not forget that both serbs and hungarians will have to be better protected by clear and broadly based agreements on language rights, autonomy and administrative boundaries. only in this way can future outbreaks of ethnic hatred in and around serbia be prevented.

on behalf of the ppe-de group. - mr president, i should like to begin by expressing my sincere thanks to doris pack, who became the member of the european parliament for culture for the year 2005 last night. doris will be partly responsible for the way she's been acting as an ambassador for the circus. i would also like to thank commissioner figel, who has given a very good answer to the questions in mrs pack's report. charlie chaplin once said, "i remain just one thing, and nothing only, and that's a clown. it places me on a far higher plane than any politician". mr president, we politicians must deal with this. but there's also another saying, coming from william shakespeare, who says, "it is meat and drink to me to see a clown". both statements indicate how highly a circus performer like the clown is valued. and, indeed, who does not have fond memories of clowns, acrobats, jugglers, but also of dunkers. the circus is a product of its time, but it is deeply rooted in our european culture. the circus has existed in its present form since the 18th century. but even centuries before that, acrobats and jugglers populated markets and fairs. they moved from place to place to display their art. and that hasn't changed much. but this! the commission is also proposing that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. given the role of cities as development areas of knowledge and economic activities, it seems useful to me to record the specific influence of cities on the achievement of the lisbon objectives. i am glad that the rapporteur is paying attention to this. the rapporteur is also right to point out that both the commission and the member states can contribute to urban development, the commission in monitoring the side effects of general economic and regional policies on cities and the member states by drawing up development plans for their cities and urban areas. although cross-pollination is necessary and possible here and there, this balance alone seems to me to be in keeping with the principle powers of both member states and the commission. i can therefore wholeheartedly support the calls for member states to pay more attention to urban policy. i therefore sincerely congratulate the rapporteur on this fine report.

in writing. it is naive to think that the member states of the european union can build a wall around themselves that protects us from economic threats from outside. in the long run, free trade offers the greatest prospect for prosperity and stability in the world. any attempt to protect one's own economy will not be worthwhile in the long run. the report takes little account of the developments that china has undergone and ignores the fact that as china develops further, there will be more attention paid to the position of minorities and the environment in the country itself. the pressure from europe and the finger-pointing will only backfire. the problems of textile imports are typical of the schizophrenic approach of the european union. first we encourage free trade and then we go back to setting quotas which are unworkable for entrepreneurs. moreover, it is not advisable to maintain the arms embargo and thus to put china on a par with rogue powers such as north korea and iran. if europe really wants to build lasting trade relations with china, we must sit down as equal partners and put our complacency aside.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the waste is disposed of in a manner that is compatible with the common market. the committee on the environment has adopted 90 amendments, and the council has accepted 50 of them in full. 17 amendments have been replaced by 12 compromise amendments and the remaining 23 have been dropped as part of the overall compromise. i think that the result is justified, especially when we consider that the position of the european commission on the legal basis requires unanimity in the council. i would like to thank the council and in particular the british presidency for the constructive and pleasant cooperation. the same is certainly true of the shadow rapporteurs of the other groups. i am glad that in such a short time a compromise package has been reached, which has received such broad support from the groups. in the last stage of the negotiations we have mainly discussed the two most important aspects. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. i want to say something about this. first of all, enforcement. we have had a regulation on the shipment of waste since 1993. it appears that this regulation is not being properly applied. there are a lot of illegal waste shipments. you are probably familiar with the example of a british company which was caught illegally exporting to china in march this year as many as 60 shipping containers of rotting and smelly household waste. the forged documents indicated that it was a matter of recycling old paper. last week the uk environment agency announced that a survey of shipping containers found that 75% of waste shipments were illegal. in france, in june 2005, it was even found that 100% of the waste shipments checked were not in order. we can conclude from this that it is very important that there are clear rules for waste shipments and that structural good enforcement is needed. so not just an occasional enforcement action. the compromise package now includes an obligation for member states to carry out checks, including checks on paper and physical transfers. in addition, member states are obliged to cooperate in the prevention and detection of illegal shipments of waste. the council also agreed to the deployment of permanent staff from the member states to ensure that this cooperation is effectively established. it therefore provides for the support of regular physical checks on actual shipments. i would ask the european commission to continue enforcement actions within the framework of the impel network and to structure them into the organisation. then there's the matter of abandoned ships. all the groups have expressed concern about the dismantling of scrapped ships. as many more ships will be scrapped in the coming years due to the phase-out of single-hull tankers, global anti-dumping measures in, for example, asia are essential. unfortunately, strict measures on the safe and environmentally responsible decommissioning of ships taken unilaterally by the european union can easily be circumvented by changing the flag of a ship to a non-european union flag at the end of its service life. the ilo and imo must therefore work hard to achieve mandatory conditions which will provide an effective solution to the problem of abandoned ships. the council has stated that it will increase the capacity for the decommissioning of ships in the european union. the council also declared that it would make every effort to reach the global agreement on the decommissioning of ships mentioned earlier. as much as i sympathize with some of the amendments tabled by the greens, i must, as rapporteur, recommend that they be rejected in view of the agreement reached with the council. finally, mr president, i would urge the european commission not to bring another case before the court of justice on the legal basis. i think that the environment is not being helped by a legal battle between the european institutions. that is bad for the progress of the implementation of this regulation. i hope that the european commission will also see that the revision of this directive is of greater importance, and i count on the support of commissioner dimas in this.
i would like to thank mr blokland for his excellent report. he has devoted himself to the problem of waste and has shown himself to be a staunch defender of environmental quality. waste must be transported for processing and recovery. if it's good, we'll have less and less waste. more and more of the things we throw away and discard are given a second or third life. for a waste to be processed in a meaningful way, it needs mass. in order to be profitable, waste must be of a certain size. the waste markets have therefore become cross-border and common rules are therefore urgently needed. the question is first of all, what legal basis do we choose? i think it is clear that the first priority is to protect the environment. the aim is not to strengthen the internal market and i am glad that the council has taken parliament's position. i am also glad that the council recognizes that there is a problem with the dismantling of ships. it is all too easy to put an abandoned ship to the dock for the last time in a country where environmental regulations are not so important. unfortunately, waste treatment is still a sector with a reputation for illegal practices, whether justified or not. in the interests of the waste sector, illegal practices must be tackled. waste treatment is a normal and respected activity, vital to society, the economy and the environment. let us then deal with it as carefully as possible and member states must work together to combat illegal transport and practices, but there is of course no point in setting the administrative bar too high. small shipments of waste, for example, waste generated during transport, do not require complicated procedures. in addition, under producer responsibility, operators should be able to take back parts themselves without first filling in a whole pile of paper. we can agree on that pretty quickly. the pse group supports the compromise package put forward by mr blokland and we will in no way support the green amendments on animal waste. finally, much remains unclear in the waste sector. european legislation is still fragmented and consists of a large number of fragments. there is a need for framework legislation. framework legislation to promote waste prevention, reuse and recycling. legislation that gives clear guidance to entrepreneurs and shows where the long-term environmental objectives lie. legislation that also provides clear definitions and, above all, promotes innovation in all member states. legislation which will also promote employment in the waste sector, because that is what we urgently need. let us hope that we do not have to wait too long for the proposals for the framework legislation.

environmental protection. if not, the environmental legal basis is more appropriate, as it allows member states to take further action themselves. europe is not here to impose deterioration on the member states or to deprive them of the possibility of pursuing a decent environmental policy. europe is there to improve living conditions together. the legal basis for the environment is also clearly less complex and therefore also fits into the better regulation project that we all want. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected. of course, there must be a good environmental reason for a ban. in the committee on the environment, there was a strong majority in favour of limiting the use of f-gases. many companies are working with for more than 30 years, the european communities and then the european union have been expanding their membership and eating up other partnerships. since the great enlargement of 2004, when more new member states suddenly joined than in the 1970s, 1980s and 1990s combined, something has changed. before, every enlargement was a success, but now enlargement is seen by many as a threat. the example of spain and ireland, which quickly filled in all the gaps with generous financial contributions from the european union, will probably not be repeated. many newcomers become dependent on the export of cheap agricultural products, of cheap mining products and especially of cheap labour, while having to import expensive new technology. in the old member states, increasing payments to the newcomers and, above all, rising unemployment are expected, possibly due to low wage competition. nevertheless, the new member states are still lagging behind. romania and bulgaria were decided in april to join as the last of the 10 newcomers in 2004. i now hear comments in this parliament about drawing a definitive external border for europe, about limiting the solidarity contribution from rich to poor member states, about national referendums allowing the present member states to reject newcomers and even about suspending any enlargement until the voters in france and the netherlands would finally approve the constitution they have rejected en masse. my group has a negative view of the economic and military choices in that constitutional text and of the lack of democracy which it affirms, but our criticism is not directed against the arrival of new member states with a lower standard of living. on the contrary, their admission could help the european union to focus more on our common large-scale and crossborder problems and less on unnecessary interference with matters that can be better assessed and decided at a smaller scale. croatia and macedonia are welcome in principle, and so is turkey, if it democratizes and respects human rights. my group opposes the imposition of unworkable demands on newcomers. financial and economic demands which lead to poverty and insecurity for large sections of their population do not contribute to progress but to the degradation of society. on the other hand, if the european union is to contribute to the quality of society, we must be tough on the environment, human rights and good governance. we are now absolutely certain that there is no mining of gold in romania, at rosia montana or elsewhere, with dangerous chemicals that lead to flows of toxic water in our own country and in neighbouring countries? are we confident that the krishna gorge in bulgaria will be protected as a vulnerable natural area against the encroaching road freight traffic? are the new laws copied and translated from the acquis communautaire actually applied? is the equality of the large roma population guaranteed? how are the orphans and food security? unfortunately, these questions have not yet been sufficiently answered for both candidate countries, which means that our assessment of what needs to be done can only be made in the next year.

the socialist group in the european parliament has consistently supported the enlargement of the european union to include the countries of central and eastern europe in recent years. the accession of these countries will finally put an end to the decades-long division of europe and the great inequality between east and west which this has caused. the socialist group therefore voted overwhelmingly on 13 april this year to accept the accession treaties with bulgaria and romania. given that there was still a 20-month period between the moment of consent by the european parliament and the envisaged date of accession, 1 january 2007, this consent was given in full knowledge that further progress would be needed in a number of important areas in both romania and bulgaria in order to be able to fully meet the requirements of membership of the european union. but my group also had confidence that both countries should be considered capable of taking the measures necessary for final accession and actually implementing them. it must be said that the inclusion of a safeguard clause allowing the date of accession to be postponed by one year and the firm commitment of both the commission and the council to fully involve the european parliament in the decision on the possible application of this safeguard clause played an important role in giving our consent. the socialist group is consistent in its policy on the enlargement of the european union; as was the case with the earlier accession of 10 new member states last year, we want the accession of bulgaria and romania to be a success for the people of these two countries, of course, without, however, being an unacceptable burden for the present european union. the reports presented today by the commission on the progress of preparations for accession will not be used to justify waiting another year. the points of concern and criticism mentioned in these reports are, in our view, above all a guide for the governments and parliaments of both countries, bulgaria and romania, to take the necessary measures within the time still remaining. at the same time, these reports also highlight areas where both countries could benefit from further support from the european union in order to achieve the stated objective of full membership of the european union in 2007. in our support for enlargement and in our criticism of the progress of the accession process, our group has no interest whatsoever in the party or parties currently in power in either country. the only thing that matters is what those in power are doing to meet the preconditions for full membership. we hope - in the interests of the people of bulgaria and romania, of course - that this view will be shared by the other groups in this parliament. the reports presented today by the commission do not give us any reason to conclude at this stage that both romania and bulgaria will sufficiently fulfil the conditions for accession on 1 january 2007. you don't have to do that now. we do see reason for moderate optimism that both countries will be able to meet the requirements at the next review, the really important one in april next year. however, much work will still be needed, both in the decision-making and the introduction of missing legislation and in the field of practical implementation; the fight against corruption and organised crime in particular deserves the greatest attention. as a personal note - as i have been very much involved in this problem - i would like to add that both reports rightly express the commission's concern, once again, about the situation of the roma in both countries and the continued lack of an adequate and effective approach by the governments of both countries. i think we should also make that point a major part of our final assessment next year. it is an important point for us, but it is also an important point for the roma in both countries. the bulgarian government seems to have picked up the reins with renewed energy and determination after a period of stagnation and delay due to the parliamentary elections and the difficulties in forming a government. it is reassuring to note that the main political parties in the country have united to lead their country into the european union. i would like to call on the main political parties in romania, the romanian government and the president of the country to show the same unity of opinion on accession and not to waste precious time on political harassment. finally, i would like to announce that my group, the socialist group, will, of course, in cooperation with the european commission, make its own analysis of the progress of the accession process in the coming months. in this way, we will be able to take a well-considered decision in the spring, in accordance with the agreements reached, as to whether both countries are really ready in time to take on all the responsibilities of membership of the european union.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, romania and bulgaria have again taken the necessary and important steps in the past year. nevertheless, the list of actions for both countries is substantial and the time until 1 january 2007 is limited. it's about romania. on the basis of the progress reports of the past six months, the reports i have received from romania and my own observations, i must honestly say that i still have a number of concerns. many of these have already been raised by colleagues. i shall briefly touch on three points. first, the independence of the judiciary. in its progress report last september, the commission pointed out that the independence of the judiciary was being undermined by the heavy budgetary constraints imposed by the ministry of finance. i would like to know what the situation is at the moment. secondly, it is clear from all the evidence - and this has been said before - that corruption is still rampant in the police, the judiciary and the political apparatus. to what extent does the commission intend to solve this by means of more legislation and regulation? on behalf of the ind/dem group president, i warmly welcome the socialist and liberal group's wish to think more carefully about patents, which gives hope for the future. we have already done so and we have come to the conclusion that the human body and its parts are not patentable, in our firm belief, because their operation is not an invention of ours but a discovery and therefore not patentable. so this is true of embryos, stem cells, germ cells and the genome. you can't claim ownership of this. it is true that the european commission rightly states in the second 16c report that totipotent stem cells are not patentable. unfortunately, the commission leaves ambiguity about the patentability of pluripotent stem cells. these are also parts of the human body and are excluded from patentability under article 5 (1) of the directive. the patent which is the concrete cause of this debate concerns human germ cells. the grant of this patent infringes the principle that the human body and its parts are not patentable. it would be better if the groups could express themselves more strongly and clearly, particularly in the joint resolution. the european parliament must play its role of control properly. this means making a convincing objection to the european patent office.

in writing. a few years ago, two major deadly epidemics threatened, those of the now-discredited mystery disease sars and a possible combination of bird flu with human flu variants. i thought that was a lot of waiting around back then. international travel from disease hotspots was not or hardly controlled, little attention was paid to research into the emergence of new influenza variants, preventive vaccination of poultry was lacking and vaccine stocks for mass vaccination of humans against already known influenza variants were completely inadequate. i have previously drawn attention to these shortcomings in the parliamentary sessions of 7 april 2003 and 13 may 2003 and in questions to the european commission in 2003 and 2004. the european union and its member states were then hardly prepared for a great danger in which millions of people could die in a short time. also threatening was the need to clear human-held birds en masse. today those problems are still unresolved, but the attitude of this parliament and of the european commission has changed. those who, like the uen group, believe that unnecessary panic is being created and that the bird hunters in particular should be protected are still only a small minority. that is why i am voting in favour of this resolution.

president. - i should like to begin by expressing my sincere appreciation and thanks to mr guido sacconi, who i think has done a very good job. the reach proposal calls for testing 30 000 substances, and reach is thus a source of innovation, but also of simplification. more than 40 directives will be repealed when reach comes into force. reach is therefore good for public health, good for the environment and good for innovation. there are many fine amendments to make reach more workable and less costly and to limit the number of animal tests, but unfortunately there are still quite a few companies that are against innovation, that prefer to wallow in ignorance and have no sense in the search for safer and cleaner alternatives. i think it is a bit disappointing, to be honest, that they are getting a lot of political support. we all took the lisbon challenge. innovation is at the heart of the strong competitiveness of european industry. without continuous innovation to make products cleaner, safer and healthier, european industry will not be able to compete with china, india and the united states. the compromise now on the table cannot be supported by the dutch social democrats. it is a huge undermining if instead of 30 000 substances, only 8 000 substances are now subjected to the full reach test regime, because this will continue to create uncertainty for consumers, unnecessarily prolong the risk of allergy, cancer and occupational diseases and potentially toxic substances in the environment. and last but not least, mr president, this also throws overboard an incentive for continuous innovation by european industry.
the successful european integration of the western balkan countries is of great importance for the region and for europe as a whole. it is one of the political priorities of my group, the pse group. last week we therefore adopted a position on the european future of the western balkans, which we set out in a comprehensive strategy paper which has now been published. the starting point is our full support for the thessaloniki agenda, the achievement of lasting peace, stability and prosperity in the balkans within a process of integration into the european union and with a view of course to eventual accession. the first step in this process is to seek solutions to problems affecting the whole region. the consolidation of democracy and the rule of law, economic development, refugee policy, cooperation with the hague tribunal and the fight against corruption and crime. these are, on the one hand, conditions for successful integration into the european union, but they are also closely linked to regional security and stability in that region, which is still fragile. these problems are therefore not confined to the bilateral relationship between the balkan countries and the european union. the main stakeholders in this process are the countries themselves. cooperation between the countries of the western balkans is therefore an essential component of our strategy. the eu must provide a solid framework, but the momentum to solve these problems must ultimately come from the region itself. and the same goes for the pain points that are still there. the decisions on the state structure of bosnia-herzegovina, the relationship between serbia and a likely independent montenegro and the status of kosovo will have to be taken by regional politicians. but we also need to see the positive developments. we are cautiously optimistic. bosnia has achieved a breakthrough in reform, as far as the police apparatus is concerned, and that has been an important concession from the serbian part of that country. the commission's intention to grant fyrom candidate status reflects the steady progress made in that country. finally, we support the commission in its three cs for enlargement: i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals, but i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative of drawing up a report on the subject.
i am sure that many historians will be able to comment on what exactly happened in europe in the spring of 2005 in terms of attitudes, climate and the vote on enlargement. was it the fact that we still had to deal with the 2004 enlargement, were it the two "no's" in the constitutional referenda, i do not know, it will be seen later, but the fact is that the enlargement of the european union is under attack. many people have the impression that the majority of the population of the european union is against it. and many politicians are happy to hide behind these skeptical citizens. that is why i am glad that the european commission has kept its back straight. i am therefore pleased that the european commission is arguing, arguing - and i agree with it - that the enlargement of the european union, as we have seen it so far, is one of the most successful policies of the european union and that it would therefore be very short-sighted to go back on the promises made to romania and bulgaria, to turkey and croatia, and to the countries of the western balkans. but i am also glad that the commission is right to draw conclusions from the process as we have known it so far. the fact that implementation is more important than making promises, that - and i am echoing mr brok - the european union itself must be able to take in new countries, that an assessment by country must be based on facts and not on automaticity and, finally, that further enlargement will only succeed if politicians show political leadership and are prepared to defend enlargement in the face of their sometimes indeed sceptical populations. if the commission continues to advocate such an enlargement - based on facts, not on promises, not on automaticities, an enlargement based on political vision and analysis, not on opinion polls - then it will have the full support of my group even now.
i am sure that the commission will be able to give us a clear answer to this question, and i am sure that the commission will be able to give us a clear answer to this question. at the same time, we must realize that there has been a considerable imbalance between deepening and widening. in nice, the deepening should have taken place, but it did not, but the widening afterwards did. we must take it upon ourselves that this grounding has yet to take place. i say to my colleague wiersma in the netherlands: my party was 80% for the constitution, if yours is next time, then maybe we'll make it in the netherlands. at the same time, we must now, before the time comes, regain some credibility, and that means that we must take the criteria for enlargement more seriously than ever. if you set criteria, both in terms of financing and in terms of enlargement, you have to follow them afterwards, otherwise you lose credibility. one of these criteria is absorption capacity. we will need to have sufficient support, both institutional and popular, to enable further enlargement. we must show that we take the criteria of the countries themselves seriously. as for romania and bulgaria, i can really hope that they will be able to join in 2007, but it will now depend on them what they do in the next six months. in turkey, if we want things to go well, we will have to make it clear that around personal expression, freedom of expression, the laws will have to be adjusted; that the cyprus problem will have to be resolved, recognising the two sides of the story; and thirdly, for example, in the area of religious freedom, we cannot wait for years, until after the next election in turkey, for something to actually happen. the commission has now clearly stated: it can be done in the short term! we support the commission in this and i think we should continue in this way: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. commissioner, we must all stand firm as europeans against the populists who frighten people about enlargement, but it would be wrong to confuse this with people who are rightly concerned about the balance between deepening and enlargement.
the only way forward is to have a single european blacklist based on common criteria. my group, alde, also wholeheartedly agrees with that. the secondary proposal, while well-intentioned, was rightly considered too weak by the transport committee of this house. crude as it may sound, the dramatic events of the past year seem to have led many to a new kind of awareness, namely that a simple compilation of national blacklists is pointless. a consolidated list which is not automatically legally valid throughout the union is a gesture for the bühne without any added value. of course, that can be worth something. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. but that image is exactly what we wanted to get rid of. i therefore see this proposal as a typical example of the eu's added value. because let us be honest, the fact that, for example, onur air - and the example has been given before - was grounded at some point in the netherlands in order to be able to take off in belgium is of course absurd. such practices simply confuse the traveller, not to mention create potentially unsafe situations. a uniform list based on common european safety criteria is therefore the only step forward and is as clear as it is to travellers, to the european consumer. the european parliament has made clear its ambitions to improve aviation safety by means of many amendments. the commission has been very helpful and willing to do so, and the council has also shown its determination. we have therefore been able to table an agreement at first reading very quickly. during the debate on the proposal in the committee on transport, i repeatedly called for new blacklisting rules to be linked to the existing safa directive and for the powers of the european aviation agency easa to be extended. i am therefore very pleased with the results we have achieved together here, and with the commission's commitments to come forward with proposals in the very short term to extend and strengthen the powers of the easa; the commissioner has just indicated that these commitments have been concretely fulfilled this afternoon. another step in the right direction, for which i thank you, commissioner barrot. we are working hard to achieve coherent and clear regulations with the aim of improving safety and providing clear, unambiguous information for the traveller. no one can argue with that. finally, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs de veyrac, for all the work she has done in this area.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. failure of the engines, air pressure, oxygen supply or navigation system could result in a crash and the death of all the passengers. such a sector becomes particularly vulnerable when it is left to a free market, where new price-fighters are constantly trying to attract as many passengers as possible with low operating costs and low fares in a deadly competition. companies that cut back on safety measures can cause a proportionate number of accidents. until now, the member states and the european union have not worked well together. when unsafe airlines were excluded from taking off and landing by a member state, it was hardly publicized and had no impact on neighbouring states. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used effectively. i have previously urged an active approach to exclusion and education. dangerous companies and their successors in title should be excluded and shortcomings of other companies should be fully disclosed to users. this proposal is a step in that direction but no doubt ingenious companies will continue to look for ways of avoiding it as much as possible. i call on the commission to come forward with further proposals in the coming years, if the situation arises, so that passengers and people on the ground cannot become victims of short-sighted entrepreneurs.
president, commissioner barrot and also in particular mr darling - because at this late hour of the evening the council presidency seat is often empty and i very much appreciate the fact that the council presidency is also represented in this debate - it is a good thing that we have been able to unite on one european blacklist, which lists all airlines that are banned throughout europe. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs de veyrac, in particular for all her efforts, including during the negotiations with the council, and to congratulate her in particular on the result achieved today, which puts an end, already at first reading, to the present undesirable and unclear situation, whereby the passenger is in uncertainty or in ignorance about the safety status of an airline. the recent tragic air accidents this summer have underlined the need for this legislation. the european parliament, mr president, is the driving force behind the plan and we can be rightly proud of that, because the individual member states felt little for it at the beginning. the commission proposal was also limited to the exchange of information only. but thanks to the united and decisive action of parliament, we now have the european blacklist. the citizens of europe have every right to do so, because it is not possible for an airline not to be allowed to land in one country and to land in the neighbouring country. this underlines once again the importance of a european dimension and of tackling the problem. i have tried to make clear in this regulation the right of passengers to compensation, because without this right the regulation is a sham. it is a good thing that it has succeeded. i hope that this regulation will contribute to the safety of the aviation sector and to the improvement of the position of the passenger in europe.
mr president, over the next 20 years, more than a third of the 150 nuclear power stations in europe will have to be decommissioned. is it the taxpayers who pay for it or the polluters? the lack of funds must not lead to the prolonged operation of obsolete power stations. waste is more than the radioactive material left over after decommissioning. the commission has also found that the aid granted to the beneficiaries of the aid is compatible with the internal market. the rapporteur, mr harms, rightly proposes that energy companies should annually put money into a fund to cover all the costs of decommissioning, processing and storing radioactive waste. the inclusion of these costs in the price of nuclear energy does not make it artificially cheap through subsidies and also shows how low the economic return is. the lack of transparency in the link between civilian and military objectives is still leading to outright distortions of competition and disguised export subsidies. we agree with the proposal to draw up a list of criteria for decommissioning, which will be linked to reserve funds into which electricity companies will deposit sufficient funds for waste treatment and decommissioning.
president. - for years europe and america have done nothing against the terrible dictatorship in iraq because iraq supplied oil. instead of supporting the internal resistance in time, an external intervention was finally carried out with false arguments and without the approval of the united nations. this violent and one-sided pursuit of the views and interests of a great power is provoking sections of the shiite majority to choose, out of disgust with the western occupiers, a theocratic dictatorship on the model of iran. the positive thing is that it has now proved possible to put an end to the situation in which the kurdish north and the shiite south were oppressed wings of the central region around baghdad. the new constitution, through federalism, protects these disadvantaged areas from oppression and exploitation, to the benefit of 80% of the population. the only chance of democracy in the new länder succeeding is the rapid end to the foreign military presence and the domination of foreign companies. staying means a new provocation that will continue to provoke resistance. what is the timetable for ending the military presence of the member states of the european union and what is the european union's contribution to replacing the failing american company halliburton, which cannot repair the utilities, by supporting domestic iraqi solutions?

president. - mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. an unbridled free market economy without restraint on excessive consumption and production leads to ever-increasing greenhouse gas emissions and thus to an aggravation of the problem. economic growth is like a sacred cow, untouchable. every consumer product must be available on the market at the same time all over the world, but we must remember that even sacred cows can spread dirty gases. and i mean that not only figuratively, but literally, when you look at the contribution of intensive livestock farming to carbon emissions. the nuclear energy lobby is using the climate change issue to put itself back on the map. and, strangely enough, they don't tell us about the huge mountains of waste that they're producing and that will be a big problem for us for the next 10,000 years. let's not solve one environmental crisis with another. the billions still going to the development and promotion of nuclear energy could be better spent on wind, solar and hydroelectric power. emissions from the transport sector will still be 28% above 1990 levels in 2030. it is a difficult sector to take action on, because this sector is the symbol of an international free market economy. i therefore call for a tax on air and sea traffic. unlike emissions trading, taxation cannot shift the burden onto developing countries.
president. - i welcome the work programme presented by commission president barroso yesterday in this house. there is also much good in fighting climate change. for example, i wholeheartedly support the inclusion of air traffic in the emissions trading system and the revision of the 2001 directive on national emission ceilings. i do not think the european commission is very ambitious in this matter. we've been talking for years about drastic reductions in greenhouse gas emissions, but meanwhile, emissions are rising every year. not just globally, but within various member states. president. - if we want to be taken seriously in montreal, we must not only dare to talk about reductions, but also make firm agreements to take real measures, if necessary with a smaller number of countries. i would ask you, commissioner dimas, to come up with a strategy in which you propose measures to make our long-term objective achievable, because despite the positive impact of the proposals presented this week, they are only inspired by a short-term vision.

on behalf of the pse group president. - i shall try to do that. we're facing a very unpleasant decision today or tomorrow, actually. we are going to vote against the commission's proposal, but in my case it is not by any means a heartfelt vote. as a parliament we have a duty to contribute to the legislative process and we reject the proposal tomorrow without giving a clear direction on how it should be done. and that, as far as i'm concerned, is a missed opportunity. it turned out that almost all the parties were dissatisfied. some thought the proposal went too far, others thought it did not go far enough. but we'd have to decide that. the commission will then have a clear guide when it makes a new proposal to implement the international convention. i cannot, of course, fail to say a few words about the musk rats. of course the dutch dikes must be protected. we cannot promote animal welfare at the expense of our safety. but i am not in favour of drowning traps and i would like to call for a reflection on the catching and the method of catching muskrats. in any case, i believe that the following conditions for the use of drowning cases are essential. first of all, there must be a pest and therefore damage to the dikes. the damage is obvious in a number of places in the netherlands, in friesland, in south holland, but in brabant the damage is much less obvious. the second condition is that prevention is not effective or possible and the third condition is that the drowning trap is used only if no more animal-friendly alternatives are available. europe will not be able to present a workable directive in the short term, and that is disappointing, but it is not the end of the debate. and that, as far as i'm concerned, doesn't have to mean the painful end of the muskrats. for the time being, it is up to the member states themselves to make this work. so work on alternatives and minimize animal suffering. the rejection of this directive should not be an excuse for not starting the debate in the netherlands on the control of muskrats, and in that respect i have to thank karin scheele, who has started a huge debate in the netherlands that we would not have had without her amendment. so let us turn the present situation in a positive direction, take the opportunity which the european parliament has so far missed and make prevention a priority. develop the necessary alternatives and the netherlands can make an important contribution to a new legislative proposal.
the commission's proposal is not a new one, but it is a new one, and it is a new one. i think she has shown convincingly that this is an example of bad regulation. for example, the setting of standards for traps has not been preceded by sound scientific research. there are criteria for traps and trapping testing, and they are random. of course, the duration of animal suffering is very difficult to measure. when does stress kick in and when does the death struggle begin? moreover, especially in the case of live trapping, the proposal does not say anything about the humanity of the alternative methods that might then be used to kill animals. animals are often drowned in other ways or are killed by being strangled or killed by each other. there is an inconsistency between this proposal and the habitats directive. certain species protected under the habitats directive - i am thinking of the explicit prohibition on the capture and killing of wild otters, beavers, wolves and lynxes, for example - are also listed in the annex to the proposal on humane fishing methods. how does that rhyme with "i'm sorry"? and other animals covered by this proposal, for example the marten and the badger, are also protected or conserved in certain areas. the directive - and i cannot help but say something about it - has a characteristic dutch security problem: the protection of low-lying land against flooding by dike breaches. the muskrats' way of life undermines dikes, posing a real threat to public safety and health. the animal is an alien species in the netherlands which must of course be controlled in the most humane way, but at the moment there is no useful humane method of control which is as effective as control by means of the drowning trap. a ban on the drowning trap - and i can imagine that the proposal will at some point return to such a ban - makes effective control of muskrats impossible. the dutch security and public health are at stake. we must also look for more humane methods of fishing for musk rats, but until suitable alternatives are found, i think that a clear exceptional position for the netherlands or for a situation where safety is at stake would be good. mr president, a final comment of a more general nature. we are doing a little bit of animal protection à la carte in the european union. there is a lot going on in the field of, for example, reducing animal testing, think of the cosmetics directive or reach we are talking about now. we're doing something on international animal shipping. it's all very important, but it's also very random. the question is what europe would like to do or should do in the field of animal protection. we are not going to vote for the report, but we are going to vote for the report, and we are going to vote for the report, and we are going to vote for the report. it would be good if both we in this parliament and the union were to start thinking about what we actually want to do and what we do not want to do in the field of animal protection, instead of doing something here and there à la carte about protection.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented in the member states. firstly, i believe that this directive would make it impossible to control effectively pests in certain member states, in particular the muskrats in the netherlands. that could have major consequences for public safety. and as long as there is no exception to this in the proposal, i cannot support it. commissioner dimas, you have expressed your understanding of the concerns of many organisations. i would also like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the concern of the netherlands for the safety of the citizens when dikes are being undermined by the excavation of the musk deer. my second argument for rejection concerns the legal basis. protocol 33 to the ec treaty gives the eu competence in the field of animal welfare only when this is done in the context of agricultural, transport, internal market or research policies. the proposal for a directive is the implementation of the international agreement on humane fishing practices concluded under the common commercial policy. since the proposal harmonises the standards of the member states for animal cases in the internal market and does not serve a direct environmental objective, only article 95 can be considered as the appropriate legal basis for this proposal. i would like to hear commissioner dimas's reaction to this.
on behalf of the pse group mr president, it is unfortunate that at this late hour we are still debating a number of issues which are actually very important and certainly very urgent, particularly when we are talking about the problems of the non-proliferation drive and the non-proliferation treaty. that is why i regret that the presidency is not here tonight, particularly because the current president of the european union is also a recognized nuclear power. but perhaps it can be conveyed that the presence of the presidency is very much desirable in such debates. action to combat the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction is again very urgent in the recent period. the number of states possessing nuclear weapons seems to be growing, and that is a bad example for other countries, and at the same time - as the commissioner has already said - the risk that terrorists will get their hands on weapons of mass destruction or nuclear material is considered to be real. a strengthening of the international framework to address these risks is urgently needed. but right now we are seeing a tendency to take disarmament issues outside of international frameworks. the international community, including europe, has failed this year to amend, renew and strengthen the non-proliferation treaty, while the united states is increasingly trying to pursue its own disarmament policy, particularly in the field of nuclear non-proliferation. both are very damaging to the credibility and effectiveness of the global non-proliferation regime which remains an essential pillar of our policy. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we counter that trend and gain a stronger grip on the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction, but also of nuclear technology and nuclear chemical and biological material; the same is true of the export of conventional arms. the european union will have to lead the way when it comes to the future of the non-proliferation treaty, but it is also doing a good job on many other points such as the recent proposal, the recent lobbying for an international arms export treaty. at the same time, there are more contradictions, contradictions in the international regime, which also involve european countries. the recognized nuclear powers of the world have an extra responsibility. they are the ones who put the most pressure on countries that want to break out of the international system, and at the same time they are considering the modernization of their nuclear arsenal, and one of them even refuses to support the ban on nuclear tests. on the one hand, europe is one of the driving forces - that is another contradiction - behind the international non-proliferation regime. it is one of the absolute priorities of the european security strategy: the european union is leading the search for a peaceful solution to the iranian problem; at the same time, we are spending far less than the americans on cleaning up old nuclear arsenals. the report by mr kristovskis rightly calls for the political priority to be supported with adequate resources. finally, the pse group is very much attached to the international legal order. we have had a long debate in the commission on the paragraphs in the kristovskis report on the use of force to prevent non-proliferation, the proliferation of nuclear weapons. we still feel that the passage in the report does not do justice to our view of the international legal order and the role of the united nations in it. if those paragraphs remain as they are and if parliament decides to support that passage tomorrow, i do not exclude that our group will abstain in the final vote on this report.

in writing. there should be no secrecy or ambiguity about the composition and toxicity of chemical substances. for months, a number of chemical companies have tried to torpedo a measure that has long been necessary for the environment and public health. today it has become clear that a strong 'reach' as advocated by the united left and the green group will not win a majority here. on the other hand, many proposals from christian democrats and conservatives, who were fully in favour of the wishes of the chemical industry, were also rejected here. only those proposals on which the social democrats and the liberals agreed were given a majority. this means a weakened version of 'reach' where registration is eroded and only a positive result is achieved in respect of the licences. we share the majority view of our group that such a weak option is better than nothing. now that the epp and uen groups here say that they can still vote in favour because the council and the necessary qualified majority in this parliament will ensure that the provisions on licences will not survive a second reading, that is the reason for the two members of the socialist party in the netherlands to vote against.
in writing. in my country, the netherlands, there has been a long-standing struggle against the american imported muskrats, which are undermining our dikes and threatening to make low-lying areas uninhabitable for humans. we are, however, supporting the rapporteur, mr scheele, who proposes a general rejection of this proposal on the humane trapping of certain species. humaan means both "human" and "decent". people have taken on the most terrible things over the course of time, things that may have been human but certainly not decent. the european commission's proposal considers amputations, eye injuries, spinal cord injuries and a five-minute death struggle as humane. since 1995, trapping has been prohibited throughout the european union and there is a ban on the import of animal skins from countries where trapping is not prohibited. in 1996 a proposal was already ready for stricter requirements for the other cases. a lobby of trappers then managed to sideline the experts. then an agreement was reached with the united states, canada, and russia, which was more concerned with avoiding trade conflicts than with improving animal welfare. this proposal is based on that wrong agreement, which the european parliament has already objected to.
