missing? the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. the commissioner is right to say that we must provide more, better and faster aid. we cannot achieve this by simply giving more money, even if it is a few billion more. it is great that europe wants to free up more money and that the commission wants to hold member states to their financial commitments, but more is needed. i am thinking first of all of what we can do in our own policies, the three cs of coordination, coherence and complementarity. if they do not increase, the chances of more effective aid are considerably reduced. much will ultimately depend on the political will of the member states. i hope that the commission will find an effective strategy to promote it. secondly, mr president, the commission recognizes the great differences between african countries. the various situations and problems are clearly articulated, but there is no clear corresponding instrument to deal with the differences. we believe that a clear distinction should be made between aid to stable countries and aid to countries which are socially and economically unstable because of bad governance, corruption and mismanagement. the commission proposes that under certain conditions stable countries may even be helped by budgetary and sectoral aid, but that unstable countries also deserve support. we can't let the people down there. we are not calling for support through governments in those countries, but for support through civil society organisations, including, of course, the involvement of religious communities. they reach many people and can do a good job in health, education and other basic services. thirdly, mr president, the commission proposes that the efforts for africa should now be directed along three lines. the first line is to promote safety and good governance, which is important. the second line is that of economy and trade, providing a better investment climate, also important. only the third leg of the action plan addresses poverty reduction through the millennium development goals, such as promoting basic health care, improving education, protecting the environment and striving for jobs for as many people as possible. we believe that the latter, the millennium development goals, should be the focus. combating poverty at the grassroots level is of the utmost importance in achieving the structural stability needed for further development. we therefore believe that all policies must be an instrument for achieving the millennium development goals. that is why we consider that major infrastructure projects in africa or exchanges of partner cities and universities in the european union and africa are premature. valuable projects in themselves, but more for a later stage. finally, mr president, i would like to thank everyone who has contributed to this report, both inside and outside the ep, for their input. i look back on a pleasant and constructive cooperation.
author. president, the philippine moratorium on the death penalty of march 2000 has been reversed in the last three years. the current government is seeking to increase the number of death sentences, including for minors who cannot be sentenced to death under the law and who cannot be executed. in an effort to kill as many people as possible, victims are isolated, tortured and denied access to justice. the inability of the philippine government to stop the crime and the insurgency does not justify the use of such barbaric means. among the many filipino nationals who are victims of this is a man who also holds spanish nationality, francisco larrañaga. he has been accused of very serious crimes, but this does not justify a sham trial which ignores his alibi, nor the imposition of the death penalty, nor the exclusion of the possibility of reversing that conviction. this dual nationality does contribute to the attention paid to this misdemeanour in europe. even if no european citizen's life is in danger, everything must be done to end the death penalty everywhere in the world.
author. mr president, burma has made a reasonable start as an independent state shortly after the second world war. the main problem was that the inland population had different languages and cultures from the coastal population and that the indigenous peoples wanted to have as much autonomy as possible. the failure to comply with them, the failure to give them sufficient consideration, has led to violent conflicts. these conflicts gave the army the opportunity to become more powerful and more independent. eventually, this army took over all state power and also a large part of the economy. it is a mysterious kind of dictatorship, without ideological objectives, which could make it more popular with certain sections of the population. within that dictatorship, there is constant strife, with leaders being deposed and even imprisoned. there are also conflicting economic interests within the leading group in mining, tropical forest clearing and opium production. the tactics against the democratic opposition also vary. the only time free elections were allowed, the result was subsequently annulled because the vast majority had voted for the opposition. regime change seems so far almost impossible. for years, foreign companies have profited from this dictatorship. burma can supply cheap raw materials because the people are more or less enslaved and nature and the environment are not protected. under the influence of international protests by various movements, these economic contacts have been greatly reduced, but they have not disappeared. only continued and reinforced isolation can bring about change in the long run. it is good that the proposed resolution calls for a ban on all companies registered in the european union investing in or lending to burma. only in this way can we help to put an end to rape and looting by government forces, to forced labour, to forced displacement, to political prisoners and to the exclusion of citizens from the administration.

the rapporteur has succeeded in finding a majority for her report in the committee on transport. although a majority, it was remarkable how many members abstained there (20 in favour, 7 against and 19 abstentions). it is not necessary to explain that i was very disappointed about this. i therefore disagree with the previous speakers. article 81 of the treaty states that market distorting cartels are incompatible with community law. however, paragraph 3 of the same article sets out a number of exceptions which must be met in order to allow such partnerships. on this basis, liner shipping is covered by a block exemption under regulation 4056/1986. the commission has ordered an investigation into the extent to which this block exemption is still allowed in 2005. are the conditions of article 81 (3) still met? the commission has concluded that this is not the case and therefore intends to review the rules. the alde group supports the commission in this (see, for example, amendment no 10). the rapporteur points out in paragraph 9 that the four cumulative conditions of article 81 (3) are only partially met. at the same time, the rapporteur is working to maintain a partnership. this raises a number of questions, to say the least. if it is only partially fulfilled, then we can say quite clearly that it is not fulfilled. the four conditions are really cumulative. my colleagues from the ppe-de and pse were concerned about the consequences of a possible review for the shipping companies in the sme sector. which companies should they be? the report on batteries and accumulators was adopted by a large majority in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection considers that the 41 amendments to this report improve the council's common position. however, in the committee on the environment we have not yet reached agreement on one important aspect, namely the reduction of the heavy metals mercury, cadmium and lead in some of the batteries. parliament has made its position clear on this at first reading. after the first reading, there have obviously been objections, or perhaps i should say it differently: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the market for batteries is not distorted. in order to try to reach an agreement, i have tabled a modified proposal for the plenary, namely amendment no 42. this is a weakening of the amendment adopted at first reading. the restriction of the prohibition on heavy metals covers three points. firstly, amendment no 42 restricts the cadmium ban to portable batteries. industrial batteries are thus excluded from the cadmium ban. the exemption granted to industrial batteries at first reading was not enough for industry, which felt that this would put them too much in the danger zone. just so we 're clear , my amendment no 42 has removed industrial batteries from the danger zone altogether. secondly, amendment no 42 also restricts the ban on lead to portable batteries, so that industrial lead-acid batteries are also completely excluded from the ban. thirdly, certain categories of portable batteries are exempted from the lead ban. this applies to node cells, and the batteries for hearing aids are no longer in danger. so we have listened to the comments of the foundation for the deaf and hard of hearing. fourthly, the cadmium ban on portable tools is postponed for four years. therefore, there is still sufficient time to switch entirely to li-ion and nimh batteries as alternatives to nicd batteries. these alternatives have been widely available for almost 10 years. i have seen that both the socialists and the greens have tabled a similar amendment to article 4. i can also support these amendments in general terms. finally, mr president, i would like to say a few words on three other points, namely definitions, producer responsibility and batteries integrated into appliances. as for the definitions, i think they should be included in an article and not in a recital. i therefore oppose amendments nos 46, 48 and 50. the council's common position retains subjective elements in the definition of portable batteries. the words 'can be carried by hand' are subjective and the environment committee therefore prefers a definition which includes a clear boundary. in amendment no 12 this is a limit of 1 kg, so these are portable batteries. in addition, amendment no 12 links it to consumer applications as defined in the directive on electronic waste. the commission has already proposed a number of amendments to the directive, including a number of amendments to the directive on the protection of the environment. the committee on the environment feels that amendments nos 12 and 13 provide the greatest clarity on these definitions. as far as producer responsibility is concerned, i should point out that this is a guiding principle for several other directives and now also for the battery directive. i agree with the council's common position on this. amendment no 44 proposes a shared responsibility. this is very confusing in practice and very complex in implementation. i believe that we must maintain producer responsibility and therefore oppose amendment no 44. amendments nos 18 and 40 prohibit the integration of batteries and require that they be easy to remove. on the surface, this seems like a good thing. however, i understand that this will cause problems for many applications, especially if the battery has a longer life than the device. moreover, this provision is unnecessary, since the directive on electronic waste contains the obligation that batteries should always be removed from collected electrical equipment. mr president, on behalf of the committee on the environment, i recommend that the plenary vote tomorrow on the amendments to the report, taking account of my comments.
mr president, as has already been said, the mifid (markets in financial instruments directive), formerly the isd (investment services directive), is a major legislative programme for the internal market in capital. the aim is to create a level playing field for different types of investment services, with a focus on increasing transparency before and after the investment, consumer protection and the promotion of competitive prices with the greatest possible liquidity. in europe, these instruments can be a major step towards a better investment climate, and we can use them well at the moment. the complexity of the directive requires careful consultation with the various market players. a large part of that work is carried out by the supervisors, under the direction of the committee of european securities regulators (cesr). the european commission cannot do this alone with its limited manpower. i am very much in favour of this careful approach and this important role for the supervisors. they are our main ally in serving the public interest and in avoiding being guided by the partial interests of certain large market players or certain national markets, which unfortunately was still the case when this dossier was being prepared. the european parliament has found a balance between these different interests and it is now up to it to ensure that this balance is maintained. this means that we must be able to continue to play our part. that is why we are demanding an important right in the context of monitoring comitology, namely the so-called right of revocation. the commission has repeatedly stated that it would support us in this, and the council has done so with a certain degree of reluctance, but it has not succeeded in correcting this point in the treaty in the context of the discussion on this subject. but now that things are quiet, there is no reason to put that point on ice either. the so-called lamfallussy procedure is applied to an increasing number of cases in the field of financial markets and it is therefore very necessary to structure the european parliament's right of revocation. this is not a prestige project or a prestige battle, we are really concerned with remaining involved in what we outlined at level 1 and now want to see reflected in the implementation we have delegated.
for more than two years, the european parliament has been receiving tens of thousands of petitions from european citizens, many of them dutch, about the abuse of a law on urbanisation projects (lrau) in the valencia region. the law allows developers to unlawfully take land from homeowners for no or little compensation, and then turn it over to pay for the cost of roads, sewerage and street lighting. fortunately, parliament has taken up this problem and tomorrow we shall be voting on the report by mr fourtou, a liberal. it urges valencia to improve the law - a process already under way following a visit by a european commission of inquiry - and to set up a procedure for case-by-case assessment of the situation of homeowners, including compensatory measures. i have had contact with dutch people in the region before and i am glad that the urgency of the situation is finally being recognised. it is good that valencia has already begun to revise the law at the insistence of europe, but those words will now have to be translated into action to prevent more owners from being unfairly disadvantaged.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr bersani, for his report, but i would also like to thank a number of shadow rapporteurs and colleagues from the alde and ppe-de for the way they have dealt with this dossier. i would also like to join mr garcía-margallo y marfil in saying that i am glad that you are here for this dossier, mr kovács, and not mr mccreevy, who was just here. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it has always been very difficult in this house, including in the previous period, to reach common positions on this point. i have the impression that we will be able to reach agreement on this now, and i hope that you, mr kovács, will also be able to reach agreement with the council or at least with a large number of member states. i am glad that sobriety on this point takes precedence over the clash of ideological weapons. sobriety also requires that we look at the modest role of the european union in this. i would like to distance myself completely from the remarks that have just been made about brussels taking power away from the member states and such comments. i think it is quite simple to say that in this area it is necessary to guarantee and ensure that the internal market and equal conditions of competition are not distorted, and that simply in order for companies to operate in the european union, simplicity and security must be provided. the call for this common consolidated basis is not without reason coming from the business community: unice, the round table, they're all companies that have asked for this. this has nothing whatsoever to do with seizing power at european level, it is simply meeting the demands of business. i think it's important to look at what the win-win situation might be: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. many of the new countries have low rates, but also very broad bases for these taxes, and i think the outcome of this process of harmonizing the tax base will be that the rates will be much closer together than they are at present, and that is the kind of transparency we need. the discussion of what then needs to be done next, including any minimum tariffs or forks or whatever, comes later. it is very important now to take that first step and we support you in that, commissioner kovács.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, firstly i would like to thank the rapporteur for all the good work she has done: it was not an easy task. the year of equal opportunities for all is very welcome because fundamental rights are the close to the hearts of the european citizens and they must be made a reality for all citizens. against this backdrop there are two issues i would like to raise. first, there is the budget. i think that everybody in this room will agree that it is embarrassingly insufficient – eur 15 million for fundamental rights! if i compare that to the money we spend on a lot of other things that are less valuable than fundamental rights, i have to say it gives me stomach pains. the second issue is decentralised implementation. that is very good, but i am rather worried about the possibility of cherry-picking by the member states. i am not entirely reassured by all the warm words we have heard in recent years about fundamental rights, because the reality is different and the commissioner himself mentioned that some member states are still lagging behind in the implementation of anti-discrimination legislation. there is still a hierarchy of discrimination and i think that with this programme for the year of equal opportunities for all we should not be encouraging this. i would like to ask the commissioner to commit the commission here and now, publicly and on the record, to ensuring that member states will use the funds available for this year to target all forms of discrimination equally. this is not to say anything about the way the money is distributed, but we want certain – let us say – more ‘awkward’ groups that are currently suffering discrimination – homosexuals, certain reglious groups or the roma, for example – to have equal access to this programme with all the other groups that are suffering discrimination. i am looking forward to a positive answer.

mr president, we had proposed an oral amendment but we will withdraw it now because we are satisfied, for the time being, with the statement of the european commission, yesterday evening, that it is committed to fighting all forms of discrimination in all member states and that it is prepared to urge all member states to allocate the financial resources accordingly. however, we will keep a close eye on the commission and the execution of this programme. we will talk to the commission again, should it not live up to this promise. under that condition, we withdraw the oral amendment.
i have said many times in discussions with colleagues here that the greens also do not consider the right to privacy to be sacrosanct, and that infringements of it can be justified if the measures in question are really necessary, proportionate and effective. it is clear to me that what we need in the fight against terrorism, the fight against organized crime, is more investment in targeted investigations, more investment in analyzing existing data and better cooperation between intelligence and security services. money can only be spent once, even our euro. the money is going to mass surveillance, to big fish, instead of that targeted surveillance, and i think this is really the wrong choice. mr president, i could have supported the increase in telephone traffic and all the arguments that minister clark gave were also about telephone traffic only, so that was easy. but what's the point of going to record how long someone is on the internet and the logon and log-off data. we're in an age where people are often on the computer all day. so this recording is pretty pointless. mr president, we are always talking about better regulation in this house and then you get this nonsense. the cost to the business community has not even been calculated! mr president, at the beginning of october it became known that more than 200 police officers from the rotterdam police force had tried to look into the file of former feyenoord player van persie. the computer wouldn't give the agents access, but it would register their names. big brother is watching you. not just known citizens who are suspects in a criminal case, but police officers as well. that is what concerns me most about this proposal, the misuse of the files by staff of the competent authorities. my principal objection to this proposal is that the long-term retention of data on every citizen is not only an invasion of his privacy but also that he is in fact being treated as a suspect, but without his rights. isn't this cure worse than the disease? it has not been demonstrated that this proposal will have any effect, so i would like to hear a clear voice from the commission and the council on how this directive will be tested and evaluated. the rapporteur, mr alvaro, has done an excellent job and it is unheard of that a deal has been struck between the major groups without any reason. this seriously undermines our way of doing business. i support the rapporteur's line and i would like to end with a quote from george orwell: "some groups are more equal than others".
madam president, i should like to focus on the internet part of this directive. mr clarke, you think that the lack of massive quantities of traffic data is a threat to our security. i disagree. practice shows that it is the lack of online security that offers unlimited opportunities to criminals and terrorists. i will give you a practical example. last month, a group of three criminals who control a network of over 100 000 hacked pcs was rolled up by the dutch police. control over those unsecured pcs gave the criminals the power to perpetrate all kinds of crimes, from massive identity theft, attacks on vital infrastructure and the unlimited opportunity to grant anonymous access to and use of all services of the internet, for whoever was willing to pay. imagine what could happen if these men offered their services to terrorist networks. imagine also how useless traffic data is, because it points at innocent people. the methods used to track these criminals down are specified in the cybercrime treaty, which has been signed by all member states but implemented by only a few. it is those methods that we need to fight terrorism. it is indisputable that this directive is intrusive. it is questionable whether it will help. on the internet side, it is even technically unfeasible. i completely agree with my colleagues mrs in't veld and mr lambrinidis. i fear a false sense of security. i fear that part of this directive could even pose a threat to our security.

president. - mr president, i have to say that i am not in favour of the amendment tabled by mr glinne, but i would like to ask the commissioner to give me a brief explanation of his position. we are paying for it with air pollution, noise pollution and far too much space. this requires a radical shift from road to rail and water and the reduction of unnecessary transport. one of the ways of achieving this is by making road transport itself pay for all the inconveniences and thus become more expensive than rail transport. switzerland is setting us a good example. the compromise proposal for a toll is better than the current directive, but there are too many exceptions. the great danger is that member states will wait for the commission's promised calculation model, which will have to be agreed by the council and parliament again in two years' time. this allows interested governments to try to delay the transfer of environmental costs indefinitely. the most polluting trucks will be exempted until 2010. smaller trucks below 12 tonnes are excluded from the directive until 2012 and member states can simply grant exemptions for these lighter vehicles. furthermore, there is no guarantee that the toll revenue will go to sustainable means of transport such as the freight rail. member states may, if they so wish, invest the money entirely in asphalt. my group would have supported amendments aimed at more and faster results. with this compromise, they'll be out of the picture. the most important thing is that we now at least start to introduce this much needed toll on freight traffic.

the reform of the european union sugar regime is accompanied by accompanying measures. the reduction in the guaranteed price is not only affecting our sugar sector, but also the acp sugar exporters. the european union's support is a major contribution to the competitiveness of the acp sector, in particular through greater efficiency and diversification. in nine years' time these countries will no longer be so dependent on their sugar production. the acp countries and the european sugar sector must also be made competitive in such a way that they can eventually face competition from third-country exporters such as brazil without an eu price guarantee. this is the transformation in which eu sugar policy can and must guide the acp countries and the european sugar sector. the resources needed for this must come from chapters 4 and 1 of the european budget. it is a matter of development and agriculture.
in writing. the directive on data retention, pushed through under the british presidency, was not expected by many in europe. in the future, citizens' and businesses' telephone and internet data will be stored for years to combat terrorism and crime. this blatant invasion of privacy and creates great legal uncertainty. who has access to this data? who's using it? it's not being handled well. it is not excluded that telecommunications companies use the data for commercial purposes. on the other hand, it is far from certain that compulsory data storage will help in the detection of terrorists and criminals. while the directive applies to the whole of europe, the definition of 'serious crime' is left to the member states. it is unclear who will pay for the huge databases needed for this. the directive is not only unnecessary but has been rushed through by the council and parliament. the rapporteur has been ignored. all the guarantees that were built into the commission on civil liberties have been thrown overboard by the council of ministers. once again, it is clear how ill-equipped our democratic institutions are to deal with this kind of demagoguery by which governments respond to the fears of the people. under the guise of terrorism, fort europe is becoming more and more a police state.

president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) on the common organization of the market in bananas (com (97) 0406 - c4-0179/97). the financial easing of travellers. the latter even anticipate the behavior of the former. for example, an official letter from the dutch government, dated only a week and a half ago, mentions the fact that many border crossers put a small amount of money in their passports for the bulgarian customs officials. if the bulgarian media would finally capture this corruption at the border visually, that would certainly help, says an insider. perhaps a tip that the commissioner can pass on to his bulgarian counterpart. the fight against corruption is also a priority for the romanian authorities. however, there is a concern about the apparent tension between the desire to give publicity to the fight against corruption, both in the eu and in the public, and the reluctance that should be commonly shown in court cases. does the commissioner share this observation of the netherlands and, if so, what concrete action does he envisage with regard to the romanian authorities? i hear in this debate talk of fighting corruption and strengthening the rule of law, but there is a tension there. the commissioner knows this very well from his experience in romania. in short, the nascent romanian legal system must not be sacrificed on the altar of eu integration, otherwise we will miss the boat. finally, mr president, a brief remark on the protection of intellectual property. that's what we usually refer to in this house as china. the rapporteur, mr moscovici, rightly urges the same thing in bucharest, because the romanian shops selling counterfeit goods are well known. in short, boekarest is invited to perform.
i would like to ask the president-in-office, ladies and gentlemen, to be careful today. a call to ourselves to take our own criteria and procedures seriously. i say this especially to colleagues who know now, at this moment, that romania will not make it to next year. they felt or felt that europe had gone too fast in the past with enlargement and now they feel that we have to be a little more strict and that whatever romania does, it will never be good enough. i say this also to the members who already know that romania will succeed regardless of whether it actually fulfils the conditions set. there is every reason for parliament to remain critical until the last moment, especially in areas as sensitive and difficult as judicial reform and the fight against corruption. they are not only the main problem in romania, but in all the applicant countries. the questions of six months ago are still crucial today: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. are there new people with new insights coming into important positions? is it really possible to fight corruption at the highest levels? these are questions that we do not have to answer now. our task now, ladies and gentlemen, is to be clear, to be clear and to make clear what we think should be done in romania. in may 2006, the time has come to judge.
president, enlargement has done the european union, including the old countries of the union, a great deal of good, and we must turn against populists who pretend that enlargement has been bad for us. i am convinced that romania and bulgaria will also enrich the union. the accession of these countries will not only benefit them, it will also add something to the union. friends, when i say this, it does not mean that we should not be attentive to the criteria, to the values of the union. there is simply a lot of work to be done in that area. according to a list by transparency international last month, bulgaria is number 55 on the list of corrupt countries and romania is number 85, along with countries like mongolia and the dominican republic. the fact that corruption exists at the highest levels is of particular concern. the border controls and everything that has to be done there is also a gap, and so are the rights of minorities. bulgaria and romania have done a lot, but there is still a lot to be done before the criteria are actually met. we were forced to decide very early in parliament, 20 months before accession, but the real decision of parliament on when these countries can join will not be taken until we have the latest information, which is in april and may next year, probably in may. i think we must be fair in this, we must let the facts speak, we must increase the pressure now and romania and bulgaria must do everything possible to achieve real results in practice in the field of corruption. i think it would be a great sign if we saw that senior officials and old politicians were simply put on trial for their corruption. that sounds like a wonderful signal to me. we must base our decision on that, fairly but objectively and also in accordance with our values. i trust, knowing the commissioner, that he will also operate in this way, that if the result is good enough, the countries in question can join in 2007, but that if there is still a gap, the commission will propose to postpone accession for a year. maintaining the criteria is good for maintaining support for enlargement, it is good for the credibility of the european union and, mr president, last but not least, it is very good for the people of romania and bulgaria themselves who deserve our level of freedom and democracy.
i would also like to thank mrs attwooll very warmly for her work, her report and the way in which she has put women's networks in europe on the map. i also want to thank the commissioner. it is not the first time that he has shown that he has a great social feeling towards the fishing industry. i would also like to thank him for his active involvement of all sectors of society in the fisheries sector. why is that so important? there are three reasons why it is essential that fisheries should be given a wider role than just economic factors. when i look at a crisis in my own region, the shrimp crisis, and the role that vinvis has played in it, i see that the women's network has contributed to a solution and i simply have to say that it is a very good thing that there is a social widening of the fisheries sector. i am also very pleased that this network has now been given its place within the regional advisory council for the north sea. but it is particularly important in the field of training, in the field of social status, in the field of the economic impact of fisheries. i think it is a good thing that the women's network, vinvis, and other organisations are being given a place in the fisheries sector, given the high price of fuel and a number of other things which have social implications. in this respect i was also very pleased with the hearing which the rapporteur organised in july with a large number of women's organisations and i think it would be good if this kind of activity were organised in the future by parliament and also by the commission. i would like to thank the rapporteur again, mr president, for this report and i hope that the commissioner will also continue to implement the recommendations. his answer on this point raises reasonably positive expectations of such an approach and i hope that we as politicians will also work together to ensure that all sectors of society within the fisheries sector are given the place they deserve within the sector.
first of all, mr president, i would like to thank margaret attwooll very sincerely for her very good report on the role and significance of women in fisheries. women's work is often invisible and mrs attwooll's report helps to bring it into the spotlight. the fishing industry is a heavy industry: long hours and hard and unfortunately also dangerous work. more women on board can be an incentive to improve working conditions there. work that is too difficult or too dangerous for women is not good for men either. in many sectors it has been found that from the moment women entered, working conditions improved. increased involvement of women in safety on board ships is essential to make real progress. we can also expect women and women's networks to pay attention to sustainability. sustainable fishing is essential to provide fish for future generations. sustainable fishing methods with minimum by-catches and good control are essential. this month the quotas are being fixed and it is very important to take scientific advice seriously, otherwise the next generations will be fishing behind the net and women are often sensitive to this. it may be time to come up with a new policy, to experiment with fisheries reserves for a few years, to put fisheries reserves on the map, to give fish stocks the opportunity to recover. the maritime strategy which is now being prepared is a good opportunity to discuss this. i call on everyone to involve women in this debate in all countries. women are also often the traders and buyers of fish. sustainable fishing is only possible if consumers are fully involved. approval marks to ensure quality and durability are a necessary step. it is good to involve as many women as possible in this. in short, like my colleague attwooll, i expect a lot from women.
mr president, i should like to propose, through an oral amendment, a merger of amendments 14, 12 and 5. this concerns the very important but also sensitive issue of adoption in romania. we want to propose to the romanian government that it settle a number of cases that are still pending, as a consequence of a moratorium on international adoption, proposed in the past by this parliament. it is an important oral amendment. i will read it carefully so that everybody knows what we are voting for: ‘notes with satisfaction the improvements made by romanian authorities in the area of the protection of children and urges the romanian government to settle the cases of applications for international adoption made during the moratorium of june 2001, ensuring that all cases are examined in light of the un convention on the rights of the child and the romanian law on the legal status of adoption, with the goal of allowing inter-country adoptions to take place, where justified and appropriate, in those special cases’.
mr president, in the last few days i have received questions from other groups on the wording of amendment no 2 tabled by my group. in addition, a separate debate and vote was held on part of this subject, the allegation of the existence of cia camps in romania and elsewhere and the need for investigation into this. i therefore propose that we limit ourselves in this amendment to the position of this candidate country with regard to restrictions on wrongdoing. the following amendment to the english text is made to this effect: we add "allegations of the existence" and we delete "but also to undertake informal investigations". i assume that the objections to the original text have been removed.
the british colonial administration did not leave a parliamentary democratic structure in hong kong, but it did create the expectation that the people's republic of china would introduce that system there. when hong kong was returned to china, the formula of two systems in one country was devised. the illusion was that this would mean that hong kong, unlike the rest of china, could have a parliamentary democracy based on universal suffrage and a multiparty system. it was said that it would be a test ground for future developments in the rest of china. we now know what the two systems amount to. it means capitalism under the control of the ruling party in most of china and capitalism without that control in hong kong. in both cases, democracy is lacking. the situation in hong kong, where a small select group is left with the responsibility of self-government, is of course far preferable to that in tibet, where violence, intimidation and re-education are the instruments of central authority. my group did not co-sign the resolution, but i will support it. i would also like to remind you that the dalai lama has already addressed the plenary session of this parliament.
the european parliament has been calling for a new constitution, a new constitution, a new constitution, and a new constitution. the last 14 years have indeed seen profound changes in this area. the economy is now more like america's. the resulting gap between rich and poor is now greater than in the european union. the situation of democracy and human rights is certainly no better than in the last years of the soviet union under gorbachev, and the repression in chechnya is greater than the crimes of stalin. when it comes to democracy and human rights, russia continues a bad tradition. the european union has not changed russia so much as the perception of it in the countries of the european union. in the past, in western europe, we saw the east, led by the soviet union, as an economic competitor and a military opponent. today, we see russia as an ally and an energy supplier. the former german chancellor, now on a russian payroll, says that russia is now a democracy in the purest sense. in these circumstances it is not surprising that there is less and less room for independent television stations and that freedom of association and assembly is being restricted. citizens are getting less and less involved in government policy. with a law that puts non-commercial organizations of citizens under government control, can stop their funding and can dissolve these organizations, the return to a wrong past is further continued. it is therefore quite right that this parliament should join the criticism of the council of europe.
